# Migration sources

This document records what the per-source migration modules of `packages/coding-agent/src` know about each product they read. Each source was measured against a real install or a real bundle. The notes here keep the paths, environment variables and formats that the code depends on.

## Purpose

The migration reads another agent product's user state and maps it onto labunbun. Each source needs the exact data roots of that product, and the exact file names and key spellings. A wrong path reads nothing. A wrong key reports a configured user as empty. The comments in the source modules carry the evidence for each claim, and this document keeps those claims in one place.

## Behavior

Each source is a group of four files. A home module names the data roots. A read module opens the documents. A plan module turns the parsed data into labunbun settings and MCP servers. A session module converts stored transcripts into labunbun history entries. The migration never writes into a source tree and never reads a credential value.

## Why it is like this

A migration that guesses a path can import a tree the product itself no longer reads. It can also miss a live fallback root and report an installed product as absent. Where the source product is readable, each claim carries the file and line it came from. Where the source is a minified bundle, the claim comes from a literal string in the bundle. Deliberate omissions are recorded next to the code that makes them.

## Known traps

- A fallback root can be live for some paths and dead for others. Read the per-path table, not a global rule.
- A file can hold a key under a name the product never uses, with the real name as a deserialization alias.
- A state path that is absent is not an error. Report it as absent, and do not claim a read.
- A credential can hide in a URL string or in a key name. Never copy a value.

### Codewhale

Codewhale is a rename of DeepSeek-TUI, and `~/.deepseek` is a live fallback root. A user who used the product under its old name has the whole tree there and nothing under `.codewhale`. A reader that looked only at `.codewhale` reports that user as absent.

The reference workspace is a Rust Cargo tree at `G:\Bunttta\Codewhale-main`. Every path claim in the Codewhale modules carries the source file and line it came from, because readable Rust gives real anchors. Qoder is the opposite case: its citations are literals from a 12 MB minified bundle, because no line number there is stable. Read the Codewhale citations as claims to check, not as decoration. Two of them were corrected once, and both corrections are named below.

Evidence anchors:

- `LEGACY_APP_DIR` is `crates/paths/src/lib.rs:16`. `CODEWHALE_APP_DIR` is `crates/paths/src/lib.rs:13`.
- The fallback mechanisms are `crates/config/src/lib.rs:6158-6170`, `crates/tui/src/config/paths.rs:240-263` and `codewhale_home()` in `crates/paths/src/lib.rs:121-138`.
- The isolation rule is `crates/config/src/lib.rs:6105-6111`.
- The dead variable reader is `crates/paths/src/lib.rs:70-78`.
- The tilde rules are `crates/paths/src/lib.rs:169-189`, and the absolute-path check is `crates/paths/src/lib.rs:163-201`.
- The config override pair is `crates/config/src/lib.rs:145-150`, and the TUI pair walk is `crates/tui/src/settings.rs:2466-2471`.
- The settings candidate triple is `crates/tui/src/settings.rs:2447-2462`, and the first-match rule is `crates/tui/src/settings.rs:2016-2044`.
- The project config iteration is `crates/config/src/lib.rs:3938-3941`, and the project restriction rules are `crates/config/src/lib.rs:3846-3855` and `crates/config/src/lib.rs:3857-3872`.
- The MCP struct is `crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:508-516`, and the server keys are `crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:562-658`.
- The skill precedence is `crates/tui/src/skills/mod.rs:985-991`, and the loader's own directory is `crates/tui/src/skills/mod.rs:188`.
- The instruction documents are `crates/tui/src/project_context.rs:354-364`, and the order rule is `crates/tui/src/project_context.rs:348-353`.
- The hook config is `crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:379` and `crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:440-444`.
- The secrets store is `crates/secrets/src/lib.rs:833-841`, and its shape is `crates/secrets/src/lib.rs:515-528`.
- The extension host denylist is `crates/tui/src/extension_host/supervisor.rs:340-358`.
- The importer that ships with the product is `crates/tui/src/commands/groups/config/import_claude.rs` and `crates/tui/src/import_claude.rs`.

**Roots and environment variables**

- `CODEWHALE_DEFAULT_DIR` is `.codewhale`. `CODEWHALE_LEGACY_DIR` is `.deepseek`.
- `CODEWHALE_HOME` moves the whole tree, and it is the only variable that does. The product reads it through `absolute_path_env`, so the value must be absolute after `~` expansion. A relative value makes the product stop with `PathOverrideErrorKind::Relative`.
- This import falls back to `.codewhale` for a refused value and reports the reason in `CodewhaleHome.rejectedHome`. It does not read `~/.codewhale`, and the report names the skip, because that tree is one the product itself refused to open.
- `CODEWHALE_CONFIG_PATH` and `DEEPSEEK_CONFIG_PATH` move the config file only. Both are live, and the TUI settings resolver walks the same pair to find `settings.toml` beside the file. Each name points at a whole file, not a directory, and the product applies the same `~` expansion and absolute-path test.
- An empty or whitespace-only variable value is unset. The product trims a Unicode value, so a value of spaces counts as unset too.
- `DEEPSEEK_HOME` is dead. Only `legacy_deepseek_home_override()` reads it, and nothing in the workspace calls that function. Tests remove the variable to neutralise it, and the product documentation tells the user to rename it to `CODEWHALE_HOME`. The legacy directory is live. The legacy variable is not.
- Other `DEEPSEEK_*` variables are live. `DEEPSEEK_APPROVAL_POLICY` and `DEEPSEEK_ALLOW_SHELL` are the second halves of explicit `[CODEWHALE_X, DEEPSEEK_X]` pairs. `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY` is a live provider credential.
- `CODEWHALE_HOME` wins outright when it holds an absolute path. The legacy root never follows it, because a user who exports the variable isolates a profile on purpose. The product's own note says the legacy root "never follows `CODEWHALE_HOME`", and that callers must suppress the legacy fallback when the home is explicit.
- A `~` value alone names the home, and `~/x` is the home with `x` appended. The product accepts both separator characters there, and this import does too.
- The product's relative-value message is "`<VAR>` must be an absolute path, got `<path>`". The tilde case raises `PathOverrideErrorKind::HomeUnavailable`. The code keeps that branch because only a `~` value with an empty home can reach it.

**The per-path fallback**

The fallback is per path, not global, and three product mechanisms disagree. `resolve_state_dir` checks primary, then legacy, then primary for any state subdir. `default_user_state_path_from_environment` applies the same existence-checked rule, spelled per name, to `config.toml`, `mcp.json`, `skills`, `notes.txt` and `memory.md`. Everything routed through `codewhale_home()` has no fallback at all: that set is `agents/`, `fleets/`, `plugins/`, `themes/`, `audit.log`, `constitution.json`, `prompts/constitution.md` and `workflows/`.

The table `CODEWHALE_LEGACY_FALLBACK` records which path is which. A reader that applies one global rule to every name reads a `~/.deepseek` tree that Codewhale no longer uses. It then imports settings, skills or MCP servers the product no longer loads.

- The rows with fallback are `config.toml`, `permissions.toml`, `mcp.json`, `skills`, `notes.txt`, `memory.md`, `settings.toml`, `tui.toml`, `sessions`, `state` and `tool_outputs`. A `true` value means the product checks the legacy root for this name.
- `permissions.toml` is a companion of whatever `config.toml` resolved to, so it inherits that resolution instead of one of its own.
- `settings.toml` has two legacy candidates, and the second is not under the home at all: `dirs::config_dir()/deepseek/settings.toml`, which on Windows is `%APPDATA%\deepseek\settings.toml`.
- `tui.toml` inherits the `settings.toml` roots through `with_file_name`.
- `sessions` resolves through `ensure_state_dir("sessions")`, whose write form relocates a legacy `sessions` tree into the primary on first creation.
- No fallback: `agents`, `fleets`, `plugins`, `themes`, `audit.log`, `constitution.json`, `prompts/constitution.md`, `workflows`, `secrets`, `keyring-locks` and `imports`. `workflows` uses a hardcoded `.codewhale` literal.
- One override disables every fallback. The product calls an explicit home "an isolation boundary". The rule says that resolvers "must not fall back to ambient legacy `~/.deepseek` data outside that root". A user who exported `CODEWHALE_HOME` to isolate a profile gets an empty legacy tree on purpose.

**Resolution results**

`CodewhaleStateLocation` reports three states. The file was in the live root, the file was in the pre-rename root, or nothing is there. The third state is ordinary, and it is not an error. A report with one sentence for all three states claims a read that did not happen.

- The existence test is a parameter of both resolution functions, so a pure test can drive all three branches without a filesystem.
- A `false` row matters to the plain path resolver too. A caller that asks for that name asks for a path the product computes from the home alone.
- The settings candidate function returns every candidate rather than the winner, so the reader can say which candidate answered and which was not there.
- The three-way answer is the third clause of the product's own resolver made visible. The product returns the primary path when nothing exists, because it is about to write there. A reader that cannot tell that case from "found it" reports a read it never made.

**The settings documents**

Codewhale has more than one settings document. This import reads or names five.

- `config.toml` holds the provider, model and mode document. It is the only document with a project layer.
- `permissions.toml` holds the typed allow, ask and deny rules, beside the resolved `config.toml`.
- `settings.toml` is the TUI preference store. The product builds three candidates and takes the first that exists. The primary comes first, then the legacy home, then a platform config directory with no `~/.` at all. On Windows that third candidate is `%APPDATA%\deepseek\settings.toml`. On Linux it is `~/.config/deepseek/settings.toml`. This import resolves the two home-relative candidates and names the third through `CODEWHALE_LEGACY_CONFIG_DIR_NAME`. The third one sits outside the home the caller gave.
- `tui.toml` is the superseded preference store. The product folds it into `settings.toml` on load and moves it aside with a receipt. This import names the file and does not read it. A read as a second settings document imports the same preferences twice, from a tree the product considers spent.
- `hooks.toml` is the project half of the hook config. The product deserializes `HooksConfig` separately from the root config, the same way it does for the TUI's own `Config`. A first pass over the root `ConfigToml` field list found no `hooks` field and concluded that Codewhale had no hooks. That conclusion was wrong: the field is not on the struct, and the struct is not the one that reads it. The project half depends on workspace trust and exact-byte approval, because the project hooks run code from a repository. The report names that gate instead of a silent read.
- The config-file override moves `permissions.toml` and `settings.toml` with `config.toml`. `permissions.toml` sits beside the resolved config file, and the product looks for `settings.toml` beside the override.

**The project layer**

- The only project-scoped settings document is `<workspace>/.codewhale/config.toml`.
- The brief listed a project `settings.toml` and a project `mcp.json`, and neither exists.
- The CLI refuses a project-scoped settings write outright. Its message says that the key "is a user setting stored in settings.toml and has no project scope".
- `mcp.json` is user-global, and the product refuses a configured relative `mcp_config_path` with a warning.
- The product reads both `<workspace>/.codewhale/config.toml` and `<workspace>/.deepseek/config.toml`, and the `.codewhale` one wins when both exist.
- A project config can only tighten `approval_policy` and `sandbox_mode`. This import reports such a file and never claims it. A claim of "restrictions the project asked for" widens the target's posture. That is the one direction a migration must not move on its own judgement.
- The product keeps "no project config" and "a project config that is broken" apart on purpose. The product's own note says the distinction is security-relevant. A typo can make the file unparseable. If the report says "absent" for that file, the project loses its restrictions and falls back to the user's more permissive baseline.
- `<workspace>/.codewhale/anchors.md` has a legacy companion. The product also treats a `.codewhale` directory that is itself a symlink as a win even when the file inside is absent. That is how a user redirects the whole directory. This import does not reproduce the symlink clause.

**MCP**

- `mcp.json` holds the MCP servers. The file's own key is `servers`, and `mcpServers` is a deserialization alias. This import accepts both and prefers `servers` when a file carries both. That choice is the only one consistent with the alias rule. The alias applies only when the canonical name is absent.
- The server struct has no `rename_all` attribute, so every name on disk is the bare Rust name. The key list is snake_case, and no camelCase spelling of it exists.
- The recognised server keys are `command`, `args`, `env`, `cwd`, `url`, `allow_private_network`, `transport`, `connect_timeout`, `execute_timeout` and `read_timeout`. The other keys are `disabled`, `enabled`, `required`, `enabled_tools`, `disabled_tools`, `headers`, `env_headers`, `env_http_headers`, `bearer_token_env_var`, `scopes`, `oauth` and `oauth_resource`.
- `env_headers` also deserializes as `env_http_headers`. The tool lists are `enabled_tools` and `disabled_tools`, not `enabledTools` and `disabledTools`.
- `enabled` and `disabled` are both real and independent. `enabled` defaults to `true` and `disabled` defaults to `false`. The import reads both keys when a file carries both, so it treats `disabled: true` as the switch-off.
- Three entry keys hold credentials by the product's own account: `headers`, `env` and `bearer_token_env_var`.
- The product's own note on `headers` says the values pass through as-is, with no environment substitution. It says that a real token there "lives in plain text" in `~/.deepseek/mcp.json`. That sentence names the stale path. It is independent evidence that the rename happened after the note was written.
- `bearer_token_env_var` names a variable rather than a value, but the name has no credential shape that a scan can rely on. The field's own doc calls the variable a bearer token.
- `env_headers` is not in the credential list. Its values are looked up in the environment at request time, so the file holds a variable name and no token. This import still drops the key, for a different reason: this build's MCP client has nowhere to put it.

**Skills and agents**

- The product's skill precedence is, in its own words: project order `.codewhale`, `.agents`, `.claude`, `.opencode`, `.cursor`, then an opted-in flat `skills` root. Global order is `.codewhale`, `.agents`, `.claude`, then legacy `.deepseek`. Project roots outrank global roots.
- Only Codewhale's own roots are imported. `~/.agents` is the shared agentskills.io home, and the `agents` source of this repository owns it outright. A second import lands two copies of every file in it. File writes are keyed on the target path. The second copy then appears as a skill that Codewhale wrote, although the `agents` run already wrote the same file.
- `<workspace>/.agents/skills` is imported, because the `agents` source reads `join(home, ".agents")` and nothing else, so no source claims the project-level shared tree. If that ever changes, the target-path de-duplication turns the second claim into a reported skip rather than a second write.
- The shared tree list is `.agents/skills`, `.agents/AGENTS.md` and `.agents/instructions.md`. Two of those entries belong to the `agents` source outright. The third is `~/.agents/instructions.md`, which `agents-read.ts` does not read, so the Codewhale read module handles that one file separately. The call site filters every shared-tree entry by existence.
- The second shared entry is the product's global agents skills directory. The instruction documents are the vendor-neutral rows of the product's project context table.
- `~/.deepseek/skills` is named for the report even though the reader reaches it through the fallback. The trap runs in the other direction too. The skill loader has its own `default_skills_dir` that hardcodes `.codewhale` with no fallback, and a companion function of the same name has one. Two functions share one name, and the loader decides what a skill is. One of the two reads a `.deepseek/skills` tree, and the other does not. This import follows the one that falls back and says which root answered.
- `~/.codewhale/agents/<id>.toml` holds Fleet personal agent profiles. The project half is `<workspace>/.codewhale/agents`. Both names exist as separate constants, and both are named here.
- `~/.codewhale/fleets/<slug>.toml` holds fleets, and `~/.codewhale/plugins/<name>/` holds plugins.

**Instruction documents**

- The product declares six global instruction documents, in this order: `.codewhale/AGENTS.md`, `.agents/AGENTS.md`, `.deepseek/AGENTS.md`, `.codewhale/instructions.md`, `.agents/instructions.md`, `.deepseek/instructions.md`.
- Three roots exist per file name, not one. The product's rule is that `.codewhale/` takes priority over vendor-neutral `.agents/`, which takes priority over legacy `.deepseek/`.
- `WHALE.md` is never loaded. The product has a deprecated file name for it and a warning for the user. The warning says that WHALE.md is ignored, and that project instructions belong in AGENTS.md. The Codewhale authority policy belongs in `.codewhale/constitution.json`. This import names the three deprecated paths so a user who has one is told it does nothing.
- Project rules live in `.codewhale/rules`, as `.md` files in file name order. The product's rule list also has `.claude/rules`, and that entry is added only when the Claude import format is enabled. This import reads only the Codewhale-native directory, because the Claude directory belongs to a different product's files. There is no global rules directory.

**Sessions**

- The session directory comes from `ensure_state_dir("sessions")`, one `<id>.json` per session. A home that still holds only `~/.deepseek/sessions` is a real state, because the product moves that tree on first creation. This is one of the two paths the product is known to move.

**Trees this source names and never opens**

- `~/.codewhale/secrets/secrets.json` is the credential store. Its shape is `{ "entries": { <name>: <value> } }`, plus a `legacy_deepseek_migrated` flag. Account tokens live in it too: `token_type`, `access_token` and `refresh_token`. The account module says that account sessions must not touch the OS key store, because this file is the store. The file uses Unix mode `0600`, and its parent directory uses mode `0700`. On Windows the ACL model is too different to enforce those modes in code.
- Nothing in this import opens the secrets file. The code asks only whether the path exists. A path is not user content, but a report is something a user may paste into an issue.
- `~/.codewhale/keyring-locks/` holds per-user lock files for the OS key store. The product notes that authority there is per user, not per home or profile. This import names the directory, never lists it and never opens it.
- The product's own extension host denies twenty home entries to subprocesses. That denylist is the right list for a migration to name. This import reads three of those entries elsewhere, on purpose and with their own rules: `sessions`, `mcp.json` and `settings.toml`. `state.db` is in the list and this import never opens it.
- The twenty denylist names are `secrets`, `credentials`, `tokens`, `state`, `state.db`, `sessions`, `session-archives`, `session_index.jsonl`, `tool_outputs`, `composer_history.txt` and `composer_history.jsonl`. The other names are `remote-control`, `integrations`, `audit.log`, `logs`, `memory`, `mcp.json`, `mcp.json.bak`, `config.toml.bak` and `settings.toml`. The product's note says the list covers its runtime home, the ambient `~/.codewhale` home and the legacy `~/.deepseek` home.

**The importer that ships with the product**

Codewhale ships its own Claude Code importer, and this run saw it present. The `/import-claude` command lives in a command module of 305 lines and an engine module of 543 lines. The engine reads `~/.claude.json` and `~/.claude/settings.json` in a bounded, read-only way and builds a plan, never a silent import.

This import reports that importer and never invokes or emulates it, for two reasons.

- It writes into `~/.codewhale/imports/`, inside the very tree a migration must leave untouched. That breaks the first invariant of this repository: sources serve reads, never writes.
- What it does is narrower, not different. A bare invocation writes a report and an unapplied bundle. The `--apply` flag makes exactly one change. It copies `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` to `~/.codewhale/instructions.md` when the destination is absent. MCP servers are named for a manual import and hooks are refused outright.
- A user who runs both tools finds that the Codewhale importer already moved `CLAUDE.md` into `~/.codewhale/instructions.md`. That file is one this import does read as a global instruction document. The report names that interaction.

**Small rules**

- The home comes from the `home` argument. The environment comes from an `env` parameter rather than a `process.env` read. Nothing in the module calls `os.homedir()`, so a test that points this module at a fixture cannot reach the developer's own install. The source environment coverage test has nothing to report.
- The content check that a directory holds something is spelled out in this module rather than imported, because `migrate-types.ts` owns that function. An import closes a cycle that the source registry keeps one way.
- The per-profile agent file name is `<id>.toml`, built from the profile id.

**The read module, and its guarantees**

- `codewhale-read.ts` takes every path from `codewhale-home.ts`, with the reference `file:line` it came from. Nothing in it throws. A read that fails becomes a line in `RawCodewhale.skipped` that says what failed and why. That is the convention `qoder-read.ts` and `antigravity-read.ts` use. A migration that stops on one damaged file loses every other source's import to make a point about that file.
- A skipped name is a label, not a resolved path. It is a bare name where that is unambiguous and a forward-slashed relative label where it is not.
- Every field of `RawCodewhale` is a value read out of a file, a count, or a line that says why neither happened. No field holds a credential and no field holds a conversation.
- `present` is not "a settings file was read". A home with only skills, only sessions or only a project `config.toml` is a real install with something in it. That is the same argument `qoder-read.ts` makes about a CLI-only home.
- `RawCodewhale.documents` carries the report of the two-root problem. "Codewhale's settings came across" hides three facts. A document in `~/.codewhale` is a current install. The same document in `~/.deepseek` is a pre-rename install the product still reads. A document under `root: "absent"` was not read at all. To collapse the three is to claim a read that never happened.
- The parsed `config.toml` field is `null` rather than `{}` on purpose. A home where the file is present and unusable must say which of four things applies. Those are: a directory where a file was expected, unreadable, not parseable, and not a TOML table.
- The project `config.toml` is never merged with the user one.
- The reader keeps the two hook blocks apart for the same reason the product does: the project half depends on workspace trust. `HooksConfig::load_with_project` appends project hooks after global ones, and only after workspace trust and exact-byte hook approval. A hook runs code, so an untrusted repository's `hooks.toml` is inert in Codewhale. To merge them here imports a command the product itself refuses to run.
- `mcpPath` and `mcpPresent` are fields because there is no project MCP file to name. A report that prints "no MCP servers" for a home with no `mcp.json` at all says the same sentence twice.
- The shared `~/.agents` paths take the same exclusion `kimi-read.ts` makes with `KIMI_SHARED_TREE`. A second import writes every file twice, and the report credits the later copy to Codewhale. The list is filtered by existence, so an absent entry is not a report line.
- Each state document carries the product's purpose for the file, quoted rather than paraphrased.

**The credential scrub, in the reader**

- `config.toml` carries credentials in four shapes, and a key-name scan sees none of the shapes that matter. `api_key`, `webhook_token` and `sandbox_api_key` match `looksLikeSecretName` and are removed. `base_url` and `http_headers` are not caught by a name. A credential can sit in the userinfo or the query of a URL. The values under `http_headers` are tokens under a key that is not credential-shaped. Both are handled on their own terms.
- The scrub runs three mechanisms in order. A key-name scrub deletes every key that matches `looksLikeSecretName` or `CODEWHALE_SECRET_KEY` at any depth, and one line records the full path. This catches `api_key`, `webhook_token`, `sandbox_api_key` and a nested `search.api_key`. A credential-table drop deletes every key in `CODEWHALE_CREDENTIAL_TABLE_KEYS` with its entries named one by one, so the report can say "left off `Authorization`, `X-Api-Key`". A URL check deletes every key in `CODEWHALE_URL_KEYS` whose value trips `urlCredentialProblem`. The line names the shape (`URL_USERINFO` or `URL_PARAMETER`) and not one character of the URL.
- `CODEWHALE_SECRET_KEY` is `/authorization/i`, the same single gap `looksLikeSecretName` has in Qoder. No Codewhale key uses the word today. An MCP `headers` block or a hand-edited `[providers.*]` entry can.
- Below a key in `CODEWHALE_ENTRY_MAP_KEYS` the immediate children are names the user chose. The set is `providers`, `servers`, `mcpServers`, `skills`, `custom_models`, `extraKnownMarketplaces`, `enabled_plugins` and `plugin_configs`. The walk steps over them and matches only each entry's own keys. The key above was still tested and still deleted if it matched. `providers` is a flattened map, so a user who wrote `[providers.my-gateway]` gets an entry called `my-gateway`. A scrub that deletes `providers.my-key-router` deletes a live route because its name holds three letters.
- `CODEWHALE_CREDENTIAL_TABLE_KEYS` is `http_headers`, the MCP credential keys, `env_headers` and `env_http_headers`. The product's own `is_upstream_auth_header` calls these header names credential-shaped. The product's own comment says a stored header lives in plain text in `~/.deepseek/mcp.json`. And `config.toml` is an ordinary `0644` file, unlike `secrets/secrets.json`. `bearer_token_env_var` names a variable and does not hold a token. The importer drops it, because this build's MCP client has nowhere to put it. A name that resolves to a bearer token is a name a report does not echo without a note. The importer drops `env_headers` for the same reason even though its values are not in the file at all. The file holds a variable name, and to drop it loses an indirection rather than a secret.
- `CODEWHALE_URL_KEYS` is `base_url`, `sandbox_url` and `origin`. The hole was found in Qoder and then fixed across all fourteen earlier sources. An MCP entry's `url` is deliberately not in the list. A URL with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` removed is a different URL that points at nothing, so a credential there loses the whole server. That decision belongs to `planCodewhaleMcp`, where the loss can be reported as the loss of a server. A first version of the shared guard used `new URL` as its main path and mis-parsed 11 of 16 hand-edited URLs.
- The scan stops at depth 8 (`MAX_CREDENTIAL_SCAN_DEPTH`). The cap stops a pathological document from a megabyte-deep walk. The deepest attested provider key is three segments (`providers.<name>.api_key`). A key deeper than the cap stays in place, which is the one thing the scrub can get wrong. The report states that fact and does not pretend it away.

**The parsers, and what their recovery means**

- `readCodewhaleText` calls `statSync` first, because absent and there-but-unreadable must arrive as different answers. Both otherwise arrive as exceptions, and a home that never installed Codewhale produces a report full of "unreadable" lines. A directory where a file was expected gets a reason of its own.
- `readCodewhaleJson` tries plain `JSON.parse` first. `mcp.json` is plain JSON in the product, so the `parseJsonc` recovery is a superset of what Codewhale accepts. A file this reader can still parse is one Codewhale rejects, which is the opposite of what recovery normally covers. The report line says so rather than a silent read.
- `readCodewhaleToml` carries the same numeric-key retry `step-read.ts` and `grok-read.ts` document. That is the parser's deviation, not Codewhale's. TOML 1.0 allows a digits-only segment after a dot (`[providers].0 = 1`), and `Bun.TOML.parse` rejects the whole document over it. Codewhale parses with the `toml` crate, which is spec-compliant, so such a file is one Codewhale reads. To quote the segments is a no-op under the spec and recovers every other setting. The report names the segments it requoted.
- A TOML failure line is a fixed phrase, never the parser's message. A `toml` error can quote the line it failed on, which may be a credential.
- The reader reads a directory's entries in name order, and an absent or unreadable directory adds nothing. Most of the directories this module looks for are optional. The caller reports the absence of the thing they were for, not the absence of the directory.
- `permissions.toml` is `{ rules: Vec<ToolAskRule> }` and nothing else, with `deny_unknown_fields`. A rule carries `tool`, `command`, `command_exact`, `path`, `workspace` and `action`. A file with a key the product has since removed fails for the product too. So a parse failure here is a fact about the user's install rather than about this reader. The parser's message is never printed. No credential can live in a rule: every field is a tool name, a path or an enum. That is why this is the one Codewhale settings document that can be carried whole.
- A rule with no tool name is skipped with a line of its own, because the tool is the only field the schema needs. The reader reads the rules in file order. A `rules` key that is not an array gets a line of its own. An action other than `allow`, `ask` or `deny` becomes `other`. An absent action becomes `ask`.

**Hooks, rules, skills and sessions**

- `HookEvent` is `#[serde(rename_all = "snake_case")]` and `Hook` has no such attribute. Every field on disk is its bare Rust name, while every event value is snake_case.
- A hook's `condition` is flattened to a variant name and an argument. The file's own comments differ on the serde term: one says external, another internal. The shape is not in doubt. The argument lands as a field beside `condition`, not inside it, so a nested-object read finds nothing. `tool_name` carries `name`, `tool_category` carries `category`, `mode` carries `mode` and `exit_code` carries `code`. `Always` is the unit variant and carries nothing. Both halves are carried in the read. The planner needs the variant to decide whether a hook can run here at all. It needs the argument to say which one the hook named.
- The `condition` field decides whether a hook can be imported at all. The hook table's other keys are `event`, `command`, `timeout_secs`, `background`, `continue_on_error` and `name`. The block's keys are `enabled`, `default_timeout_secs` and `working_dir`, beside the `hooks` list.
- The hook timeout defaults to 30 seconds, which is the product's own default.
- `plugin_authority` and `project_authority` are `#[serde(skip)]`, so nothing in a file can claim either. `enabled` defaults to true, the serde default, so an absent `enabled` means on. An `enabled = false` switches the whole table off.
- `name` is ambiguous in Codewhale's own format, and the reader must choose. `Hook.name` is for the log and `ToolName`'s single field is also `name`. So a hook with a `tool_name` condition and a distinct log name cannot have both. To read the key once, as the condition argument, is the interpretation that always has a defined value. The planner uses it as the matcher.
- The project hooks file is named whether or not it exists, because its absence and its presence mean different things. A project that has never written one is not a project whose hooks were missed. A `[hooks]` that is not a table gets a line. So does an unreadable project file, or one that is not a TOML table.
- The reader reads project rules from `.codewhale/rules` only. The extension filter is `.md`, so a `.txt` beside them is named rather than carried. A directory in the rules folder is named rather than walked.
- The reader reads global instruction documents in the product's precedence, and the first copy of each basename wins. A collision between the two Codewhale roots is recorded rather than dropped. A user who has one in both learns which one was used. The `.agents` rows are skipped rather than read.
- `WHALE.md` earns a name and no read: the product reads one only to warn the user about it.
- Skills take the first root that holds a name, and a collision is recorded rather than dropped. The order is the product's: project roots outrank global ones, so the caller passes them in product order. The roots are the global one and the two project scopes. A skill directory with no `SKILL.md` gets a line. Codewhale's own layout is `<dir>/<name>/SKILL.md`, so silence there reads as a miss.
- The skills root is taken from the already-resolved `skills` location, not from the resolved home. `CODEWHALE_LEGACY_FALLBACK` is true for `skills`, so on a pre-rename install the tree is `~/.deepseek/skills`. To ask for `<home>/skills` instead reports a full skills tree as no tree at all. That is the exact failure the two-root table exists to stop.
- Sessions are a count, not a list. `SavedSession` is a single JSON document per session with `metadata.workspace` recorded, and `codewhale-session.ts` reads its envelope on the history arm. This count tells the report how many there are before the user asks for any of them.
- `<workspace>/.codewhale/anchors.md` is read, and absence is silent by contract. A project that never wrote one is not a failure and does not get a report line.

**The read entry point**

- `readCodewhale` resolves paths against `home`, `cwd` and `env` and never calls `os.homedir()` or `process.cwd()`. A fixture laid out by a test and a developer's own tree are the same code path.
- `cwd` is `string | undefined` rather than defaulted, so a test that forgot it cannot read whatever directory the runner happened to be in. `env` defaults to `process.env`, which is what `readSources` passes. A test that asserts on content must pass `env` rather than rely on the ambient one.
- The reader sorts `RawCodewhale.skipped` by name before it returns it. So two runs over one home produce the same report, not one that changes with the order the filesystem handed back.
- Nothing short-circuits: a home with an unparseable `config.toml` still yields its skills.
- The five state documents are resolved through the same per-path rule the product uses. The reader reads a path that lives under `~/.deepseek` from there, and the report says so. The reader reads each document on its own, so a home without one still yields the other four.
- The MCP entries are scrubbed on their own rather than through the document walk, because `mcpServers` is reached through its alias. A walk over the parsed document must know both spellings to find it.

**The plan module and the mode pair**

Codewhale's `sandbox_mode` needs no translation at all. `crates/config/src/lib.rs:3799-3804` lists `read-only`, `workspace-write`, `danger-full-access` and `external-sandbox`. Two of those four are byte-for-byte the same as this build's own vocabulary (`packages/agent/src/types.ts:49`). That is not a coincidence. The confinement axis maps one to one and the approval axis does not. The reader must read the pair as two values rather than one.

`approval_policy` has two vocabularies in two files, and only one of them is `config.toml`'s. `crates/config/src/lib.rs:3794-3796` gives the root key's five values: `on-request`, `untrusted`, `never`, `auto` and `suggest`. The doc comment on `check_config_toml_choice` (`crates/config/src/lib.rs:3822-3825`) names the trap in the product's own words. `use-tui-default`, `ask`, `auto-review` and `full-access` are `settings.toml` values for the `/settings` editor, and `config.toml` does not read them.

`crates/config/src/settings_schema.rs:257-272` confirms which values the editor offers. Its final comment is the one that matters. `never` is a managed-policy value only, accepted from `config.toml` and shown read-only, and never offered by the editor. A migration that reads `approval_policy` out of `settings.toml` with `config.toml`'s table maps `ask` and `full-access` to nothing at all.

The type the policy resolves to is `AskForApproval` (`crates/execpolicy/src/lib.rs:209-227`): `UnlessTrusted`, `OnFailure`, `OnRequest`, `Reject { sandbox_approval, rules, mcp_elicitations }` and `Never`. `UnlessTrusted` skips the ask for a trusted prefix. `OnFailure` allows, and asks only after a failure. `OnRequest` always asks. `Never` never asks and denies the commands it will not approve. Two of the five values map, one maps to nothing, and the one that maps to nothing is the dangerous one.

- `auto` maps to `agent`. It is the loosest policy Codewhale has: `approval_policy_rank` (`crates/config/src/lib.rs:3873-3881`) gives it rank zero. The doc above the function says the Full Access baseline is that loosest policy. The importer claims the sandbox half from `sandbox_mode`, so nothing rests on a guess.
- `on-request`, `untrusted` and `suggest` map to `ask`. Three names for one posture: `approval_policy_rank` puts all three at rank one, and the TUI's own bridge says so directly (`crates/tui/src/config.rs:4426-4434`).
- `never` maps to nothing. `AskForApproval::Never` never asks and denies the commands it will not approve, and nothing here does both: `ask` asks and `agent` runs. A map to either changes what runs in one direction or the other, so the value is named and skipped. An import of the absence is safe and an import of a guess is not.
- `settings.toml`'s `ask` maps to `ask`, and `full-access` maps to `agent` with the sandbox unconfined. `auto-review` and `use-tui-default` map to nothing, and each has a reason. `use-tui-default` is the absence of a choice, so an import of it writes a posture the user never stated. `auto-review` is a classifier that approves the calls it judges routine (`crates/tui/src/config.rs:4426-4434` groups it with `auto`), and no mode here does that. `ask` puts a person in the loop and `agent` runs everything.

The two tables are `CODEWHALE_APPROVAL_POLICIES` and `CODEWHALE_SETTINGS_APPROVAL_POLICIES`. Two tables rather than one merged list is the difference between a map and a guess. Both are exported for the row count, not for the values. A row added here without a line in the mapper test's table is an import whose claim nobody checks.

Two of the four sandbox values map one to one, because this build's `SANDBOX_MODES` and the product's list are the same two strings. The other two map to nothing.

- `read-only` is the tightest confinement Codewhale has, and this build has no read-only sandbox. A claim of `workspace-write` for it moves the setting in the wide direction on the user's behalf. Wide is the one direction a migration must not move in on its own judgement. A user who wants it sets `/mode` after.
- `external-sandbox` maps to nothing for a second reason. It routes `exec_shell` through an external backend's HTTP API at `sandbox_url` with a bearer token (`crates/tui/src/config.rs:3137-3141`), and this build has no such backend. There is nothing to point it at.

The whole `config.toml` sandbox vocabulary is exported so a report can name a value's rank.

Both halves of the mode pair come from separate keys, and the importer claims neither of them alone. That is the property `claimModePair` exists to enforce, and this planner enforces it by hand. Codewhale states `approval_policy` and `sandbox_mode` as two independent root keys (`crates/config/src/lib.rs:927-928`). A `claimModePair` call puts one sentence about a single value onto both report lines, and both are wrong. `antigravity-plan.ts` makes exactly this call for exactly this reason.

Which sandbox an `auto` approval policy gets is a real question, and this is the answer. `auto` is Codewhale's loosest policy and the file may say nothing about confinement at all. A claim of `agent` alone leaves a session that auto-approves everything inside a workspace sandbox, a combination no user chose. So when `sandbox_mode` is absent, the importer claims neither of the two keys. That is the `t3-plan.ts` argument. To write the strictest posture because the schema supplies it by default is the fail-open failure, and so is to write the loosest.

`claimModePair` writes one shared detail onto both keys, and it is written for a source whose one value does two jobs. Its sandbox note says either that the source's mode also meant no confinement, or that the source had no separate confinement setting. Both sentences are false of Codewhale, and a report line is not the place to be approximately right about where a confinement setting came from. What `claimModePair` buys is kept: a mode cannot be written without a sandbox beside it, and that rule is enforced above.

`settings.toml` carries a posture of its own. A report line points at the file the value is in rather than the one the pair usually is. A line that points at `config.toml` for a value that is not in it sends the user to the wrong file. `settings.toml`'s vocabulary is consulted only when `config.toml` said nothing. The two files are two documents rather than one merge, and `config.toml` names the value in the table this planner checks.

The reason for a skipped `approval_policy` value is a sentence rather than a flag, because each value is a different reason. A report that printed the same four words for all of them is indistinguishable from an importer that did not try. The same holds for `sandbox_mode`.

The top-level keys this mapper accounts for in `config.toml` are `providers`, `approval_policy`, `sandbox_mode`, `model`, `default_text_model` and `custom_models`. `providers` is walked for its `base_url`, `model` and `context_window`. Everything else is named by one aggregate line at the end rather than left behind. The keys it accounts for in `settings.toml` are `approval_policy`, `default_provider`, `model` and `theme`.

The importer reads `default_text_model` only when `model` is absent, and the reason is a doc comment. `default_text_model` is the "TUI-compatible default DeepSeek model" (`crates/config/src/lib.rs:888`) while `model` is the root selector. An install that set both means both, and the root one is the closer analogue of what this build stores. It wins and the other is named.

A model that resolves to nothing here gets a report line, not a write. `resolveModelReference` is the shared check. A model this build does not carry becomes a reported skip rather than a value that fails at the first request.

**The permission rules**

`permissions.toml` is small and typed, an unusual shape worth a sentence. `PermissionsToml` (`crates/config/src/lib.rs:625-630`) is `{ rules: Vec<ToolAskRule> }`, and `ToolAskRule` (`crates/execpolicy/src/lib.rs:112-137`) is six fields, none of them a credential. Unlike everything else in `config.toml`, this file is carried whole.

Three of the six fields have no counterpart here, and each one costs a rule.

- `action = "ask"` is the forced-approval rule. This build's permission lists are `allow` and `deny`, and there is no "always ask" list. An ask rule becomes an allow rule, which is wider, or a deny rule, which is narrower than what the user wrote. Neither is the rule they wrote.
- `tool` names a Codewhale tool (`exec_shell`, `edit_file`), and this build's rule grammar has no tool field: a rule matches the command line. A rule with no `command` has nothing to match on, so the importer drops it.
- `workspace` pins a rule to one repository. This build's rules are global, so a workspace-scoped rule imported as global applies everywhere. That is wider than the rule the user wrote.

`command_exact` is the subtle one and it goes the safe way. `crates/execpolicy/src/lib.rs:120-122` says the approval card's remembered grants set this. The approval of one safe command cannot silently allow a later invocation with extra arguments. A prefix rule for `git status` matches `git status --amend` here, and an exact rule does not. So an exact rule is imported as its own text and reported as narrower or equal. A rule with a `path` but no `command` has no command text at all and is named.

**The MCP servers**

The transport is decided the way the product's own fields describe it. `McpServerConfig` (`crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:562-658`) has both `command: Option<String>` and `url: Option<String>` with no `#[serde(rename_all)]`, so both on-disk spellings are the bare names.

- `command` is present: stdio, with `args`, `env` and `cwd` alongside. An empty `command` string still means stdio, because the field is an `Option<String>` and `Some("")` is a value. A read of "absent when empty" reclassifies a malformed entry as a transport-less one and gives the wrong reason.
- `url` is present and `command` is absent: HTTP, where `transport` chooses streamable HTTP or legacy SSE. `crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:578-582` says the default is Streamable HTTP with an SSE fallback, and `transport: "sse"` forces the legacy one.
- Both, or neither: named, because the entry itself gives no rule to pick one.

`disabled` and `enabled` are both real and independent (`crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:594` and `crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:596`, where `enabled` defaults to true). `disabled: true` is the switch-off and the server is not imported. An import of a server the user turned off adds one to the target that they had deliberately removed.

The importer drops every credential channel and reports the names. No value reaches this function: the reader deletes them and writes one `skipped` line per name. `env_headers` is the fourth channel and the mildest. The file holds an environment variable name, and this build's server config has no field for that either. A `url` that carries a credential loses the whole server, for the reason `qoder-plan.ts` argues at length. A URL with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` removed is a different URL that points at nothing.

A `cwd` written on another machine means a different directory here. The product's field is a `PathBuf` that it resolves against its own current directory (`crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:569-571`). A relative value depends on where it was written. Only an absolute value is copied. Three per-server timeouts (`connect_timeout`, `execute_timeout` and `read_timeout`, `crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:588-592`) and a container-level `timeouts` block (`crates/tui/src/mcp.rs:513`) have no field in this build's server config.

The importer counts the credential blocks here so the report says what the scrub cost. `env` survives the scrub as a table of names whose values are gone, so a count here is not a re-check. The `containsSecret` flag on a copied server is computed rather than assumed. A future change that does start to copy a value has to make it true on purpose.

**Hooks**

`HookEvent` is `#[serde(rename_all = "snake_case")]` (`crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:26-27`), and `ALL_HOOK_EVENTS` (`crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:81-97`) declares fifteen events. `as_str` (`crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:117-135`) is the persisted spelling, and the two agree exactly. Seven of the fifteen have a counterpart here and nine do not. The nine are the half that matters. A hook under an event this build does not fire is imported and never runs, which is a silent loss dressed as a successful migration.

The nine are spelled out rather than left to a default branch. The table's shape says "this is the whole list", and a variant added upstream is a diff here rather than a silent no-op. The table is exported for the row count, for the same reason the approval tables are.

The block is reassembled into the Claude shape rather than reimplemented, because `normalizeClaudeHooks` knows this build's event names and how it clamps a timeout. Codewhale's entry is already very close: an event, a shell command, an optional condition and a timeout. What it has that this build's entry does not is `background` and `continue_on_error`.

The importer drops `background`, and that is the safe direction. This build's handler has no such field, so an imported hook runs synchronously and the turn waits. The importer also drops `continue_on_error`, and that is a wide move, so the report counts it and states it rather than leaves it implicit. This build never stops on a hook that fails. A Codewhale hook that set the flag to false, which meant "abort the turn", becomes one that does not. The count keeps the tally honest.

`condition` decides importability and it is the sharpest field here. `HookCondition` (`crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:217`) is `Always`, `ToolName { name }`, `ToolCategory { category }`, `Mode { mode }` and `ExitCode { code }`. `always` and an absent condition map onto "no matcher" and import. `tool_name` maps onto this build's matcher, which is what a matcher is. `exit_code`, `tool_category` and `mode` are skipped, not approximated.

A matcher is a subject pattern, and "only when the exit code was 2" is a different predicate. `tool_category` and `mode` name Codewhale's own taxonomies (`"safe"`, `"file_write"`, `"shell"` and `"plan"`, `"agent"`, `"yolo"`). Their meanings are not written down anywhere this build can check. A hook skipped for its condition gets a report line rather than a drop. An imported hook on the wrong predicate is worse than one that never came across.

`[hooks].enabled = false` switches the whole table off and nothing is imported. A user who turned their hooks off has hooks that are off, and an import of them adds a behaviour they deliberately removed.

Two maps are used rather than one list of a union type, because the two shapes land in different places in the Claude-shaped document. A matcher-less handler goes in a group with no `matcher`, and a tool-scoped one needs a group of its own that carries the matcher. That split means neither shape is ever cast to the other, which is what an earlier draft did and what TypeScript correctly refused. The one sentence for a hook this build cannot run is written once, as a closure rather than a helper that takes `items`. Both call sites already have the array in scope. A module-level sink for one push is mutable state shared by every source in the run.

The Claude-shaped document `normalizeClaudeHooks` understands maps an event to an array of groups, and a group to a `matcher?` and a list of handlers. The matcher-less group comes first because that is the shape every other importer produces for a hook with no matcher.

**Assets, names and the leftovers**

The instruction documents, the project rules and the skills come across as rule files and not memory entries. The reason is on `planMemoryAsRule`'s own detail line. This build merges rule files with the memory it has rather than replaces it. That is what a document the agent re-reads at the top of every session wants.

A name collision is folded into the path rather than resolved. Two documents that want one target path is the case `planAntigravityAssets` guards against, and a resolution there picks a winner without a report. `AGENTS.md` and `instructions.md` are two different documents in Codewhale's own precedence list, so they get two different target names rather than one `imported-codewhale.md`. `splitDir` takes the directory part of a path so a neighbour file's origin can be named.

`summarizeNames` stops at five names and appends "+N more". That is right for a list of server names and wrong for the two tables this module quotes. A list that names which paths fall back to `~/.deepseek` exists so the user can find the one they care about. A list that stops at five cannot answer that for the paths it left out. Both tables are short, fixed, and fully readable in a report line, so `allNames` prints them whole. `noFallbackPaths` gives the state names whose readers go through `codewhale_home()` alone, with no legacy fallback.

Two of the leftover lines are the report's most important sentences for this source. Both are facts about a rival path rather than about a file this importer missed.

- Codewhale ships its own Claude Code importer. The `/import-claude` command (`crates/tui/src/commands/groups/config/import_claude.rs`, 305 lines, and `crates/tui/src/import_claude.rs`, 543 lines) reads `~/.claude.json` and `~/.claude/settings.json` and, on `--apply`, moves `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` to `~/.codewhale/instructions.md`. After a user runs it, their `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` now sits in `~/.codewhale/instructions.md`, a file this importer reads. The report says so: the run saw a rival path and did not take it.
- Codewhale is a rename of DeepSeek-TUI. `~/.deepseek` is a live fallback root for some readers and not for others, which is what `CODEWHALE_LEGACY_FALLBACK` records. That difference makes the tree this import reads not always the tree the user still writes to.

Which root answered each document is stated plainly, with "neither" as one of the answers. Codewhale is two-rooted and the fallback is per path. A report that names no root for a document hides three facts. One is a current install. One is a pre-rename install the product still reads. One is a file that is simply not there.

The third is the ordinary state of a home that never wrote one. It is listed as `absent` rather than omitted, because an omitted row is indistinguishable from a bug in the walk. The legacy root gets one line per state path whose resolution differs, because the answer is not uniform. That is the sentence a user with a pre-rename install needs. The shared `~/.agents` tree is named because the `agents` source owns it.

The credential store, the `keyring` locks and `state.db` are named and never opened. Existence is the only thing asked of the store, and the name is what a report may print. The file holds `access_token` and `refresh_token` in plaintext. It is the one file here that Codewhale itself protects (`0600`, parent `0700`) rather than to the umask. `tui.toml`, the superseded store, and the third `settings.toml` root that nobody guesses are named rather than opened.

The report names the project `config.toml` in place of a merge, and the reason is the product's own. A project config can only tighten the posture. A claim of what it asked for moves the target's posture in the wide direction at import time. Wide is the one direction a migration must not move in on its own judgement. The product's own extension-host denylist is named once. A user can then see what was out of scope without a read of twenty lines to find out.

**The plan entry point**

Every parameter of `planCodewhale` is one something below uses. `claimScalar` serves the model and the two mode axes, and `claimPermissionList` serves `permissions.toml`. `mcpServers`, `markMcpSecret`, `existingMcpServers` and `force` serve the one place a credential can still reach a written file.

There is no `claimModePair`, and that absence is a decision rather than an oversight: the importer claims the pair per field, for the reason above. There is no `claimEnv`, and that absence is a fact. The one place Codewhale stores an environment variable's value is an MCP server's `env`, and those values do not come across. Nothing else in any of its documents holds one.

`claimHooks` is present, and the late find is worth a note. An earlier draft of this header said Codewhale had no hooks at all, on the evidence that `ConfigToml` (`crates/config/src/lib.rs:882-995`) declares no `hooks` field. That is a false negative of a familiar kind.

`HooksConfig` is deserialized separately from the root config (`crates/tui/src/hooks/config.rs:379`, `Configuration for hooks (loaded from config.toml)`), exactly as the TUI's own `Config` is. To read "the field is not on the struct" as "the document does not exist" is a familiar error. So is reliance on a field list for a settings file. It is corrected here rather than quietly.

Nothing carries a credential, and the four shapes that can hold one are all named. `config.toml` holds them at `[providers.*].api_key` (a credential-shaped key, caught by name) and `[providers.*].base_url`. A URL can carry a credential in its userinfo or query. `[providers.*].http_headers` is the third shape: its values are tokens under a key that is not credential-shaped.

The rest are `[search].api_key`, `[vision_model].api_key`, `[lifecycle_outbox].webhook_token` and a top-level `sandbox_api_key`. The reader drops every one of them before this module runs. Two more channels are the MCP `headers` and `env` blocks and a `url` that carries a credential.

This source's id is spelled once, in `SOURCE`, as a plain literal with no cast. The union and every table keyed by it live in `migrate-types.ts`, and the only thing the local buys is one spelling rather than eleven. The call lives in `migrate.ts`, in that file's arm for `codewhale`, which is where this planner is reached from. `CODEWHALE_MCP_CREDENTIAL_KEYS` is re-exported so a caller can name the credential keys without a read of the reader.

### MiMo Code

MiMo Code is an unminified TypeScript tree, so every path claim carries a line number. The citations are `packages/shared/src/global.ts`, `packages/cli/src/config/*` and `packages/cli/src/storage/db.ts`. A line number there is as stable as a literal string.

**The README is wrong about the data roots, and this source follows the code.** `README.md:385` claims `%LOCALAPPDATA%\mimocode\`, and `README.md:422` claims the macOS path `~/Library/Application Support/mimocode/`. Neither path appears anywhere in the tree.

`packages/shared/src/global.ts:2` imports `xdg-basedir`, and the file has no `process.platform` test at all. Lines 1-50 of that file are four `path.join` calls over four XDG constants. On Windows the tree is at `%USERPROFILE%\.config\mimocode`, and on macOS it is `~/.config/mimocode`. A future reader meets the README first, so this disagreement is repeated here.

**The four roots**

- `MIMOCODE_HOME` set gives `$MIMOCODE_HOME/config`, `/data`, `/state` and `/cache`. Unset gives `{xdgConfig,xdgData,xdgState,xdgCache}/mimocode`, with the app name `mimocode` from `global.ts:6`.
- `config` and `data` are companions under either root, not nested directories. That is why the single `SOURCE_ROOTS` string of labunbun cannot express this source, and why `migrate-types.ts` needed a `detectionRoots()` branch for it.
- The `config` root holds the settings documents, `AGENTS.md`, `skill(s)/` and `agent(s)/`. The `data` root holds `mimocode.db`, `auth.json`, `mcp-auth.json`, `memory/` and `log/`. The `state` root holds nothing this import reads, and it is named so a report can point at it. The `cache` root holds `bin/` (`global.ts:70`) and anything else MiMo Code downloads.
- `resolveMimocodeHome` throws on a relative value (`global.ts:29-33`, `MIMOCODE_HOME must be an absolute path, got: …`), so a user with such a value has a MiMo Code that refuses to start. This import must not throw, because a migration that aborts on one source's misconfiguration loses every other source's import. It falls back to the XDG spelling and reports the refusal.
- The home field keeps `$MIMOCODE_HOME` as written. The report can then say the tree is unusual without a path that may belong to another machine.
- The absolute-path test accepts both separator spellings on purpose. `path.isAbsolute` in `global.ts:29` is platform dependent, and a value written on Windows is absolute in every sense that matters on Linux. A test of only this process's spelling can refuse a configuration that works.
- Nothing in this module calls `os.homedir()`. The `home` argument is the only home source, which lets a test point at a fixture.

**Environment variables**

- `MIMOCODE_HOME` is the one tree override. A non-empty absolute value wins outright, and the four roots become its subdirectories.
- `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_DIR` is an extra config directory, searched beside `<config>` for every asset.
- `MIMOCODE_CONFIG` is an extra config file, merged after the global one (`config/config.ts:820-822`). It is a file, not a directory. A confusion of the two makes a reader look for `<file>/mimocode.json` and find nothing.
- `MIMOCODE_DB` relocates the session database. The value can be absolute, relative to `<data>/`, or `:memory:`.
- `MIMOCODE_TUI_CONFIG` names the TUI's own document. It is merged after `<config>/tui.json(c)` and before the project document.
- `xdg-basedir` reads `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, `XDG_DATA_HOME`, `XDG_STATE_HOME` and `XDG_CACHE_HOME`, and each value is used verbatim when truthy. The fallback is `path.join(os.homedir(), …)`, with no `~` expansion and no resolve. A relative value therefore stays relative and resolves against the directory where the process runs, which is also where MiMo Code looks.
- An empty value is falsy to `xdg-basedir` and falls through to the built-in path. A value of whitespace is not falsy, and this import reproduces that rather than a trim. The reason is the same as in `kimi-home.ts`, which does not trim `KIMI_CODE_HOME`.
- The environment block is a parameter of every function, and the code reads members of that parameter, never the ambient block. `test/source-env-coverage.test.ts` scans `src/*.ts` for that spelling and fails on a name absent from `MIGRATION_ENV_VARS`. That guard once let `CURSOR_DATA_DIR` pull a developer's real Cursor tree into every test in the repository. The scan is a plain text match, so a mention of the spelling in prose lands in the report exactly like a read. The comment here names the shape in words to keep the explanation and drop the false report. The Qoder reader makes the same trade with its `QoderEnv`.

**Settings documents**

- The three global settings files are `config.json`, `mimocode.json` and `mimocode.jsonc`, in the order `loadGlobal` merges them (`config/config.ts:630-636`). The `.jsonc` file is last and wins. `config.json` is first and loses, which is the opposite of what the file names suggest.
- `mergeDeep` is a deep merge, not a replace, over up to seven files in a fixed order. A machine with all three files runs a document that no single file describes.
- After the global three come the `MIMOCODE_CONFIG` file and then every `.mimocode/mimocode.json(c)` found by a walk up from the current directory (`config/config.ts:825-830`). The walk goes through `ConfigPaths.files`, which walks up and then applies `toReversed()`, so the nearest directory is applied last.
- Every one of these files is JSONC. `loadFile` runs the text through `ConfigVariable.substitute` and then `ConfigParse.jsonc`. So a comment and a final comma are legal in a file whose extension promises plain JSON. A read with `JSON.parse` alone throws away a whole settings document.
- `MIMOCODE_LEGACY_KEYS` lists the keys deleted from every loaded document before the schema sees it: `history`, `auto_worktree`, `theme`, `keybinds` and `tui` (`config/config.ts:61-75`). The `Info` schema is strict (`config.ts:500`, "additionalProperties: false in openapi.json"). A document that carried one of these five keys fails validation while the keys stay in. A reader that does not delete them reports five keys the user never set as unhandled, or refuses the whole document. MiMo Code deletes them first, so a file that carries them is a file MiMo Code reads. This import removes them too and names them in the report. A user who wrote `theme` is owed the sentence that nothing reads it now.
- `tui.json` and `tui.jsonc` hold the TUI's own layer, separate from the 41-key document.

**Scrub rules**

- `MIMOCODE_ENTRY_MAP_KEYS` lists top-level keys whose value maps a user-chosen name to an entry. The set separates a key that names a slot from a key that names a thing. `headers.authorization` is a slot: the credential sits under it, and a delete removes the credential and nothing else. `mcp.keyboard-mcp` is the name of an MCP server the user created. A delete removes the server, its command array, its environment and the directory it runs in.
- The secret-name test matches `KEY` inside `keyboard-mcp`, and a naive walk treats that match as a credential. The scrub therefore drops credential-shaped keys inside an entry and never the entry itself. Below one of these keys, the immediate children are names the user typed, so the scrub steps over them and cleans only the contents.
- This is the same rule `qoder-read.ts` applies to `mcpServers` and `hooks`. The rule is stated here rather than inherited. MiMo Code's own redaction list (`config/mcp.ts:82`) is a text match (`mcp.ts:91-93`). That test flags `keyboard-mcp`, `monkey` and `/etc/keys` as sensitive, which is wrong in exactly this direction.
- Every entry is still tested as a key and still deleted when it matches. `mcp` does not match, and a settings file whose top level carried `apiKey` still loses it. The exemption applies to the next level.
- The entry keys are `mcp` (server name to `Local` or `Remote`, `config/mcp.ts:16-73`), `provider`, `agent`, `command`, `model_groups`, `lsp`, `permission`, and the two provider arrays.
- A `provider` entry is a provider id to `ProviderConfig` (`config/provider.ts:64,89`). Its `options` may hold `apiKey` and its `headers` may hold a bearer. An `agent` entry is an agent name to a definition with a `prompt` body. An `lsp` entry maps a language to `{command, environment}`, with `lsp.<x>.env` as the credential channel (`config/lsp.ts`). A `permission` entry maps a verb or a path pattern to `allow`, `deny` or `ask`. The `command` key is at `config/config.ts:115`, and `model_groups` is at `config/config.ts:158`.
- The `permission` entry is here because of a real false positive that a test found. The secret-name test matches `SECRET` and `KEY` as substrings, so a rule as ordinary as `read: {"~/secrets/*": "deny"}` had its pattern deleted as a credential. That change turned the user's deny rule into nothing. The only line about it was the scrub's own, which claimed a credential the user never wrote.
- `MIMOCODE_NON_CREDENTIAL_SUBTREES` holds one entry, `permission`, for a subtree that provably holds no credential at any depth. The difference from the entry-map list is one more level of user-chosen names, and a test found it. `permission` has two levels of user-chosen names: the verb, then the path pattern (`config/permission.ts:56`). The entry-map exemption protects the first level and then walks the verb's contents as ordinary keys, so the pattern was tested as a key.
- The whole `permission` subtree is safe, and that is provable rather than assumed. `Rule` is `Schema.Union([Action, Object])` over `Action = Schema.Literals(["allow", "deny", "ask"])` (`config/permission.ts:8-16`), so the only leaf values under `permission` are those three words. No `apiKey`, `token` or `headers` exists at any depth. The exceptions a walk finds are all key names, and the names are the user's.
- A subtree belongs in the set when its leaf values come from a closed vocabulary that the schema pins. The absence of a credential is not enough on its own. `tools` is a map of tool name to boolean, and it fails that test, so it is not in the set.
- The `enabled_providers` and `disabled_providers` keys are arrays, so the exemption does not apply. They are listed so a reader does not have to re-derive the set when one of them grows an object member.
- The scrub stops at depth 8 (`MAX_CREDENTIAL_SCAN_DEPTH`). The deepest real key is `provider.<id>.options.apiKey` at three segments, and the cap stops a pathological document from a megabyte-deep walk. A key deeper than the cap stays in place, which is the one thing the scrub can get wrong.
- The key names the shared matcher misses are `authorization` and `bearer`. MiMo Code's own redaction list carries `authorization` (`config/mcp.ts:82`), and `bearer` is the other spelling a user pastes out of a dashboard. `MIMOCODE_SECRET_KEY` is `/^(?:authorization|bearer)$/i`.
- `keybinds` is the second false positive, found by a test. The shared matcher finds `KEY` inside any key name, and `keybinds` is a legacy key a user plausibly still has. The fix is `MIMOCODE_NON_SECRET_KEYS`, the legacy key set itself, rather than a narrower matcher. A narrower shared matcher changes every source, and a new legacy key belongs in the set by construction.
- Nothing in the settings is expected to be a bare secret. The provider keys live in `auth.json` and the OAuth refresh tokens in the `account` table, and neither is read. The name goes into `skipped` and the value stays out of the import. A credential can then never reach a planner, a plan, a report or a written file. The scrub is written anyway, because a guard that no reader can check is not a guard. The credential channels that do exist in a settings document are `provider.<id>.options.apiKey`, `provider.<id>.models.<m>.headers`, `mcp.<n>.headers`, `mcp.<n>.environment`, `mcp.<n>.oauth.clientSecret`, `lsp.<n>.env` and `formatter.environment`.
- The MCP URL is the other channel that is not key-shaped. `mcp.<name>.url` is checked only as http or https, so `https://user:token@host/mcp` passes with the token inside a string every reader treats as a safe identifier. `mimocode-plan.ts` is where `urlCredentialProblem` catches it.
- `mcp.<name>.command` is an array, not a string plus an `args` key: `Local.command` is `Schema.mutable(Schema.Array(Schema.String))` (`config/mcp.ts:18-20`), and element `[0]` is the program. A read as a string drops the executable.

**Assets and entry names**

- The directory names are `skill` and `skills`, `agent` and `agents`, `mode` and `modes`, `command` and `commands`, `plugin` and `plugins`. Both spellings are always live. The product uses a brace expansion, not one deprecated name and one current name, so a user with `skill/` gets those skills read.
- `config/skills.ts` scans `{skill,skills}/` for a `SKILL.md` at any depth. `config/agent.ts:129` scans `{agent,agents}/` for a `.md` at any depth, and `config/command.ts:29-34` scans `{command,commands}/` the same way. `config/plugin.ts:33` scans `{plugin,plugins}/` for `*.ts` and `*.js` one level down.
- The asset roots come from `ConfigPaths.directories` (`config/paths.ts:24-39`). The list is `<config>`, every `.mimocode` that a walk up from the current directory meets, `<home>/.mimocode`, and `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_DIR`.
- An entry name is path-relative and keeps its nested segments (`config/entry-name.ts:12-16`). A helper removes the text through the first search root. So `agents/team/reviewer.md` is named `team/reviewer`, not `reviewer`. A flat name creates collisions. Two teams with a `reviewer.md` each become one name. The file-write report then shows one kept and one skipped, for two skills the user has. A skill here is one directory per name. A name with a `/` therefore becomes nested directories at the target, which is the only lossless spelling.
- The name roots are the whole `patterns` arrays that the product passes. That is why `.claude` is in the command list and not in the agent list.
- The command roots are `/.mimocode/command/`, `/.mimocode/commands/`, `/.claude/command/`, `/.claude/commands/`, `/command/` and `/commands/` (`config/command.ts:47-52`). The agent roots are `/.mimocode/agent/`, `/.mimocode/agents/`, `/agent/` and `/agents/` (`config/agent.ts:146`).
- **The asymmetry is real and checked.** MiMo Code names Claude Code's commands but has no spelling for Claude Code's agents. The same is true at the load level. `ConfigPaths.claudeCommandDirectories` (`config/paths.ts:41-55`) returns `<home>/.claude` and every `.claude` that a walk up from the current directory meets. `config/config.ts:844-847` gives exactly that list to `ConfigCommand.load`. There is no `claudeAgentDirectories` and no `ConfigAgent.load` call for it. So the reader reads `.claude/commands/*.md` and not `.claude/agents/*.md`. A read of the latter imports files the product itself ignores.
- The two `.claude` directories this import can name are `<home>/.claude` and `<cwd>/.claude`. The product's walk stops at the project root, so the intermediate directories depend on where the project sits, and the read roots walk them.

**Skills from other tools**

- MiMo Code scans four trees that are not its own: `.claude`, `.codex`, `.opencode` and `.agents` (`skill/index.ts:24-27`). The reader reads each tree through a `skills/` glob for a `SKILL.md` at any depth. The scan order is the load order, and a later root wins a same-name collision against earlier non-bundled skills. `.agents` sits last among the brand roots. The `.mimocode` config directories load after all four, so its own skills win a collision against a borrowed tree. The tree at `<home>/<dir>` is scanned (`skill/index.ts:232-235`). For the project scope, the scan covers every `<dir>` that a walk up from the current directory meets (`skill/index.ts:257-261`).
- `.agents` is on unless `MIMOCODE_DISABLE_AGENTS_SKILLS` is set. The other three need an explicit `MIMOCODE_ENABLE_CLAUDE_CODE_SKILLS`, `MIMOCODE_ENABLE_CODEX_SKILLS` or `MIMOCODE_ENABLE_OPENCODE_SKILLS`.
- `~/.agents` is also a tree that this repository migrates as its `agents` source. A second import here writes every skill twice, once attributed to `agents` and once to MiMo Code. Both report lines then say "imported". That is the same bug that `KIMI_SHARED_TREE` in `kimi-read.ts` stops.
- None of the four trees is imported, and the other three are named instead. They are other products' files. A read of `~/.claude/skills` under the MiMo Code name files Claude Code's skills under the wrong product. The user's `claude-code` migration already imports them.
- `opencode` is the one labunbun source that does not import its `skills/` tree. It harvests the tree for its own inventory and imports only its providers, MCP and `AGENTS.md`. The report therefore names that directory and tells the user to copy it by hand.

**Instruction documents**

- The three document names are `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and `CONTEXT.md` (`session/instruction.ts:17-21`). `AGENTS.md` is unconditional. `CLAUDE.md` is gated on a flag whose default loads it. `CONTEXT.md` is marked deprecated in the source's own comment, so this import reads it and reports it as deprecated.
- A project `AGENTS.md` below 500 characters also pulls in `CLAUDE.md`.
- The global documents come from `session/instruction.ts:28-36`. The `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_DIR` `AGENTS.md` comes first, then the `<config>/AGENTS.md`, then `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`. The last one is a fourth source's file and is named, not read. This repository already migrates `~/.claude` as `claude-code`. A second read here lands a second copy of the same instructions under a different product's name. That is the failure that `OPENCODE_VENDOR_DIRS` in `opencode-home.ts:233` stops.

**Memory**

- The three scopes are `global`, `projects` and `sessions`, from the pattern at `memory/paths.ts:47`. A memory file is `<data>/memory/<scope>/[<id>/]<key>.md`.
- `global` carries no id (`scope_id = scope === "global" ? "" : (idMaybe ?? "")`, `memory/paths.ts:50`), and `<key>` may hold `/` for nested directories.
- The parser reproduces `memory/paths.ts:46-53`, with the optional id segment and the no-id rule for `global`. A `global` entry and a `projects/<slug>` entry are different documents. A reader that filed them all under one name writes two rules to one path.
- The index of the key is decided by the scope. A fixed index gets a nested `global` key wrong and drops its first segment. The product's own regex capture makes the same decision.

**The database**

- The primary name is `mimocode.db`. `$MIMOCODE_DB` wins outright and is used verbatim when it is absolute or `:memory:`. `:memory:` is not a file, so the resolver returns `null` rather than a path. A path there sends the reader after a database that cannot exist.
- The channel name is a build-time constant. A nightly writes `mimocode-nightly-20260925.db`, and no reader can know which nightlies exist. The data root is listed and matched instead, exactly as `opencodeDatabasePath` does for OpenCode's channel databases.
- The primary name is checked first, so a machine with both a release and a nightly database reads the release. That is the same preference `getChannelPath` makes for the `latest`, `beta` and `prod` channels.
- The database names are sorted, so the choice is reproducible across two runs over one install. `mimocode.db` sorts before every `mimocode-…` name only by luck of the ASCII table. The primary is therefore checked by name first, and the sort does not carry the decision.
- Every connection runs `PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL` (`storage/db.ts:93`), so a written database has a `-wal` and a `-shm` sidecar beside it. A `-wal` file is the cheapest evidence that MiMo Code is active or crashed mid-write. A reader that copied only `mimocode.db` copies a database whose most recent transactions are still in a file it left behind.

**Credentials, named and never opened**

- `auth.json` sits under `<data>` and is written with mode `0o600` (`auth/index.ts:9`, `:96-98`). It holds every provider key and OAuth refresh token of the install. `mcp-auth.json` sits beside it and holds the per-server OAuth entries (`mcp/auth.ts:32`).
- The `account` table holds `email`, `url`, `access_token` and `refresh_token`, all `notNull` (`account/account.sql.ts:6-17`). The `session_share` table holds `id`, `secret` and `url` (`share/share.sql.ts:5-12`), and the `secret` is the bearer half of a share link.
- Nothing in this repository opens any of these files or tables. A migration report is something a user may paste into an issue, so the sentence that names them quotes the names and nothing else.
- `history_fts` is a derived table and not content. It is SQLite's full-text shadow over the message bodies, and its text is a copy of what `message.data` already says. A read as a transcript duplicates every turn and presents the duplicates as separate messages. `external_import` is in the same list.

**Subagent turns**

- The `agent_id` column has a `notNull` default of `main` (`session/session.sql.ts:94`), and both of the product's own readers filter on it (`session/session.ts:964` and `session/message-v2.ts:1217`).
