- A subagent's tool calls and answers are written into the same session's `message` and `part` tables under a different `agent_id`. A reader that takes every row gets the user's turn followed by the whole of a task the agent ran on their behalf. The report then says the user asked for it. So this source reads only the `main` rows.

**Managed configuration, report only**

- The MDM directories come from `config/managed.ts:23-36`: `/Library/Application Support/opencode` on macOS, `<ProgramData>/opencode` on Windows with a fallback of `C:\ProgramData`, and `/etc/opencode` elsewhere. The `ProgramData` value stays in angle brackets. This import never reads it. A spelling in code puts a name in the source-env coverage report for a variable that no line reads.
- **The three directories still carry the `opencode` name.** MiMo Code is a fork, and the rename did not reach this function. A search of the tree for `mimocode` therefore misses all three directories. Nothing here is state that a migration reads. It belongs to the administrator who deployed it, and the next policy push overwrites it. It is named in the report and never read.

**Read roots and the walk**

- The read roots follow the product's precedence order, weakest first, so the last-wins de-duplication of the reader is the product's own. `ConfigPaths.directories` (`config/paths.ts:24-39`) returns `<config>`, every `.mimocode` above the current directory, `<home>/.mimocode`, and `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_DIR`.
- The two walks use a root-first helper, which is why `ConfigPaths.files` applies `toReversed()` when it wants the nearest file applied last (`config/paths.ts:19-22`). The home walk passes the same value as start and stop, so it yields exactly one directory.
- The product folds the directories with `mergeDeep` in that order (`config/config.ts:846-892`), so a later directory overwrites an earlier entry of the same name. The nearest `.mimocode` beats `<config>`, and `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_DIR` beats everything. The product also collapses duplicate directories to one entry.
- **This source uses last-wins and not first-wins.** Qoder's reader keeps the first root, because Qoder's own precedence is first-wins. That reflex here keeps `<config>` over the project directory that MiMo Code loads. A user who shadowed a skill in their project runs the project's copy. An import of the global copy hands them a skill that their own tool overrode. The collision is reported either way, so the copy that lost is named rather than dropped. The direction of a collision is invisible in a test that does not assert which copy won, so the test asserts it.
- The walk carries the absolute-path root explicitly, and a lost root was a real bug that shipped for one commit. The first version removed the empty path segments and then tested the first segment for an empty string to detect a POSIX root. The removal deleted that segment first. The test was false for every POSIX path, so a result lost the `/` at its start and came back relative. It passed on Windows, where the drive letter is a real segment, and it failed on the ubuntu and macOS CI jobs. The assertion output showed the evidence: an expected `/tmp/lbb-.../code/app/.mimocode` against a received `tmp/lbb-.../code/app/.mimocode`. The root is now detected before the filter, from the text itself.

**The reader**

- Nothing in `mimocode-read.ts` throws. Every read that fails becomes a line in `RawMiMoCode.skipped` that says what failed and why, the convention `antigravity-read.ts` and `qoder-read.ts` use. A migration that stops on one damaged file loses every other source's import to make a point about that file.
- A `skipped` name is a label, not a resolved path, and it never holds a value read out of a credential-shaped key. The reader sorts the list by name before it returns it, so two runs over one home agree.
- `readMiMoCode` resolves everything against `home`, `cwd` and `env`. It never calls `os.homedir()` or `process.cwd()`, so a fixture and a developer's own `~/.config/mimocode` are the same code path. `cwd` is `string | undefined` rather than defaulted, so a test that forgot it cannot read the runner's directory. Nothing short-circuits: a home with a damaged settings file still yields its skills.
- The merged settings field is `null` rather than `{}` when no layer was readable. The reader keeps four layer-failure reasons apart: a directory where the walk expected a file, unreadable, not a JSON object, not parseable. An absent file produces no line at all. In a fresh install, that is the ordinary state.
- The reader reads the TUI document for a count of its top-level keys. The planner claims nothing from it, because this build has no analogue for keybinds or presentation. The sentence it earns is that there was one and none of it came across. `$MIMOCODE_TUI_CONFIG` is named whether or not the file exists and is never read. It merges after `<config>/tui.json(c)` and before the project files (`cli/cmd/tui/config/tui.ts:110-115`), so it can shadow every key of them.
- The first `AGENTS.md` that `mimocodeGlobalInstructionPaths` finds is the one instruction document this source reads, and absent is silent by contract. The module reads only `AGENTS.md` of the three names: `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md` belongs to the `claude-code` source, and the product's own comment marks `CONTEXT.md` deprecated. Both are named instead.
- The module counts and names mode files, and never reads them. A mode is a posture rather than a persona. The product's own comment describes it as `{build, plan}` and marks it deprecated (`config/config.ts:181-189`). This build's subagent file has no field for a posture, so a read imports an agent whose trigger does not come across.
- Plugin entry points are file names, never contents. `config/plugin.ts:33` globs `{plugin,plugins}/*.{ts,js}` and turns each hit into `pathToFileURL(item).href`, a file URL the engine imports at start-up. A copied path names code this tree does not have, and a copied body is another product's program.
- The reader checks for the database and lists its sidecars, and opens none of them. `mimocode-session.ts` opens it read-only, and only when the user asked for history, which is why the counts here are 0 and the planner says so.
- `readMiMoCodeText` calls `statSync` first, because absent and there-but-unreadable must arrive as different answers. Both failures arrive as exceptions in other cases, and a home that never installed MiMo Code gets a report full of "unreadable" lines.
- The reader tries `JSON.parse` first and `parseJsonc` second. The recovery is not a superset the way it is for Qoder: MiMo Code already parses every settings file as JSONC. The recovered flag therefore means the file carried comments or a comma at its end. That is a fact worth a line, not a warning. The failure line is a fixed phrase. It says the text is damaged rather than decorated. `JSON.parse` quotes the text it choked on, and the product's own parser message prints the entire file (`config/parse.ts:36`).
- An absent settings file is not an error. It is the normal state of four of the five, and a line for every project a user visits is noise. A file that exists and does not parse is the opposite, because it changes what the merge produces, so it is named. `config.json` is not looked for inside a `.mimocode` directory. The product's second pass keys on the directory name and reads only `mimocode.json` and `mimocode.jsonc`.
- The merge is remeda's `mergeDeep` as `config/config.ts:54` uses it, plus the one array exception `mergeConfigConcatArrays` makes (`config/config.ts:52-58`). The module exports it because the code is pure and it takes a list of documents. Objects merge and all other values get replaced, so an array under a key is a leaf. A project `.mimocode/mimocode.jsonc` can drop a global provider or an MCP server, not only add one. `instructions` is the one union: a project's list adds to the global list. A reader that replaced it drops the global instructions for most users.
- The report labels come from provenance, which records the last layer that carried a key by name and not the layer whose value survived. The two differ for a key a later layer set to `null`, which the merge installs as a leaf. The product has no such record, so this is the importer's own aid. A key the merge produced falls back to the first global document's path, which is right for the only way that happens. The module trims a sub-key label to the last layer that holds `key.subkey`. The stored settings never change, only the label. A top-level name alone sends a user to edit an MCP server in a project file that never mentions it.
- The product loads the `.claude` commands first so the `.mimocode` ones override on a name collision. That is the reverse of what "load first" suggests, so the reader lets the last writer of a name win.

**The permission rules**

- Only exact-name verbs are mapped, and `edit` maps to two tools on purpose. MiMo Code's own `EDIT_TOOLS` is `["edit", "write", "apply_patch", "multiedit"]` (`permission/index.ts:614`), so `edit: "deny"` denies writes and edits. `Edit` alone turns that deny into a hole, so the faithful pair is `Edit` and `Write`. `read` covers `READ_TOOLS = ["read", "view_image"]` (`:615`) and this build has no image tool, so `Read` alone loses nothing. `bash` is `Bash` and nothing else. `BashOutput`, `KillBash` and `TaskUpdate` have no MiMo Code verb, and a rule for one of them is a decision the importer invented.
- Everything absent from the table is a report line, not a guess. `task`, `actor`, `codesearch`, `lsp`, `doom_loop`, `skill`, `external_directory` and `*` name things this build has no tool for or does not mean the same way. `*` is the spelling that denies everything (`config/permission.ts:61-63`, a bare string action becomes `{"*": action}`), and the closest rule here changes what every tool can do.
- Insertion order decides, because `evaluate` uses `findLast` (`permission/evaluate.ts:11-14`) and the product's `permissionPreprocess` (`config/permission.ts:25-30`) exists to keep the order. Two rules for one tool and pattern resolve to whichever the user wrote last. A reader that sorted the keys resolves every such pair the other way.
- `expand()` is not reproduced. `permission/index.ts:583-589` rewrites a `~/` or `$HOME/` at the start against `os.homedir()` of the machine that ran MiMo Code. A substitution here lands this machine's home in a rule that meant the source machine's, which widens a path pattern. The specifier is copied verbatim and the fact is reported.
- The product's default action is `ask` (`permission/evaluate.ts:14`), so its rules narrow an ask-by-default posture. There is no ask tier here. `ask` rules are left out of both lists rather than turned into an allow, which runs the calls the user meant to be prompted for. That is `opencode-plan.ts`'s rule.
- In MiMo Code the later rule for one tool and pattern wins, and this build resolves deny over allow whatever the order. The two agree when the deny comes later and disagree when the allow does. The report names the disagreement, and the importer keeps the deny because it is the narrower side.
- A bare `permission: "deny"` becomes `{"*": "deny"}` in the product's own transform (`config/permission.ts:61-63`), so it is normalised rather than read as no rules at all. `__originalKeys` is metadata the product's preprocessor adds at parse time, and it is skipped by name.

**MCP servers, planned**

- The MCP entry key set is `type, command, environment, enabled, timeout, sampling, url, headers, oauth` (`config/mcp.ts:16-73`). `Local` is `{type: "local", command: string[], environment?, enabled?, timeout?, sampling?}` and `Remote` is `{type: "remote", url: string, enabled?, headers?, oauth?, timeout?, sampling?}`. A key outside the set is named in the report.
- The legacy `{enabled: boolean}` form works only because the config merge is deep. A global entry's full definition and a project's `{enabled: false}` fold into one object. On its own it looks like a server with nothing to run. The report names it as what it is rather than as an empty server. A server with `enabled: false` is left out, because an import adds a server the user turned off.
- The importer drops `headers` whole. MiMo Code ships a redactor for it (`config/mcp.ts:170-182`), so it agrees they hold bearer tokens. A header name is not a credential, so the reader's key-based scrub never sees them. A dropped block loses the server's authentication, which the report says.
- `environment` values are not copied and the names are. A name that `looksLikeSecretName` matches was already deleted by the reader with a `skipped` line, so what is left are names the user chose.
- The URL cannot be half-dropped. A URL with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` removed is a different URL that points at nothing. So the importer does not carry the server across at all, and `containsSecret` is `true` on that line although it wrote nothing. The value the line is about is a credential.
- `sampling` is a policy for `sampling/createMessage` (`config/mcp.ts:6-14`) and this build's MCP client has no such switch. The fact that the user set one is the report line. `timeout` is milliseconds and the code default is `DEFAULT_TIMEOUT` of 30000 (`mcp/index.ts:42`, resolved as `entry.timeout ?? cfg.experimental?.mcp_timeout` at `:1178` and `:1156`). The schema's own description says "Defaults to 5000" (`mcp.ts:28`, `:63`) and the code says 30000. The code is what runs, and the stale sentence is quoted so a reader who finds it knows why the line says 30000.
- `oauth` is a union of a record and the literal `false` (`config/mcp.ts:59-61`), where `false` means "do not auto-detect", so the guard checks the record first. The fields named are `clientId`, `clientSecret`, `scope` and `redirectUri`. A client secret is a credential. The rest describes a sign-in flow against a server that did not run here, so the user signs in again by hand.
- The `command` array is checked three ways: non-empty, all strings, and free of `{env:VAR}` or `{file:path}`. The third check is the one that refuses a server. MiMo Code expands both placeholders before it parses the config, and this build expands neither. The argv written here is a literal MiMo Code never ran.

**Placeholders, and one bug**

- `{env:VAR}` and `{file:path}` are detected and never substituted (`config/variable.ts:32-45`). `ConfigVariable.substitute` expands both into the config text before the parse, so by the time the reader reads a settings file the placeholders are gone. A file that still holds one was written by something other than the product, or the substitution failed. A verbatim copy keeps the user's text. An expansion here freezes an environment lookup into a literal secret, which is the failure the scrub exists to stop.
- The placeholder guard was once broken by `undefined`. `Array.find` answers `undefined` when nothing matched, and `undefined !== null` is true. A guard written against `null` fired for every server, refused all of them and wrote no `.mcp.json` at all. A test that only checked a placeholder passed. The absent value is normalised with `?? null` rather than compared against the wrong sentinel.

**The model, and the keys this build cannot take**

- `model` is a `provider/model` string and the two registries differ: `ConfigModelID` is a plain `Schema.String` (`config/model-id.ts:12-14`) that holds whatever models.dev calls it. The value is resolved against this build's registry. A model this build does not carry is a reported skip rather than a `model` value nothing can resolve. The importer claims the key only when the file states one. A copy of the schema's default writes a model on the user's behalf.
- `small_model` and `vision_model` are named and never claimed. This build has one `model` key. Its `fallbackModels` list means "try these when the first fails", which is a different decision rather than a smaller one. `model_groups` is a set of models the picker offers together, and this build has no grouped picker. An import of the members writes several models where the user chose one.
- A config-defined `agent` entry is a record whose `prompt` is the agent's whole system prompt. Beside it stand `model`, `temperature`, `steps`, `permission` and a free-form `options` (`config/agent.ts:115-125`). A subagent file here carries `name`, `description`, `model` and a body, with no field for the rest. A copy of the prompt alone imports an agent that is silently not the one the user tuned, so only the names are held. `mode` is deprecated in the product's own words (`config/config.ts:181-189`) and is `{build: AgentRef, plan: AgentRef}`, which agent handles which posture.
- The handled set covers `model`, `small_model`, `vision_model`, `model_groups`, `mcp`, `permission`, `tui`, `agent`, `mode` and the five legacy keys. `tui`, `agent` and `mode` are there because each gets a line of its own rather than the catch-all. The legacy keys are there because the leftover line gives each a stronger sentence than the catch-all's. A second sentence about one key in different words reads as the weaker one.

**Report labels**

- A report label carries the file a value came from, plus the layer when that file is not the user's own. `config.json` is the first of the three global documents and a project `.mimocode/mimocode.jsonc` beats all of them. A label that named only `<config>/config.json` sends the user to a file that no longer decides anything. The `global-*` sources print no suffix, because all three sit in the user's own `<config>` and a suffix says nothing new.
- Two layer names print as prose rather than enum values. `project` reads as a fragment, so it prints as "project layer". `custom-file` prints as `$MIMOCODE_CONFIG`, the variable that produced the file, because nothing else lets the user find it. Even a key with no sub-key keeps its name in the label. A bare path sends the reader to a document with 41 keys and no statement of which setting the sentence is about.

**The asset, vendor and rule lines**

- A skill is copied verbatim because the two shapes are the same: a directory with a `SKILL.md`. An agent is a `.md` file in either case and keeps its place in the tree. A nested agent becomes a nested path rather than a name two teams collide on. A command is prose the user wrote and becomes a skill through `planCommands`.
- The instruction document becomes a rule file and not a memory entry. This build merges rule files into the memory it holds rather than swap it out. That is what a document the agent re-reads at the top of every session wants. No document means no report line, the same contract the other importers keep.
- The vendor-skill line prints once per tree per root and only when the tree exists. The point is to name a directory the user can go and look at. A directory that is not there is not worth a row.
- The managed-configuration line is named unconditionally. An earlier version sat inside the plugin guard, so a machine with no plugin directory and an administrator who pushed policy printed nothing about it. Silence is the state the line exists to stop, because the directory's name is the only evidence there is.

**The last lines**

- The legacy-key line is the one that earns its place. The five keys are deleted from every loaded document before the strict `Info` schema ever sees it (`config/config.ts:61-75`, `:500`). A user who wrote `theme` looks at a key that nothing reads. MiMo Code itself does not read it either. The migration is the moment to say so, and "unhandled key" is a weaker and vaguer sentence about the same fact. The sentence also says where the `theme`, `keybinds` and `tui` settings moved: to `tui.json`. For `tui` itself the sentence says the document is already in force, so an import changes nothing about it.
- `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` and `MIMOCODE_CONFIG_DEFAULTS` are not read. They are inline JSON in the environment, so a read parses an arbitrary value a parent process exported. A report cannot attribute such a document to a file. `MIMOCODE_AUTH_CONTENT` is the same, though `auth/index.ts:76` still reads it as a fallback. `util/credential-env.ts:15` strips it from the environment of every child the engine spawns, because any child can otherwise read the whole `auth.json` out of it.
- A credential sentence says the file was not opened. It does not imply the importer checked. The instruction is to sign in again. A migration that copied an access token also has to explain why the token is in two files now.
- The report names unhandled keys per layer, not over the merged document. The merged document's top-level keys are the union of every file's. One line over it names each file's unhandled keys against whichever file the user reads first.
- There is no mode pair and no environment claim in this planner. MiMo Code has no permission mode at all. `permission` is a rule map whose unmatched default is `ask` (`permission/evaluate.ts:14`), so there is no mode-and-sandbox pair to claim. The 41-key document has no `env` block, so there is nothing to carry into `settings.env`.

### Alma

**Alma is closed source, so the evidence is different here.** Every path claim in the Alma modules comes from the shipped bundle, `out/main/index.js` inside `app.asar` (Alma 0.4.160). That bundle is minified to two lines, so a line number is a lie. The comments carry the string literal instead, because a literal is searchable and a line number drifts with the next build.

**The four roots**

- The Electron `userData` root is `%APPDATA%\alma` on Windows. It holds the only real database, `chat_threads.db`, and the plugin storage.
- `~/.config/alma/` holds the identity documents, `skills/`, `plugins/`, `mcp.json`, `hooks.json`, `groups/`, `cron/` and `memory/`. It is the only root this importer reads files from.
- `~/.alma/` holds `bin/`, `npm-cache/`, `activity-records/` and `cache/`. Nothing in it is importable, and it is still a detection root on purpose. A user who only installed the CLI has this and nothing else.
- `~/alma/` is written without the dot, and it is the most-missed path in the product. It holds the chrome-extension stable copy and `worktrees/`. `.alma` and `alma` are two different directories in the same home. An importer that assumed the dot looks at a tree that is not there.
- The app never derives one root from another. The counts in the bundle are 39 occurrences of `getPath("userData")`, 78 of `".config","alma"`, 15 of `".alma",`, and one of `"alma","chrome-extension"`.
- The four root names are attested as joined segments rather than as substrings. Both `".alma","bin"` and its three siblings appear, and `"alma","chrome-extension"` appears once inside `getStablePath()`.
- The `userData` directory name, `alma`, is the one claim not taken from a string literal. Electron derives the basename from the app's `name`/`productName` in `package.json`, and `this.dbPath=Y.join(e,"chat_threads.db")` uses whatever `getPath("userData")` returns. The `process.cwd()` fallback is why the name is a parameter. On a machine where `getPath` fails, the database lands beside the process. A report that named one path is wrong for the other case.
- `APPDATA` is the only environment variable, and only to reach the first root. Alma has no `ALMA_HOME`-style variable anywhere in the bundle. `source-env.ts` lists `APPDATA`, which keeps `readSources` hermetic on a machine with a live Alma install.
- There is deliberately no `process.platform` test in `almaAppData`. The variable is absent everywhere but Windows in practice, so the test changes nothing for a real home. It changes one case: a test that puts a `chat_threads.db` under a temporary `APPDATA` must be believed on every CI job. A supplied value is trusted on every platform, and an absent one is `undefined` on every platform.
- The macOS and Linux `userData` paths (`~/Library/Application Support` and the XDG data base) are not guessed at. A path this source names but did not read is better than one it names wrongly, and the report says which platform it is on.
- Nothing in the module calls `os.homedir()`. The `home` argument is the only home source, which lets a test point at a fixture.

**Detection uses all four roots**

- The order is `~/.config/alma`, the `userData` root, `~/.alma`, `~/alma`. The first two carry importable content, so a home with more than one is detected by the one that has something to read.
- Each of the other roots has a state where it is the only one that answers. A user who installed the app and never opened it has `~/.config/alma` alone. A headless user has `~/.alma` alone. The `userData` root is all a user who only ever chatted has.
- The `userData` root also makes detection fire for a user who deleted `~/.config/alma` to clean up. That user lost the settings and kept the conversations.
- `userData` is `null` off Windows and is absent from the list there, rather than replaced by a guessed path.
- A root counts as present when it exists and holds something (`readdirSync` length above zero, inside a `try`/`catch` that answers false).

**Files under the configuration root**

- `mcp.json` is quoted whole: `this.configPath=J.join(n.getPath("home"),".config","alma","mcp.json")`. The shape is `{"mcpServers":{…}}`, and the variant is chosen by `"command" in e` versus `"url" in e`, two one-line functions that exist for no other purpose.
- `hooks.json` is quoted in two pieces, so a search for the assembled literal `".config","alma","hooks.json"` finds nothing. The name is hoisted into a constant first. This is the clearest Alma example of a trap that this repository meets again and again. A search for the assembled path is not a search for the path. The same holds for every name below, where `mcp.json` is inline and `SOUL.md` is not.
- The other paths are `skills/`, `plugins/`, `memory/`, `groups/` and `cron/` under the configuration root, plus `<userData>/chat_threads.db`, `<userData>/plugin-storage/<plugin id>`, `~/alma/worktrees` and `~/alma/chrome-extension`.

**Identity documents**

- The five are `SOUL.md`, `USER.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `SECURITY.md` and `HEARTBEAT.md`, in the product's load order.
- None of them is an instruction document. `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and `ALMA.md` appear zero times in the bundle. That is a literal count over 3,104,824 characters and not an inference. What Alma has is a persona and a relationship, in five files.
- The bare-name counts are `SOUL.md` 13, `USER.md` 22, `MEMORY.md` 4, `SECURITY.md` 3 and `HEARTBEAT.md` 10. The 22 for `USER.md` is three `getOwnerInfo()` implementations that are almost the same (Telegram, Discord, Feishu), not 22 documents.
- A sixth file, `~/.config/alma/api-spec.md`, is written by the app on every start-up. It is a file the product creates and the user reads, not a settings key, and it carries the whole `interface AppSettings { … }` declaration.
- Only `MEMORY.md` and the files in `memory/` have destinations here. The other four are named in the report.

**Skills, six roots and a seventh**

- The six roots, in the product's order: `~/.config/alma/skills`, `~/.claude/skills`, `~/.codex/skills`, `~/.agents/skills`, `~/.claude/plugins`, then for a workspace `<workspace>/.agents/skills` and `<workspace>/.alma/skills`.
- Five of the six belong to somebody else, and the split is the whole reason the importer does not loop over the list. Alma's own two roots are imported. `~/.agents/skills` and `<workspace>/.agents/skills` are shared with this repository's `agents` source, so they are named and never read. `KIMI_SHARED_TREE` in `kimi-read.ts` is the precedent, and a test plans `alma` and `agents` together over one shared skill and asserts exactly one write. The three foreign roots are other products' homes. The report names them and stops there.
- The seventh root is not in a home at all: `process.resourcesPath/bundled-skills`, resolved by `resolveBundledSkillsDir()` against six candidate paths. It is Alma's shipped content, and the user never edited it.
- Precedence is first match wins. A shadowed skill is invisible in Alma: the product loads the higher-priority copy and never reads the loser. A reader that imported both hands the user a skill Alma never loads. A reader that imported only the first says nothing about the second. `RawAlma` carries a collision list and the report prints it.
- The bundled count is counted, not quoted: 40 directories in the 0.4.160 win-x64 package, and each one holds exactly one `SKILL.md`. The names include `alchemy`, `browser`, `computer-use`, `plan-mode` and `web-fetch`. Alma copies them into `~/.config/alma/skills` on first run behind a `.bundled-skills-migrated` marker, so a migrated home holds both copies. The report says "40 of these are Alma's own bundled skills and are not yours".
- A skill is a directory that holds a `SKILL.md` whose frontmatter carries `name` and `description`. `parseSkillMd` is a nested ternary that refuses a file with either value absent. Each refusal gets its own log line. The frontmatter fence is `/^---\n([\s\S]*?)\n---\n?([\s\S]*)$/` after CRLF normalization, so a file with no `---` fence is refused before the YAML is parsed. This importer applies the same two requirements and reports each refusal by path. A skill Alma ignored is a skill the user believes is loaded.

**Hooks**

- Alma has four events: `tool.willExecute`, `tool.didExecute`, `chat.message.willSend` and `app.willQuit`. Each is attested by a `trigger`/`getMatchTarget` pair, and there is no other hook event.
- Three map onto this build's events by rename: `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse` and `UserPromptSubmit`. The map is `ALMA_HOOK_EVENT_MAP`, exported from `alma-home.ts` so a test can check the table rather than this prose.
- `app.willQuit` has no counterpart and is not mapped to `SessionEnd`. That event fires when a conversation ends. This one fires when the application quits, a different moment with different work left over.
- A `tool.*` matcher tests the tool name. A `chat.message.willSend` matcher tests the message content, and this build tests every matcher against `payload.tool_name`, which is empty for a prompt event. A content pattern matches the empty string and fires on everything, so the importer drops those matchers and counts them.
- The default matcher is `^$`, tested against `""`. An empty pattern matches everything, so an Alma matcher of `""` is not "match nothing".
- The default timeout is `10_000` ms (`r.timeout ?? 1e4`), and the unit is already this build's. A converter written against the sources that use seconds turns a ten-second hook into one of 10,000 seconds. The 600,000 ms clamp still applies.

**Settings are a row, not a file**

- `app_settings` has two occurrences in the bundle, both the same table. `DEFAULT 'default'` is not decoration: `getSettings()` reads `where(is.id, "default")` and `saveSettings()` writes the same literal, so there is exactly one settings row per database.
- The reader reads the blob through a whitelist of 18 top-level keys, and that choice is the whole credential story for this source. Alma's settings object is the application's entire configuration state. `chromeRelayAuthToken`, `tts.apiKey`, `network.proxy.password` and the bridge tokens for Telegram, Discord, Feishu and Weixin are ordinary members of it. So are `general.theme` and `chat.defaultModel`.
- A whitelist cannot leak. A key outside the list is never copied out of the blob. A credential-shaped key that a future release adds has no path to a planner, a plan, a report or a written file. The price is that a new key nobody adds to the list gets an unhandled report line, and `RawAlma.unhandledSettings` exists so that cost is visible.
- The whitelist comes from the `interface AppSettings` the app itself ships. That is one coherent schema rather than a set of forty call sites. It is not complete. At least nine top-level keys the app reads are absent from the declared interface. `alma-read.ts` states the discrepancy rather than hides it.
- `ALMA_SETTINGS_NOT_READ` is the credential boundary written down. It comes from `saveSettings(` and `JSON.parse(settingsData)` call sites. Every entry is written by a live code path and read by another. The names are `chromeRelayAuthToken`, `telegram`, `discord`, `feishu`, `weixin`, `heartbeat`, `crystal`, `threadBrief`, `mobileRelay`, `mobile`, `team`, `tts` and `plugin-provider-models:*`. `general.defaultWorkspaceId` and `general.windowsShell` are also beyond the shipped interface. A `*` at the end is a prefix match, and the ids there are the user's plugin ids.
- The list exists so a report can say why a key was passed over without a search of the minified bundle. `isAlmaCredentialKey` is exported because the sentence differs. A known credential key gets `it is a credential or a container of one, and nothing under it was opened`. An unknown key gets `this importer has no mapping for it`.
- The narrowed settings are `theme`, `windowsShell`, `defaultModel`, `temperature`, `maxTokens`, `autoCompact` (`enabled`, `threshold`, `keepRecentMessages`), `autoApproveToolRequests`, `agentsEnabled`, `allowSubagentDelegation`, `memoryEnabled`, `networkTimeout`, `fontSize`, `workspacePath` and `hashlineEdit`.
- What is deliberately not narrowed: no `general.language`, no `general.autoStart`, no `network.proxy` (a credential), no `tts` (a credential). The set also leaves out `keybindings`, `terminal`, `webSearch` and `whisper` (no equivalent here). The reader reads `themeConfig` only for the mode that `general.theme` names.

**Threads and the archive**

- The drizzle schema the app queries through declares 22 `chat_threads` columns: `id, title, model, tools, tools_compact_view, prompt_app_id, workspace_id, artifact_workspace_id, is_generating, is_favorited, is_favorite_pinned, favorite_pinned_order, sidebar_pin_slot, is_incognito, enable_artifacts, parent_thread_id, reasoning_effort, fast_mode, skill_ids, metadata, created_at, updated_at`. The `CREATE TABLE` lists eight of them, and fifteen `ALTER TABLE chat_threads ADD COLUMN` statements follow. One repeats a column the `CREATE` already made. The "eight columns" and "eighteen columns" a reader may quote are both wrong. None of the columns is a path.
- A thread's directory is `workspace_id` resolved through `workspaces.path`. The column is `REFERENCES workspaces(id) ON DELETE SET NULL`, added after the fact behind a bare `try`/`catch` that swallows a duplicate-column error. A deleted workspace leaves its threads with no path at all rather than a stale one, and there is no fallback. The application's own thread-creation path writes `metadata: {}`, so that blob does not stand in for a directory. `chat_messages` holds `id, thread_id, parent_id, slot_id, depth, message, timestamp, metadata, created_at, updated_at` and refers to its thread with `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so a deleted thread takes its rows with it.
- This source reports that plain fact and writes no placeholder. An empty `cwd`, counted under "no directory recorded", makes `narrowCandidates` drop the session. A wrong `cwd` files a conversation under a project it was never had in. `HistoryCandidate.cwdSubstitute` carries the assumed directory plus a flag to the report.
- The default `--history-scope` is `cwd`. `cwdSubstitute` is therefore the difference between a plan that holds the user's conversations and a plan that holds none while the report looks clean.
- Threads whose title starts with `⏰ Cron:` are never archived (`title NOT LIKE '⏰ Cron:%'` in both archive queries, re-checked in `archiveThread`).
- The archive root is one workspace: `initializeThreadArchiver` reads `settings.workspace.path || getOrCreateDefaultWorkspace().path` and joins `"threads"` onto it. Every archived `.md` sits under that one path whatever project the thread was had in, and the file never records which project it came from.
- Only `text` parts survive the archive (`(parts || []).filter(p => p.type === "text")`). Every tool call, image and thought block is absent by construction.
- The filename is `${created_at.substring(0,10)}_${title-slug.substring(0,60)}.md`, where the slug has `[/\\:*?"<>|]` replaced with `_` and whitespace collapsed to `_`. Two threads from the same day with the same first sixty title characters collide, and the second overwrites the first.
- The archive frontmatter keys are `threadId`, `title`, `createdAt`, `updatedAt`, `model` and `messageCount`, in that order.

**The credential surface**

- `providers.api_key` is a plaintext column (`api_key TEXT NOT NULL`), and the product's own `api-spec.md` says the keys are stored encrypted. The documentation and the schema disagree, and the schema is what the bytes do. The importer's `SELECT` names its columns and never reads this one.
- `secrets.encrypted_value` is `enc:` plus base64 or `plain:` plus base64. Electron's `safeStorage` falls back to plaintext when it is unavailable, so the `enc:` prefix is not a guarantee. The table is `secrets(name PRIMARY KEY, encrypted_value, note, env DEFAULT 1, …)`.
- `mcp_oauth_tokens` holds `access_token`, `refresh_token`, `client_secret` and `code_verifier` as plaintext columns, and `email_accounts.password` is plaintext.
- The credential files are `<userData>/.claude_subscription_token`, `<userData>/.copilot_accounts/<id>.token`, `<userData>/weixin-state/credentials.json`, `<userData>/plugin-storage/<id>/secrets.json` and `~/alma/chrome-extension/config.json`, whose whole content is `{ port, token }`.
- Two product behaviours make its own environment a credential surface. `getEnvSecrets()` returns `{ name: value }` for every secret whose `env` column is set. That column defaults to 1, so the default user has every stored secret in every shell command's environment. `redactSecretValues` replaces each value in tool output with `[REDACTED:<NAME>]`. An exported transcript may therefore carry those markers as literal text. The guard has a hole: its condition is `r.value.length < 6 || t.includes(r.value) && (…)`, and `||` binds looser than `&&`. A secret shorter than six characters is never redacted.
- The function is named in a report line and never called. That is a fact about the product, not a licence to go and search.

**The dormant MCP store and the built-in export**

- `mcp_servers(id, registry_id, name, description, config JSON, enabled, status, last_error, installed_at, updated_at)` is a real table with real CRUD methods (`getAllMCPServers`, `getEnabledMCPServers`, `getMCPServerById` and more). The configuration routes never write it: the manager's `saveConfig` writes `mcp.json` and nothing else touches the table.
- The result is not academic. The app's own `GET /api/data/export` reads the table. Alma's built-in export can therefore emit an empty `mcpServers.json` on an install whose servers are all in `mcp.json` and active. The export also strips `env` from every entry it writes (`config: { …e.config, env: undefined }`).
- This importer reads `mcp.json` and only names the table. The file is what the product runs, and the table is what nothing writes.
- A user may take the built-in export before this migration, and it is not the same thing. It writes a zip of 10 Alma-shaped files (`providers.json`, `threads.json`, `promptApps.json`, `prompts.json`, `workspaces.json`, `mcpServers.json`, `customThemes.json`, `memories.json`, `settings.json`, `manifest.json`). It omits secrets, skills, hooks and every document in `~/.config/alma`, and its `mcpServers.json` may be empty for the reason above. `POST /api/data/import` is the counterpart, and it is Alma's own format rather than this build's.

**The reader**

- Settings come from `app_settings.settings_data`, read through a read-only open of `<userData>/chat_threads.db`. There is no `settings.json` to find, and that literal appears twice in the bundle in contexts that are not this.
- Read-only is enforced twice: `readonly: true` stops a write, and the queries name their columns rather than take `*`. A `*` puts every provider's plaintext key into the process heap, where a report line or a thrown error can carry it.
- A settings blob that will not parse produces `null`, not `{}`. An empty object is a real answer ("Alma stored settings and they were empty"). The three states are an object, an empty object and `null`.
- The failure sentences are kept apart. One is no application-data directory. Another is no `chat_threads.db`, which Alma creates on first launch. Others are a database that opened with no readable `app_settings` row, and a row whose `settings_data` is not text. A blob that is not a JSON object is the last one. "There is no database" and "there is one and this row is damaged" are different sentences, and only the second is a problem.
- One skip line is written per key the user's blob actually carries and the importer did not read. A walk over the whole catalogue instead puts fourteen credential lines under a user who has two. It also reports keys the blob never held. That is the difference between a report and a leaflet.
- The reader reads providers for their `id`, `name` and `type` only, sorted by name. `type` is one of eighteen values, and only `anthropic` and `openai` mean anything to this build's model registry. The thread count comes from `SELECT COUNT(*) AS n FROM chat_threads`.
- The archive workspace comes from `settings.workspace.path` first, then from the `Default` row at `<userData>/workspaces/default` that the app creates. The archive count is `.md` files in its `threads/` directory, none of them read.
- The five identity documents are checked with `statSync`. Absence is the ordinary state for four of them and gets no line.
- Six built-in skip lines name the roots that are present and not read. The first is the `userData` root, opened read-only for `app_settings`, `providers` and the thread count. Nothing else in it is opened, and its credential files and tables are named. `plugins/` holds a `plugin.json`, `skills/`, `agents/`, `hooks/hooks.json` and `mcp.json` per plugin, and its state lives under `plugin-storage/`. `cron/` holds a prompt plus a delivery channel such as Telegram or Discord, with no equivalent here. `~/alma/worktrees` is a checkout of the user's own repository, not agent state. `~/.alma` holds binaries, an npm cache, activity records and a cache. `~/alma` is the extension copy, whose `config.json` is a credential. That last line exists so its absence from the import is not read as an empty root.
- Every read that fails becomes a line in `RawAlma.skipped` that names what failed and why. Nothing throws, because a migration that aborts on one damaged file loses every other source's import to make a point about that file. `antigravity-read.ts` and `qoder-read.ts` use the same convention.
- A skipped name is a label, not a resolved path. It is a bare name where that is unambiguous, and a forward-slashed relative label where it is not. No skipped line ever holds a value read out of a credential-shaped key.
- The scrub of `mcp.json` happens in the reader rather than the planner. `RawAlma` is what the report and the test suite are written from, so a credential in it is one `JSON.stringify(raw)` away from an issue. The importer drops `headers` whole, and replaces `env` values with their names. The names survive so the report can tell the user which variables to set again. The `env` block is walked with the map-slot exemption. A server name that reads as a credential, such as `keyboard-mcp`, must not delete the server. The URL is not scrubbed here. `alma-plan.ts` checks it with the shared `urlCredentialProblem`, because the answer is not "remove the credential" but "do not import this server at all".
- `hooks.json` is handed over as it stands to `normalizeClaudeHooks`, which knows this build's event names. A file with no `hooks` key produces `undefined`. That is the difference between "Alma has no hooks" and "the file was there and held nothing".
- `readAlma` never calls `os.homedir()` or `process.cwd()`, so a fixture home and a real home run the same code path. `env` defaults to `process.env`, which is what `readSources` passes, so a developer on Windows gets their real `%APPDATA%\alma`. A test that asserts on content must pass `env` rather than rely on the ambient block. This is the one way a fixture test can read a real home by mistake, with no warning.

**History**

- The database location has no `home` argument, which is worth a line of its own. The only Alma database lives under Electron's `userData` root, which `APPDATA` names on its own. A reader that searches `~/.alma/chat_threads.db` or `~/.config/alma/chat_threads.db` finds nothing. Every other session reader here takes `home`, and this one cannot.
- The query that lists threads uses a `LEFT JOIN`. An inner join drops every orphaned thread, and an orphan is precisely the case the report exists to say something about.
- The column list is explicit for the same reason `readAlma`'s is. No `api_key`-shaped column exists in this table at all, and the list is still explicit. `metadata` is a JSON blob whose keys are the app's business, and a `*` is one keystroke away from a credential. Two columns stay unread.
- Messages come back `ORDER BY created_at ASC, depth ASC`. `depth` is the column the app keeps for a branch of the conversation. A message edited into existence later still sorts after what it was edited from, not by its own timestamp.
- The `message` column is a JSON blob with the shape `{ role, parts: [{ type, … }] }`. Only `type: "text"` parts contribute, which is Alma's own filter and the reason its markdown archive has no tool calls in it. The role passes through verbatim, with the app's own archiver as the rule (`t = "user" === e.role ? "User" : "assistant" === e.role ? "Assistant" : e.role`). A row can carry a role this build has no message type for.
- The importer counts rows whose blob will not parse and does not pass any of them over. A transcript with a hole in it is still worth an import, and the count tells the user there was one.
- Only `user` and `assistant` become turns. A third role joins the count, and the count reaches the report. A silent drop produces a transcript with a hole and no explanation.
- No tool calls are reconstructed, and that is a limitation rather than a choice. The whole message JSON is in the blob, so a tool call is there as a part with a `tool-` type and an `input`. To rebuild one, the importer has to invent an id, a name and the `toolResult` row that answers it. This build rejects a transcript that half-pairs them. A tool call with no result, or the reverse, fails the messages API on the first `--continue`, and that is worse than a shorter transcript. The text survives and the actions do not, and `alma-plan.ts` says so where the user reads about their conversations.
- `ALMA_NO_WORKSPACE` is a constant rather than a string built at the call site, so a test can assert the text. A note whose reason drifts is a note no reader can check. The reason names the workspace that was never set or was deleted. It says the thread was listed under Alma's own configuration root so it is reachable, and filed there on import.

**The planner**

- The source id is a plain literal, `alma`, with no cast, spelled once in the module. The union and every table keyed by it live in `migrate-types.ts`. The call that reaches this planner lives in `migrate.ts`.
- The planner claims two scalars and one mode pair. The mode pair is the one to watch. One boolean produces both halves, and the report says which half Alma never expressed. No key in Alma's schema chooses a permission mode, and no key chooses a sandbox.
- `security.autoApproveToolRequests` absent claims nothing, because Alma's `false` is not a choice: `!0 === t?.security?.autoApproveToolRequests` reads `false` for `undefined`. A stricter posture written on the user's behalf is the fail-open failure `t3-plan.ts` argues at length. Qoder's `planQoderPermissionMode` holds the same posture and declines to import a schema default.
- `true` maps to `agent` plus `workspace-write`. The mode half is `agent`, because the switch is Alma's "never ask" and nothing else, and the single call site approves with `{approved: true, reason: "approved", action: "allow_once"}`.
- The sandbox half is deliberately not `danger-full-access`, which is what a source whose one value means both gets (Qoder's `bypass_permissions`). Alma has no unconfined setting anywhere, so that choice invents a permission the user never granted. `workspace-write` is the default confinement of a mode that does not ask. The report says exactly that, and it does not call the pair a faithful copy.
- A non-boolean in that key claims nothing, and the reader is what makes that true: `booleanAt` answers `undefined` for anything that is not a boolean. Alma itself tests the key with `=== true`, so a string was off there too.
- `chat.defaultModel` is `"<providerId>:<modelId>"`, from the shipped interface and the picker's resolver (`` `${o.id}:${o.models[0]}` ``). The provider half is not a labunbun provider id and cannot become one: `providers.id` is a row id the app generated. The module resolves the model half alone. A bare family alias (`opus`, `sonnet`) has no provider half in Alma's format, so it goes through the shared resolver. A resolved value names the provider by its user-visible `name` and `type`, and never by its `api_key`.
- An unresolved model is not rounded to the nearest one. A near-match is a different model with the same name. A `providerId` that names no row gets a report line. That is the state of a user who deleted a provider the picker still remembers.
- `general.theme` maps `light` and `dark` onto the built-in names. `system` is a choice rather than an absent value, and it means "follow the terminal". A pin to a named theme writes an appearance the user declined. A value outside the declared three gets a report line: the settings UI did not write it, and the appearance stays as it is.
- Ten reported settings keys get a skip line with their reason: `temperature`, `maxTokens`, `autoCompact`, `agentsEnabled`, `allowSubagentDelegation`, `memoryEnabled`, `networkTimeout`, `fontSize`, `windowsShell` and `hashlineEdit`. The last is absent from the interface Alma ships, so it is a drift between the app and its own documentation.
- Key presence, not the value, decides the variant of an MCP entry. `command: ""` is a stdio server. An entry with both a command and a URL is a server Alma's manager starts twice, with two shapes. Both cases get a line of their own rather than a guess.
- `transport` defaults to `streamable-http`, and only an entry that says `sse` uses SSE. That fallback is a connection-time behaviour rather than a second declared transport. This build has one HTTP client, so an `sse` entry connects the same way. The report states that fact and does not call it a rename. An unknown transport reads as streamable-http, and the reader records the spelling.
- The URL is the third credential channel and there is no half-measure. A URL with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` removed points at nothing. A written version trades a credential on disk for a server that fails at connect time under a clean report line. The server is not carried across at all. The reason names which of the two shapes it found, and it prints none of the value. `containsSecret` is `true`, because the value the line is about is a credential. A future reader that filters for "was anything here a secret?" must not have to re-derive that. The rule dates from Qoder. Every source here uses it.
- An absolute `cwd` is kept, and a relative one is left off, because it means a different directory on each machine. The module drops arguments that are not strings and counts each one, and an `args` that is not an array adds nothing. A server that fails the shared `McpServerConfigSchema` gets a skip line with the reason.
- A name already in the target stays as it is unless the user sets `--force`. The module computes the `secret` value of a carried server rather than assumes it. A future change that starts to copy a value has to make it `true` on purpose.
- The module translates the hook vocabulary before `normalizeClaudeHooks` sees it. Alma's four raw names reach the normalizer only after that step. Without it, all four land in `droppedEvents`, and a configuration that works reads as four unknown events.
- The importer skips a handler with `enabled: false`. To switch one on here runs something the user switched off there. The empty-events skip path also states that count, which has no claim line to carry it.
- Alma lets a hook rewrite the tool arguments or the message it is about to send, through a return value of `{ decision, reason, updatedInput }`. The block half (`decision: "block"` and exit code 2) is understood the same way here. The rewrite half has no equivalent, so such a hook arrives as one that can block and cannot edit.
- The report counts a dropped matcher with its reason. This build escapes a matcher's pattern characters, so the carried matcher matches a literal rather than the tool it named. Alma runs every hook through `sh -c`, as this build does, and the default timeout of 10,000 ms applies to a handler that named none.
- The copy of a skill is verbatim, as both shapes are a directory that holds a `SKILL.md` with `name` and `description`. The reader reads only Alma's own two roots, and the report names the five it does not, in two categories with two reasons.
- `MEMORY.md` is the index and a file in `memory/` is an entry, told apart by name. Both become rule files with distinct names (`imported-alma-memory-index.md` and `imported-alma-<name>.md`). A fold of the index into the entries makes a claim about it that Alma's own reader does not. When more than one document comes across, a downgrade line says that what pointed at what is now the file names.
- The reader does not second-guess a file name in `memory/`. A file that does not look like a date is carried as an entry and named in the report.

**Why the leftovers matter**

- The lines that matter most are the ones about things a user assumes came across. They are the four identity documents with no equivalent and the five skill roots that are not Alma's. The 40 bundled skills are the product's own. Two more lines carry the dormant facts about Alma's own export, and one carries the thread archive with its three traps.
- The four identity documents are named with what they are. `SOUL.md` is a persona rather than a project instruction file, and `USER.md` is who to address rather than what to do. `SECURITY.md` is a prose rules block, and it cannot become a tool-and-pattern permission rule. `HEARTBEAT.md` is a checklist for an unattended session, with no hook to attach it to.
- The skip line for the providers says this run read them for their id, name and type only and did not migrate them. A provider is a base URL, a wire format and a credential, and this build's provider set has none of those three shapes. The `api_key` column was not read, and the line repeats the contradiction with `api-spec.md`.
- The threads skip line says `chat_threads` has 22 columns and none of them is a directory. The conversations are imported by the history phase instead.
- `planAlma` has no `claimEnv`, and the absence carries the decision. The only environment variable Alma stores a value under is an MCP server's, and those do not come across.

### Qoder

**Every path claim in this section is quoted from the shipped bundle.** The citations are `out/main/index.js`, the extracted Electron main bundle of Qoder 0.4.3, and `@qoder-ai/qoder-agent-sdk/dist/index.js`, both under the extraction this importer was written against. The desktop bundle is 12 MB over about 11,000 lines, so a line number cannot be used. Each comment carries the literal it came from, because a literal is searchable and does not drift with the next build.

**The home resolution exists twice, and this source follows the SDK's rule, which is also the desktop's fuller one.** The SDK's rule (`fc` plus `mc`) reads `configDirEnv` first, else `join(cliHome ?? homedir(), validatedDirName)`. The desktop's simpler `wfe` reads `QODER_CONFIG_DIR?.trim()` first, else `join(QODER_CLI_HOME?.trim(), ".qoder")`, else `join(homedir(), ".qoder")`. That branch hard-codes `.qoder` and never reads `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME`.

The fuller desktop copy of the SDK rule is in `WV`. There the second half is a `pxt` call on `` `${prefix}_CONFIG_DIR_NAME` ``: the same validator, called the same way. Nothing in the module calls `os.homedir()`. The `home` argument is the only home source, which lets a test point at a fixture.

**The directory names**

- `QODER_DEFAULT_DIR` is `.qoder`. The SDK ships both descriptors as one frozen pair and picks between them at module load on `const zn = xr === "cn"` where `xr = "global"`. The global branch is `configDirName: ".qoder"`. The desktop carries the same two names in its own table, `const $Kr = { qoder: ".qoder", "qoder-cn": ".qoder-cn" }`, used for the app-status file, which is a different derivation.
- `QODER_CN_DEFAULT_DIR` is `.qoder-cn`, from the SDK's `qoder-cn` descriptor. The same descriptor gives `projectConfigDirName: ".qoder"`, so a CN install writes its user home under `.qoder-cn` and its project settings under `.qoder`. The importer reads the CN name only to name the tree it did not read, through `qoderOtherConfigDir`.
- `QODER_PROJECT_DIR` is `.qoder` for both builds: `projectConfigDirName: ".qoder"` in the SDK's global descriptor and in its `qoder-cn` one. A reader who assumes the CN build renames everything gets this half wrong. One `.qoder` project directory then holds a global install's project settings and a CN install's at the same path.

**The directory-name check is reproduced, not guessed**

- The limit is 64 code points.
- The character rule is `/^[\p{L}\p{N}\p{M}._ -]+$/u`, verbatim. The same regex appears twice in the product under two minified names: `cxt` in the desktop bundle and `rc` in the SDK. Both copies carry the same bytes. `\p{M}` covers the marks that combine, so a decomposed name is legal. Both implementations normalize to NFC first, which is why the validator normalizes before the tests.
- The device-name rule is `/^(?:con|prn|aux|nul|com[1-9]|lpt[1-9])(?:\.|$)/iu`, verbatim. The `(?:\.|$)` tail is what makes `con.txt` a match and `content` not one. Without it `console` is refused, and a user can have a directory named `console`.
- The reserved set is byte-for-byte the `new Set([...])` in both copies: `.git`, `.svn`, `.hg`, `.vscode`, `.idea`, `.husky`, `.github`, `node_modules` and `bower_components`. The lookup is a membership test, so it is exact and does not match a name that merely starts with one of these. A configuration home named `.git` holds agent state inside a repository's own metadata.
- `validateQoderDirName` reproduces the product's checks in the product's own order. NFC comes first, then the length by code point: `Array.from(t).length`, so 64 astral characters pass where 64 UTF-16 units count as 128 code points. Then come the shape tests: no space at the start or the end, no dot at the end, and neither `.` nor `..`. Then the character pattern, then the device pattern, then the reserved set in lowercase. Here that step is `toLowerCase()`. The desktop uses `toLocaleLowerCase("en-US")` and the SDK uses `toLowerCase()`. The set is ASCII, so both agree on every name this can refuse, and the answer never depends on the machine's locale.
- The function returns the reason rather than a boolean, because the report needs it. A name the importer refused and a name it accepted must be distinguishable. A user reads the report to learn why their `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME` did not take effect. The two product spellings stay apart: the desktop says `"<ENV> is not a valid configuration directory name."`, and the SDK says `"<ENV> must be a valid directory name"` or `"… must not use a protected directory name"`. They are different code paths, and a mixed message names a check that one of the two paths does not have.
- The check is a migration concern rather than trivia. The name becomes a path segment under the home, and steps 3 to 6 stop a name that tries to escape it. An importer without them reads `~/.git/…` on a machine configured that way, a directory the product itself refuses to use.

**The resolution rules**

- `QODER_CONFIG_DIR` wins outright and is used verbatim. The SDK does `B.resolve(cwd, env[configDirEnv])` and never consults the other two names. It is not validated, because it is a whole path rather than a segment. It is resolved against the current directory exactly as the SDK does (`B.resolve(e, env[v.configDirEnv])`).
- `QODER_CLI_HOME` is a parent, not a home. The SDK computes `r = o ? B.resolve(cwd, o) : homedir()` and then `B.join(r, s.user)`, so the directory name is appended to whatever `QODER_CLI_HOME` names. A reader that treats it as the home itself looks one level too high.
- A blank value is unset. The SDK tests `env[x] === undefined || === ""`. The desktop uses `?.trim()` then truthiness. So a whitespace-only value is not unset to the desktop, and it is a path of `" "` to the SDK's `B.resolve`. This importer trims first, which follows the desktop and matches what a user who set the variable by accident gets.
- `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME` is validated, and an invalid value does not fall back silently. Both implementations `throw` rather than substitute the default, so a user who set it to something Qoder refuses runs a Qoder that refuses to start. The importer must not read `~/.qoder` in that case, because that is a tree the product itself rejected. It uses the default and reports the refusal through `QoderHomes.rejectedDirName`, so the report says the override was refused.
- `GEMINI_CLI_HOME` is deliberately not honoured, and the two bundles disagree. The SDK's settings loader reads `env[v.cliHomeEnv] ?? env.GEMINI_CLI_HOME` before it falls back to `homedir()`, which is a Qoder-is-a-Gemini-CLI-fork artifact. The other copy of the same derivation in the SDK, `qa() { return process.env[Oa] || Ia() }`, does not read it, and the variable appears zero times in the desktop bundle. A user with that variable set for the Gemini CLI gets a different home from the SDK than from the desktop. This importer follows the desktop, the product whose data the import reads.

**One home per process**

- Qoder resolves one home per process: `mc()` returns a single `{ user, project }` pair, chosen at module load. A home that holds both `.qoder` and `.qoder-cn` is two installs, not two halves of one. A read of both imports two builds' settings into one file and two builds' conversations into one history. The tree that did not answer is named instead, the same treatment `t3-home.ts` gives to a dev build's other `userdata` tree.
- A tree counts as present when it exists and holds something (`readdirSync` length above zero, inside a `try`/`catch`).

**The three settings layers**

- `QODER_SETTINGS_SOURCES` is `["user", "project", "local"]`, the default `settingSources` (`oc`), so a reader that opens only `~/.qoder/settings.json` reads a third of what the product reads. The three files, from `fc`: user is `join(configDir, "settings.json")`, project is `join(resolve(cwd, v.projectConfigDir), "settings.json")`, and local is `join(resolve(cwd, v.projectConfigDir), "settings.local.json")`.
- Both project-layer files live under `v.projectConfigDir`. The two halves of `mc()` are not independent. With `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME` unset they are `v.configDirName` and `v.projectConfigDirName`. With it set, both become the configured name (`return {user: t, project: t}`). A user who renames the tree also renames the per-project one. A reader that looks for `<cwd>/.qoder/` finds nothing where their project settings are. That is why the project path helpers take `qoderDirName` and not the constant.

**What `settings.json` holds, and what it does not**

- The desktop app reads the document by field. Every call that names a literal names one of five keys: `hooks`, `mcpServers`, `enabledPlugins`, `pluginConfigs` and `chatSession.builtInBrowserHosts`. No field call names a permission mode, a theme or a model. Those three are what a migration from most sources is mostly about, and on Qoder 0.4.3 the file has none of them.
- That is the most important fact in this source, and the reason `planQoder` claims exactly one scalar.
- **No credential is expected, and that is checked rather than assumed.** The string `apiKey` appears four times in the bundle, every time in a BYOK flow or an IPC message. `g5t.seal` builds `{schemaVersion: 1, apiKey}` and hands the JSON to `protectionService.protectString`. What lands in SQLite is `byok_model_credentials.encrypted_payload BLOB NOT NULL`. The plaintext key is never in `settings.json`.
- The scrub exists anyway, because a guard that no reader can check is not a guard. The MCP path is the one place a credential can still appear. Qoder's own MCP reader throws `MCP_CONFIG_STATIC_CREDENTIAL_FORBIDDEN` on a literal `authorization` or `token`, so a future version can add one under a new name.

**The scrub, and the line it draws**

- The scrub removes every credential-shaped key from each parsed layer and records each by path, with the reason `looks like a credential — name only, value never read`. The importer drops the values, so no credential can reach a planner, a plan, a report or a written file.
- `looksLikeSecretName` matches `TOKEN`, `KEY`, `SECRET`, `PASSWORD` and `CREDENTIAL` as substrings, which covers `apiKey`, `accessToken` and `clientSecret`. Its real gap is the single word `authorization`, the one key Qoder's own MCP reader refuses. `bearerToken` matches `TOKEN` and `password` matches, so those are not gaps. The second matcher is `QODER_SECRET_KEY`, `/authorization/i`, and it exists for that one word.
- `QODER_ENTRY_MAP_KEYS` is the six merge-shallow keys plus `hooks`. The distinction is between a key that names a slot and a key that names a thing. `headers.authorization` is a slot, and a delete removes the credential and nothing else. `mcpServers.keyboard-mcp` is the name of a server a user created, and `looksLikeSecretName` matches `KEY` inside it. A naive walk deleted the whole server, its command, its arguments and its directory. So the scrub drops credential-shaped keys inside an entry and never the entry itself. The entry's own contents still leave scrubbed, each with a full-path `skipped` line. That is the guarantee `planQoderMcp` depends on. Its comment that a credential-shaped name was already gone stays true of `env`'s contents. It is no longer true of the server's own name.
- The membership is derived rather than guessed. The six keys are the ones the SDK's merge treats as one level deep, so they are maps the user keys by hand. `hooks` is map-shaped by a different rule, because the merge concatenates each event's group arrays. `hGr` takes any key of `hooks` as an event name. An event named `StopSessionSecret` is the sharpest case, because a delete there takes its handlers with it. No attested event in `QODER_HOOK_EVENTS` matches a credential word today, checked name by name against both matchers, so the exemption is not a live loss.
- The scan stops at depth 8 (`MAX_CREDENTIAL_SCAN_DEPTH`). The deepest attested key is three segments (`context.fileFiltering.customIgnoreFilePaths`) and the shallowest merge-policy key is one. A key deeper than the cap stays in place, which is the one thing the scrub can get wrong.
- The scrub runs on each layer **before** the merge, not on the merged result. `mcpServers` merges one level deep, so a token in a layer a later layer partly overrode is still walked and still dropped. A scrub of the merge's output alone leaves that copy in `layers`, where `provenance` can still name its file.

**The two halves disagree about JSON, and the recovery is a superset**

- The desktop's `readDocument` parses with `goe`, which rejects a comment or a comma after the last member as `SETTINGS_JSON_INVALID`. The SDK's loader calls `dc`, and `dc(t, path)` is `JSON.parse(lc(t))`. `lc` strips a BOM at the start, keeps string literals intact and removes `//` and block comments. `lc` does not handle a comma after the last member, so a file with one fails under both halves.
- So the reader tries plain `JSON.parse` first, then `parseJsonc` once, and takes the result only when it is a non-empty object. The reader reads a file that parses only on the second try with `recovered: true`, and every caller turns that into one `skipped` line.
- That recovery is a strict superset of the product's: `parseJsonc` strips comments and a comma after the last member. A file this importer can read is one neither Qoder half accepts, which is the opposite failure from the one the recovery normally covers. The report line says the desktop reader rejects this file outright.
- The failure reason is a fixed phrase, never the parser's message. A `SyntaxError` from `JSON.parse` quotes the text it choked on, which puts a fragment of the user's file into the report.

**How the reader reads the layers**

- An absent file is not an error. It is the normal state of two of the three layers, and a report line for an absent `settings.local.json` in every project is noise. A file that exists and does not parse changes what the merge produces, so it is named.
- The one case where the product reads fewer than three layers is reproduced. `fc` computes `resolve(cliHome) === resolve(cwd)` and skips both `cwd`-relative layers when the CLI home resolves to that same directory. Otherwise the project settings are the user's global settings read a second time from another path. A CLI home set to a project directory is a real configuration.
- `provenance` is the SDK's own record (`p[f] = {source: d.source, path: d.path}`, overwritten as the layers apply). It records the last layer that carried the key, not the layer whose value survived. The two differ for a key a later layer sets to a value the merge then drops. The product reports the former, so this reader does too.
- `qoderSettingsOrigin` answers with the key's last layer. It falls back to the user layer's path for a key the merge produced rather than a layer carried. `qoderSettingsSubkeyOrigin` narrows the label again, to the last layer that holds `key.subkey`. The SDK's provenance is keyed by top-level name alone. Without the extra check, a server the user added and a project never mentioned takes the project file's label. That label sends the user to edit a file the server is not in. The layer suffix exists to stop the same mistake in the other direction.
- The reader sorts `RawQoder.skipped` by name before it returns it. Two runs over one home produce the same report rather than one that changes with the filesystem's order.
- A settings read has three states: text, absent and unreadable. `statSync` runs first so a home that never installed Qoder produces no "unreadable" lines. A directory where a file was expected is its own sentence, because `<home>/memory` is a directory here and the settings document lives one level up.
- `configDir` is always a path, never a read. A home that never installed Qoder still gives the right answer for where one is. A "not found" line for every absent path buries the paths that matter.
- `settings` is `null` rather than `{}` when no layer was readable. An empty object lets a planner claim it read a document that held nothing. An absent file produces no line at all, because a home that only installed the CLI never wrote settings and that is the ordinary state.

**How the importer reads one home**

- Nothing here throws. Every read that fails becomes a line in `RawQoder.skipped` that says what failed and why, the convention `antigravity-read.ts` uses. A migration that aborts on one damaged file loses every other source's import to make a point about that file.
- A `skipped` name is a label, not a resolved path: a bare name where that is unambiguous, a forward-slashed relative label where it is not. No `skipped` line ever holds a value read out of a credential-shaped key.
- The reader is pure with respect to `home`, `cwd` and `env`: it never calls `os.homedir()` or `process.cwd()`. `cwd` is `string | undefined` rather than defaulted to `process.cwd()`. A default is the one way a test that forgot the argument reads whatever directory the test runner sits in.
- `env` defaults to `process.env`, which is what `readSources` passes. A developer with `QODER_CONFIG_DIR` set gets that tree, which is the right answer for their machine. This is the same trade `codex-home.ts` makes with `CODEX_HOME`. It is the one way a test that reads a fixture home can quietly read a real one. A test that asserts on content must pass `env` rather than rely on the ambient block.
- The read order is the settings layers, the fields of the merged document, memory and skills, and the transcript count. The paths that are named and not opened come last. Nothing short-circuits: a home with a damaged `settings.json` still yields its skills.
- The reader reads `mcpServers` and `hooks` into their fields, and they stay in the merged document. A reader that deleted them makes `reportUnhandledKeys` name them as unhandled, which is the opposite of the truth. An `mcpServers` that is not a JSON object yields an empty map and a `skipped` line that says no server was read.
- The desktop store field defaults to the real `%APPDATA%` only on win32. Elsewhere there is no such store and the field stays `null` rather than a path that never exists.

**The merge policy, reproduced**

- `pc` is reproduced verbatim: `uc` holds the shallow keys, `cc` holds the union paths, and `path.length === 2 && path[0] === "hooks"` gives `concat`. Everything else merges into.
- The extra `providers` clause in `pc` is carried because the product has it, but nothing reaches it. A shallow key leaves `Rn` before any recursion. The clause only runs from the branch that handles a target without the key. There that branch's own guard already failed, so the policy is not consulted. From there the next path is three segments long, which no clause matches. A mutation run over this file's tests confirms that the clause changes no output. An earlier draft credited the clause with the shallow treatment of one provider entry. The name in `uc` does that work.
- `hooks` is matched on arity, not on a name list. A hook event is `hooks.<Event>`, and its group arrays concatenate. Two layers that both define `PreToolUse` produce both groups rather than one entry that wins.
- Three shapes in `Rn` look like bugs and are not to be "fixed" here. A shallow key merges one level deep (`{...i, ...o}`). So `mcpServers` gains the later layer's names and loses the earlier layer's definitions of the names both declare. A shallow key set to two non-objects becomes `{}`, because both `Object.assign` calls are guarded by an is-record test. And the union dedupes by identity.
- `Rn` and the clone helpers also skip `undefined` values and the three prototype keys at every level.
- `<cwd>/.qoder/.mcp.json` is the project's own MCP file, named and never read. The desktop's settings-location table carries it as `sourcePath: join(projectRoot, ".qoder", ".mcp.json")` beside `scopeKind:"project"` and a `scopeId` of `sha256(projectRoot)`. It is a separate location from `settings.json`, and the three-layer merge above never reaches it. A plugin directory holds a third file with the same basename (`{path:".mcp.json",type:"file"}` in the plugin manifest's own file list). It is a different place with nothing to do with either.
- The `.qoder` in that path is a literal and takes no `env`, and that is not an oversight. The desktop writes a literal while the SDK's project settings directory follows `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME`. A user who renamed the tree therefore has their project `settings.json` under the new name, and their project `.mcp.json` still under `.qoder`. This helper is the one that is right for the second.
- Why that file is not read: a project directory is not something this importer may pick for itself. `runMigration` is given exactly one, so a read of that one is possible. The reason it does not is narrower: nothing in either bundle says how the file combines with `settings.json`. The desktop enumerates locations and canonicalises them (`canonicalProjectLocations`), but the merge that combines two `mcpServers` maps from two scopes is not something these bytes answer. An import of both that calls itself a merge is a guess with a report attached. It is named instead, so a user who knows they have one is told it stayed.

**The merge rules**

- Six top-level keys are merged one level deep rather than all the way, from `uc`: `mcpServers`, `providers`, `skillOverrides`, `enabledPlugins`, `extraKnownMarketplaces` and `pluginConfigs`. The merge copies both objects into a fresh one with `Object.assign`, so it is a merge and not a replacement. An earlier draft of the file called these keys "replaced wholesale", and the bytes do not support that.
- For `mcpServers` the names union: a project layer that declares `theirs` leaves a user's `mine` in place, and both run. The entries do not. A server declared in both layers is taken whole from the later one. The merge runs over the top-level map and not into each server. A project that redefines `mine` changes its command, arguments, the directory it runs in and environment wholesale and cannot inherit the user's version. A read of the user layer alone is still wrong for that reason. The desktop reads the field by name (`readField("mcpServers", {})`) and the SDK writes back to it, so the key is one per settings file.
- Thirteen dotted paths have their arrays unioned rather than replaced, from `cc`. The union is by `Set` identity (`[...new Set([...i, ...o])]`), so two structurally equal objects at the same position count as two entries. A reader that de-duplicates them invents a policy Qoder does not have.
- The importer drops three key names wherever they appear, from `Cn`: `__proto__`, `constructor` and `prototype`. That is the product's rule and not a precaution of this importer. `Rn` skips them at every level and `Et` will not copy them. A settings file that carries `__proto__` at any depth has it ignored over there too. A reader that copied them through is the only thing in the pipeline that takes the value.
- `mergeQoderSettings` is exported because it is the function worth an argument. It is pure and takes the documents as its argument. Every claim above about shallow merge, union and concatenation is a claim about this function. A reader of one layer is right about the file and wrong about the settings.

**Conversations**

- `<home>/projects` holds one directory for each directory that was used, and each one is named by the slug function. A session's transcript is `join(t.configDirectory, "projects", Bfe(t.cwd), \`${t.targetSessionId}.jsonl\`)`, the whole function, verbatim, with one call to the one non-`u` slug. So the shape is settled: a project directory for each directory that was used, one `.jsonl` per session inside it.
- What is not settled is what is in the file. The writer is the native `qoder-runtime-host` binary. It is not in the JavaScript bundle and not in the SDK, so no record format was readable from bytes. `qoder-session.ts` counts these files and reads none of them.
- The walk counts sessions, projects and bytes with none opened. The walk counts a project directory with no transcript apart, and it gets its own sentence. A project set up and never used is a real state, and a plain number excludes it without a report. The byte total is best effort. A file that leaves between the directory read and the `stat` is still counted. It was there a moment ago, and the count is what the report leads with.
