- The slug function replaces every character outside `a-z`, `A-Z` and `0-9` with a dash (`t.replace(/[^a-zA-Z0-9]/g, "-")`). A result longer than 200 UTF-16 units becomes its first 200 units, a dash and `Math.abs(qdr(t)).toString(36)`. The comparison runs on the already-replaced string in UTF-16 units, so the cap is on units and the hash covers the original path.
- The regex has no `u` flag on purpose, and the difference carries weight. Two copies exist in the product. The copy with `/g` is the one the transcript path (`vze`) and the per-project memory directory use. A second copy with `/gu` is used only by the session-bundle exporter. With no `u` flag, a character outside the basic plane matches per UTF-16 code unit. An emoji in a path becomes two dashes, where the other spelling makes one. The two spellings name different directories for the same `cwd`, and the product uses both. This function is the one the transcripts and the project memory directory are actually under. A directory with an astral character is the only case where the two differ. A wrong choice reports "no sessions" for a project that has them.
- `qoderProjectSlugUnicode` is the `/gu` spelling. It is exported because the difference is the kind that gets "fixed" by someone who reads the other function and assumes the flag was forgotten.
- The hash is `djb2` with XOR, exactly as the product spells it: `e = 5381`, then `e = e * 33 ^ charCodeAt(i)`, in that order. The precedence makes it `(e * 33) ^ code`. The result is returned signed and the callers wrap it in `Math.abs`. A name that overflows into the negative half hashes to `Math.abs` of a negative number. A reimplementation with `>>> 0` produces a different suffix for the same name and reads a directory the product never wrote.

**Memory**

- `<home>/memory` is the machine-wide memory directory. `<home>/projects/<slug>/memory` is the per-project one (`join(t, "projects", Bfe(cwd), "memory")` in shape). It uses the same slug function on purpose, so the two directories cannot disagree about which project they are for.
- `QODER_MEMORY_ENTRY_PATTERN` is `/^\d{4}-\d{2}-\d{2}\.md$/u`, verbatim, the discriminator the product itself uses to tell an entry from an index. The reader keeps only the files whose name matches it, sorts them by name in reverse order, and reads each date as `name.slice(0, -3)`. A memory file is named for the day it was written, and the most recent days come first. A reader that took every `.md` file sweeps up `MEMORY.md` itself, which is the index rather than an entry.
- `QODER_MEMORY_INDEX_NAME` is `MEMORY.md` (`const Pfe = "MEMORY.md"`), and the name test compares with `.toLowerCase()` on both sides, so the check is case-insensitive. The index lives inside the memory directory, not beside it. This is one place where the QoderWork migration's layout looks alike and is not the same. That source reader splits a `MEMORY.md` at the root of its own source directory on `§`. Its dated entries come from a `memory/` subdirectory beside it. Qoder's `<configDir>/memory/` is the destination that reader writes into. It holds both the dated entries and the index, which the writer rebuilds from the entries.
- The reader reads both halves and they are not the same kind of thing. An entry matches the product's own discriminator, which its memory reader applies before it reads a file. `MEMORY.md` is carried under its own name rather than folded in with the entries. An import of twenty entries and the index as twenty-one asserts something about the index that the product's own reader does not.
- The index is a list in the product's own `- [title](file.md)` form.
- A file in the memory directory that matches neither shape gets a `skipped` line in the report.
- The project-scoped `projects/<slug>/memory` directory is not read by this reader, because the function that reads memory has no `cwd`. `planQoder` names the directory it did not read.

**Skills**

- The two roots are `join(configDir, "skills")` and `join(home, ".agents", "skills")`, the latter from `e.userAgentSkillsPath ?? join(homedir(), ".agents", "skills")` in the constructor `new fZt({...})`. The second is the shared agent home this repository already migrates as its `agents` source. The source labels `"user-managed"` and `"user-agents"` are the same strings `sortSkills` and the inventory rows compare against. A folder that carries `"user-managed"` is one this build reads first.
- A skill is a directory that holds a `SKILL.md`, which is exactly the shape `readSkillDirs` produces. The product's own walker treats a directory as a skill only when `join(dir, "SKILL.md")` is a file. Otherwise it recurses into its subdirectories, to a depth of 8 and a budget of 4096 skills. The reader that resolves one builds `join(qoderConfigPath, "skills", folderName, "SKILL.md")`. The frontmatter carries `name`, `description` and `descriptionZh`, which is the metadata the product reads back.
- The same folder name in both roots is one skill to Qoder and two files to a naive reader. An import of both writes one over the other while the two report lines say the keys came across. That is the reason `qoder-read.ts` de-duplicates.
- The reader takes the roots in `qoderSkillsDirs` order, the first root wins a name, and each collision is recorded rather than dropped. A user with the same skill in two places is owed the sentence. A report that claims one skill where two files were found reads as an importer bug.
- A directory in a skill root with no `SKILL.md` gets a `skipped` line. Qoder defines a skill as a directory with one, and silence there reads as a miss.

**The store this source does not open**

- `QODER_DESKTOP_DATA_DIR` is `com.qoder.app.stable`, from `resources/product.json` (`"productId": "qoder"`, `"dataDirectoryName": "com.qoder.app.stable"`, `"channel": "stable"`). The app joins that value onto the platform's app-data path before it opens `main.sqlite`. That is why the path helper takes the name as an argument. A different channel of the same product ships a different name, and the manifest is where that is settled. The CN build is not a second literal here. The bundle carries the pair `{qoder: ".qoder", "qoder-cn": ".qoder-cn"}` only for the app-status file. No second data-directory name is readable from the shipped package, because the product catalogue it comes from is not shipped. So a CN install's store is not named rather than named wrongly.
- Nothing in this importer opens the store: not this module, not `qoder-read.ts`, not any plan, and not any test. The reason is not that it is hard. The store is a live database the desktop writes while the user works (it opens with `PRAGMA journal_mode = WAL`), and two of its tables hold credentials. `existsSync` is the whole of what is asked of it, and the name is what a report may print. A path is not user content, but a report is something a user may paste into an issue. Nothing past the return value of that function reaches one.
- One caveat stops a false negative: the store can also land in a temporary directory. The app-data resolution has a `catch` that falls back to `join(tmpdir(), \`${name}-startup-\`)` when the requested path cannot be created. So "the store was not there" means "not at the app-data path", not "there is none".
- `QODER_CREDENTIAL_TABLES` names four tables that are credential-shaped by name: `byok_model_credentials`, `mcp_oauth_credentials`, `app_settings` and `account_profiles`. A report may be pasted into an issue, so the sentence that names them quotes the names and nothing else. `QODER_DERIVED_TABLES` names three derived stores: `chat_session_search_segments` and its FTS shadow, a search index over the message rows, and the turn-payload buffer, a worker queue. None is opened.
- `qoderAgentsMdPath` names `<configDir>/AGENTS.md`, and the difference between an attested name and a chosen location matters here. The name is attested: the product builds its candidate list as `[...new Set(["AGENTS.override.md", "AGENTS.md", ...fallbackFilenames])]`, so `AGENTS.md` is in the list whatever `contextFileName` is set to. That array, the three `user`/`project`/`local` scopes and the `projectDocMaxBytes` size policy all come from `dist/_worker/qoder-worker-runtime.obf.mjs` in the installed package. Nothing in that same walk pins the document to the config root. It iterates directories upward from the project boundary, with `scope:"user"` and `scope:"project"` entries at each level, and gates the whole walk on `isTrustedFolder() && (!scopeFilter || scopeFilter.includes("project"))`. The scopes are the same `user`/`project`/`local` trio the settings merge uses. So the copy that reaches most sessions is the one beside the code. This helper names the config root, the shape the other ten importers in this repository use. That is the safe half of the ambiguity. A config root with no `AGENTS.md` returns a path that is not there, and `RawQoder.agentsMd` comes back `null`. The importer reports nothing at all. Absent is silent by contract: the ten other importers made the same choice for the same file, and a test pins it. The string `AGENTS.md` appears zero times in the plain SDK bundle `dist/index.js`, which makes it a reliable source of the opposite conclusion. A self-consistent "Qoder has no `AGENTS.md`" comes out of that bundle, and the shipped worker contradicts it.

**Hooks**

- `QODER_HOOK_EVENTS` lists 18 events in the SDK's order. Seven of the 18 are events this build also runs, so those import. The other 11 are events only Qoder has, and each one is a hook that never fires here. The list turns that silent loss into a report line. `normalizeClaudeHooks` collects an unknown event into `droppedEvents`, and this list is what that collection is checked against.
- `Notification` is not in it, and this build runs that event while Qoder does not have it. The intersection is not the whole story in either direction. A source whose events were assumed to be a subset of this build's is wrong in exactly that way.

**What the planner claims, and what it says in place of a claim**

- `planQoder` claims one scalar and one hook block. There is no `claimEnv` and no `claimScalar`, and both absences are deliberate rather than oversights. The one variable Qoder stores a value under is an MCP server's `env`. The instruction on this source is that those values do not come across. There is no theme or model to claim at all, which the header explains.
- `planQoderAbsentScalars` is the most important line this file writes. It is the sentence a user who ran `/model` in Qoder is owed. It is a fact with a citation rather than an apology. The other two scalars are absent rather than unhandled.
- The line lists every settings file that was read. It says that no model and no theme were taken from any of them. Neither is in any of them, because the desktop reads its settings field by field. The model picker and the appearance setting live in the application's own state rather than in `settings.json`, so the line ends `Set them with /model and /theme`. Without the line, a report that lists four settings reads as if four settings were quietly dropped.
- The importer claims the permission key only when it is present, and that is the whole argument. The SDK's settings writer sets `general.defaultPermissionMode`. The key appears twice in the SDK bundle, 35 bytes apart inside one expression. The expression is `o !== undefined && s.defaultPermissionMode === undefined && (s.defaultPermissionMode = o)`, at byte 12207 and byte 12242. So they are one site, a read followed by a write of the same key. No third site appears, and none appears in the desktop bundle.
- The one site is the outbound direction, this build's `t.defaultMode` translated by `toQoderDefaultPermissionMode`. That proves the SDK can write the key. It says nothing about where Qoder reads it, because no read site is attested anywhere we can see. So the key is real, a user who installed the CLI side can have one, and the importer claims it when the file states one. The report says that a desktop-only install will not have it. The other option is to skip the key because the desktop does not read it. That option leaves a CLI user's stated posture behind.
- An absent key means nothing claimed and nothing said, the same call `planT3RuntimeMode` makes for an absent `defaultRuntimeMode`. An import of the schema's default writes `agent` plus `danger-full-access` for a user whose file said nothing, so the absence stays an absence. A value that is not a string gets a skip line. It is not one of Qoder's permission modes, and the session keeps the mode it starts in.
- Three things are deliberately not migrated, and each is a decision with a reason. MCP `env` values and `headers` can hold a bearer token, so both come across by name only. BYOK provider credentials need no decision. Qoder seals the key before it stores it, so there is nothing in `settings.json` to migrate and nothing to re-auth. The importer counts the sessions and never opens them.

**The permission vocabulary, from the SDK's own switch**

- The vocabulary in `QODER_PERMISSION_MODES` is the SDK's own, and the file quotes the switch that proves it. `toQoderDefaultPermissionMode` translates this build's mode names into the strings the SDK passes to Qoder's CLI. Its cases are `acceptEdits` → `accept_edits`, `bypassPermissions` and `yolo` → `bypass_permissions`, and `dontAsk` → `dont_ask`, with the snake_case spellings passed through unchanged. The right-hand column is the whole of Qoder's vocabulary: `default`, `accept_edits`, `bypass_permissions`, `dont_ask`, `plan` and `auto`. The table maps two of the six names and gives the other four an explicit `undefined`.
- `accept_edits` maps to `ask`, which is narrower than the name suggests. This build has no mode that applies edits without a prompt, so every write prompts like everything else. A migration must not make a permission setting wider on its own judgment. The report line says exactly that. It does not call the change a rename.
- `bypass_permissions` maps to `agent` plus `danger-full-access`, and the importer claims both of them together. The one value does two jobs over there: never ask, and no confinement. A claim of the mode half alone leaves a session that auto-approves everything inside a sandbox nobody chose, which is a combination no user picked.
- `default` maps to nothing, because it is the schema's absence rather than a choice. It is the value a file carries when nobody chose anything. An import of it writes a posture on the user's behalf that they never stated. `t3-plan.ts` argues at length against the same fail-open move. The absence leaves the session at this build's own default.
- `auto` maps to nothing, because it is a classifier that approves the calls it judges routine, and no mode here does that. `ask` puts a person in the loop rather than a classifier, and `agent` does not judge at all. Either choice changes the posture the name describes, so the session keeps what it otherwise starts in.
- `dont_ask` maps to nothing, because it is the far end of Qoder's range. The nearest mode here is `agent` with no sandbox, and that is not the same decision. What `dont_ask` declines to ask about is not written down anywhere in either bundle. A guess here makes a permission setting wider rather than narrower. The importer claims nothing, and the report says to set the posture with `/mode`.
- `plan` maps to nothing, because it names a posture for plans rather than a permission. It is about what the model may propose, not what it may do, so no permission mode here corresponds to it. A claim sets a sandbox the name never described.
- Any other string gets the fallback line. It is not a permission mode Qoder defines, so it never decided a session, and the permission mode stays as it is.
- The table is exported for the row count rather than for the values. A row added here without a line in the mapper test's table is an import whose claim nobody checked.

**The keys this planner accounts for**

- `QODER_SETTINGS_HANDLED` is the whole list of top-level keys the mapper answers for, read against the reader's header. The six are `mcpServers`, `hooks`, `general`, `enabledPlugins`, `pluginConfigs` and `chatSession`. `mcpServers` and `hooks` are the two the planner actually reads. `general` is in the list because `planQoderPermissionMode` handles exactly one key under it and reports the rest. The last three get their own line from `planQoderPlugins` or from `planQoderAbsentScalars`, so they are not the unhandled-key catch-all's business.
- `QODER_GENERAL_HANDLED` holds the one `general` key: `defaultPermissionMode`.
- `QODER_MCP_KEYS` is the live key set, and two sets exist in the bundle that are not the same set. The live one is the set the settings editor checks an edited entry against before it saves. Its 13 names are `displayName`, `command`, `args`, `cwd`, `env`, `environment`, `url`, `qoder_url`, `type`, `authType`, `legacySseFallback`, `headers` and `timeout`. The other is a 12-name array that includes `disabled` and excludes `displayName` and `qoder_url`. It belongs to the QoderWork importer, which reads a foreign product's file and did not write this one. A wrong set produces a wrong report.
- The difference between the two sets is why `disabled` gets its own sentence in the plan. The product writes that key (`setEntryDisabled` sets and deletes it). The live normalizer reads it, and the editor's own key set does not list it. So it is not treated as an ordinary key.
- The report label from `qoderFrom` is the file a value came from. It adds the layer when that file is not the user's own, with the key and the subkey. It reads `… → mcpServers.demo (project layer)` rather than `… → mcpServers (project layer).demo`. A project or local layer supplies any of the six shallow-merge keys in whole. An unqualified label sends the user to a file that does not hold the value. The `user` layer prints no suffix, because that is where these labels always pointed. A line that says `the user layer` tells a reader nothing.
- `qoderSettingsSubkeyOrigin` narrows the file when a subkey is given. It exists because the SDK's provenance is keyed by top-level name alone, and `mcpServers` is carried by every layer that has a server. Without the extra check, a server the user added takes a project file's label.

**The MCP plan, entry by entry**

- The transport rules come from Qoder's live normalizer, and they are not the ones a reader guesses. `hasCommand` is `typeof e.command === "string"` and `hasUrl` is `typeof e.url === "string"`. An entry with neither fails as `MCP_CONFIG_TRANSPORT_REQUIRED`, and an entry with both fails as `MCP_CONFIG_TRANSPORT_AMBIGUOUS`. So an empty `command` string still means a stdio server to Qoder. A pair of `command` and `url` is an error rather than a win for `command`, and each outcome is its own line in the plan.
- A third transport is a `qoder_url` that points at Qoder's own managed gateway. Qoder accepts one only when the URL passes a region and trusted-domain check against an environment this build does not have. There is no address to connect to, so the connector is named and never opened.
- `enabled` is a key Qoder's own normalizer throws on (`MCP_CONFIG_ENABLED_UNSUPPORTED`). A file that carries one is a file Qoder refuses to save. The entry is not a server this build can run either, and the reason worth a line is the product's. Qoder keeps whether a server runs in `disabled` instead.
- A `disabled` that is not a boolean gets a line that says a boolean is the one type Qoder accepts there (`MCP_CONFIG_DISABLED_INVALID`). A `disabled: true` entry gets a line that says Qoder keeps this server off, so the import leaves out a server the user turned off. The line points at `/mcp` to turn it on here.
- The importer reads `env` before `environment`, because Qoder's normalizer does (`const a = t.env ?? t.environment ?? {}`) and deletes both afterwards. A file that carries both gets a sentence that the names in the report are the `env` ones.
- The importer drops and counts `args` entries that are not strings, and a non-array `args` contributes nothing. The first 256 arguments come across, which is the product's own limit. The counts appear in the detail line.
- `cwd` is copied only when it is absolute. Qoder needs an absolute `cwd` in the user scope (`MCP_CONFIG_CWD_ABSOLUTE_REQUIRED`) and refuses one that escapes the project root in the project scope. A relative path in a hand-edited file is one Qoder never validated. It names a different directory on each machine, so it is named and not written.
- A URL server reads `type`. `sse` is Qoder's spelling for the transport this build's one HTTP client also speaks, so it connects the same way. The accepted set is `http`, `streamableHttp`, `streamable-http` and `sse`. A value outside that set is not one Qoder's normalizer accepts, so the reader reads the entry as a plain HTTP server.
- `timeout` is in milliseconds and is bounded at both ends. Qoder drops a value below 1000 ms without a report and caps at 2147483647 ms, which is 2^31-1. This build's server config has no timeout field at all, so there is nowhere for the value to go and the report names it.
- The importer drops `headers` whole. A header value is an ordinary place for a bearer token. `headers: {"x-api-key": …}` is a shape Qoder accepts and a shape no name-based credential scan here flags. The loss is the server's authentication, and the report says so. That is the right trade. A server that asks for its credentials again is a nuisance. A token written into a file the user later shares is not.
- `env` values are not copied and the names are. The names come across in the report so the user can fill them in. A name that reads as a credential was already gone before this function runs. `readQoder` deletes every `looksLikeSecretName` match and every `QODER_SECRET_KEY` match at every depth, and records one `skipped` line per deletion with the exact path. So this function counts the names without a second check.
- An earlier draft filtered the names again and appended a sentence about credential-shaped ones. That branch was unreachable. The reader already reports such a name by full path, so the extra sentence was a second claim about the same thing.
- The URL is the one credential channel no name-based scan can see. The token sits inside the one string every importer treats as a safe identifier. `headers` and `env` lose the credential and keep the server. A URL has no such half: a URL with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` removed points at nothing. A copy of that shape trades a credential on disk for a dead server. The report calls the copy a clean `map` at the same time.
- `containsSecret` is `true` on that line although the importer wrote nothing. The flag means `whether the migrated value is a credential`, and the value that line is about is one. Nothing reads the flag today, and a future reader who filters items for `was anything here a secret?` does not have to re-derive the answer from the prose.
- Keys outside `QODER_MCP_KEYS` are named in a downgrade line. They are not among the 13 keys Qoder's own settings editor recognizes on an MCP entry.
- A server name the target already defines is kept, with a line that points at `--force`. The final line reports `~/.labunbun/.mcp.json → mcpServers.<name>` with `copied without its credentials`. The action is `downgrade` when any downgrade above applies, and `map` when none does. The `secret` flag on a written server is computed rather than assumed. A future change that does start to copy a value has to make the flag true on purpose.
- A config that fails `McpServerConfigSchema.safeParse` is skipped. A stdio entry fails when its `command`, `args` or `cwd` are not a definition this build accepts. A URL entry fails when its URL is not an address this build's MCP client accepts.

**The hook block, through the shared normalizer**

- The shape is Qoder's own and `hGr` validates it. Each key of `hooks` is an event name taken verbatim, each event's value is an array of groups, and each group is `{ matcher?, hooks: [...] }`. The record the reader produces per handler carries `id`, `event`, `matcher`, `condition`, `hookType`, `summary`, `async`, `asyncRewake`, `once`, `groupIndex` and `hookIndex`.
- There is no timeout in that record. The unit is not unknown, the field does not exist, so there is nothing on disk for a timeout to come from. Handlers are written untimed and this build's default applies.
- `if`, `async`, `asyncRewake` and `once` have no equivalent here, and each loss has a direction. `if` is a condition Qoder tests before it runs a handler. This build's hook entry has no condition field, so a conditional handler runs every time instead of only when the condition held. That is why those are skipped rather than widened.
- `once` means run this one time, and a dropped flag makes the hook run on every event, so those are skipped too. `async` runs the hook asynchronously, and a dropped flag makes the hook synchronous. That is the stricter direction and the one safe default. `asyncRewake` has no analogue at all.
- A handler that is `http` rather than `command` has no analogue here, because this build runs shell commands and nothing else. `normalizeClaudeHandler` drops it and counts it, and the count reaches the report through the same path as the rest.
- `QoderHookCount` walks the same three levels `hGr` does, event then group then handler. It treats anything outside the shape as no match, which is the tolerance `normalizeClaudeHooks` applies. It exists so the report can say `3 handlers were left off` from the file rather than from a guess about how many were there.
- The event list is `QODER_HOOK_EVENTS`, 18 names with seven this build runs. The rest go to `droppedEvents`, which `normalizeClaudeHooks` collects so an event nobody here has a word for becomes a sentence rather than a silence. A skip line names those events, says the hooks under them never fire, and repeats the total.
- The plan lists the rest of the losses: handlers that were not shell commands, malformed entries, and timeouts the shared normalizer clamped.
- A matcher leaves with a downgrade line. This build escapes a matcher's pattern characters, so a carried matcher matches a literal rather than the tool it names.
- When no event produces a runnable hook, the skip line counts the events Qoder declares and names the ones this build does not have. On success the claim reads `N event(s) imported` with the downgrade list, or `imported verbatim` when every handler is a shell command. The event count comes from the normalized config.

**Skills, memory and the instruction document**

- A skill is copied verbatim because the two shapes are the same: a directory with a `SKILL.md` that carries `name` and `description`. There is no rewrite to explain.
- A skill name in both skill roots gets a skip line, from the collision record `readQoder` keeps. Qoder reads the first root, so the second file was not read.
- The two memory documents are told apart by name. `MEMORY.md` is the index and a dated file is an entry. Both become rule files with distinct names, because a single file asserts that an index is an entry. The index becomes `imported-qoder-MEMORY.md` and each entry becomes `imported-qoder-<stem>.md`. The distinct names matter when a user has only one kind. Otherwise both want `memory.md` as their target path, which is the case `planAntigravityAssets` guards against.
- When more than one memory document comes across, a downgrade line says so. Its `from` is the memory directory rather than `settings.json`, because memory is a directory beside the settings document and not a settings field. A line that points at the settings file sends the user somewhere with none in it. The detail says Qoder's memory came across as one rule file per document, its index plus each dated entry. This build has no memory directory and no index format to keep, so what pointed at what is now the file names.
- The `AGENTS.md` document becomes a rule file rather than a memory entry. The detail line of `planMemoryAsRule` states the reason. This build merges rule files into the memory it has. It does not replace that memory. A document the agent re-reads at the top of every session wants exactly that. The name is `imported-qoder-agents.md`, not one of the `imported-qoder-<basename>.md` names. So an `AGENTS.md` cannot collide with an `agents.md` memory entry, and a user with both keeps two files.
- No report line appears when the instruction document is absent. That is the contract the ten other importers share: a `null` is not a failure and is not worth a row. A test pins the rule in both directions.

**What the plan reports and leaves behind**

- The session count is the point of `planQoderLeftovers`. A line that says `7 sessions left behind` is worth a row even though the migration does not move them. It is the difference between a user who expected history to come across and is told, and a user who never had any.
- The session line reads `<configDir>/projects` with the transcript count and the project count. The detail says the layout is Qoder's own, one directory for each directory that was used, and one `<sessionId>.jsonl` per session. The contents are not established. The writer is the native `qoder-runtime-host` binary, which is in none of the three JavaScript bundles Qoder ships. A copy on a guessed record shape produces histories that look right and are not.
- The project's own MCP file gets a line whether or not it is there, and it is not read either way. It is a separate location from `settings.json`, so the three-layer merge does not reach it, and nothing in either bundle says how the two combine. A read plus a merge is a guess with a report attached, so the line says to copy the servers by hand.
- The other Qoder build's configuration home gets a skip line. Qoder resolves one home per process and picks the build at start-up. A machine with both `.qoder` and `.qoder-cn` has two installs rather than two halves of one. Anything only that tree holds stays there.
- A refused `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME` gets a skip line from `QODER_CONFIG_DIR_NAME`, with the reason the reader recorded.
- The desktop store line prints the path when the run supplied one, and `%APPDATA% (not supplied to this run)` when it did not. Existence is the only thing asked of it and the name is what a report may print. The store holds encrypted BYOK and OAuth credentials beside the conversation rows. The app opens it in WAL mode and writes to it while the user works. Two of its tables hold the credentials, and the conversation rows are a separate piece of work from the ones in `~/.qoder`.
- The report names unhandled keys per layer rather than over the merged document. The merged document's top-level keys are the union of the three files. One line over it names every file's unhandled keys against whichever file the user reads first. A key a project layer carried that the merge then dropped gets a report line as if it were live. One line per file also says which file holds the thing the importer does not map. The `general` sub-object gets its own pass per layer.
- The reader's `skipped` records become skip lines at the end, by name and reason.
- `SOURCE` is the literal `"qoder"`, spelled once with no cast. The union and every table keyed by it live in `migrate-types.ts`, and the literal buys one spelling rather than eleven. The call lives in the `qoder` arm of `migrate.ts`, which is where the planner is reached from.

### Cursor

**Two kinds of citation, and every claim says which kind it is.** A `DOCUMENTATION-LEVEL` claim is second-hand and carries a date. A reader can check it against Cursor's published pages only. A `SOURCE-LEVEL` claim comes from the minified bundle the CLI ships, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`. It is cited as module path, function name and build id, never as a line number. A line number in a minified file is invented precision about a file no reader can open.

**The caveat used to say this source was documentation-level throughout.** That stopped when the CLI bundle was unpacked, and the split stays visible. A reader who decides what to trust must not guess which standard a sentence was written to. Every source-level claim in this module is about shape or path arithmetic. That is the part the documentation does not cover, and it was wrong before it was checked. Every documentation-level claim is about a file Cursor documents, where the document and the file agree.

**Six facts about Cursor's shape matter most, and none of them is obvious.**

1. The rules are `.mdc` and the frontmatter has three keys: `description`, `globs` and `alwaysApply`. `globs` is a comma-delimited string rather than a YAML list. There is no `ruleType`. The four rule behaviours come out of combinations of the three keys, and a `ruleType` seen in the wild came from a generator. The frontmatter travels verbatim, and `cursor-plan.ts` says how. What does not survive is what starts a rule, and that is the downgrade. `DOCUMENTATION-LEVEL`.
2. A plain `.md` in a rules directory is ignored by Cursor. The official page says so in as many words and gives the reason: the file has no frontmatter. Those files are named rather than imported. They are a mistake, or a convention for another tool. Either way an import adds prose to the user's context that Cursor itself never showed them. `DOCUMENTATION-LEVEL`.
3. There are three permission systems and they do not overlap. The CLI's list is `permissions` in `cli-config.json` or `cli.json`. The IDE's list is `permissions.json`, which the CLI does not read. The in-app command allowlist overrides the IDE's list. This source reads the first one only, and the report says which one it was. `SOURCE-LEVEL` for the CLI's list, and for the IDE's file, which the CLI does not read. `DOCUMENTATION-LEVEL` for the third, which is an in-app feature with no file behind it.
4. Three of the trees Cursor reads are not its own. A copy of them files every one of the user's skills under the wrong tool. See `CURSOR_VENDOR_TREES`. The exclusion is per asset kind, because the three kinds do not read the same directories. A flat list misses a tree Cursor really reads or claims one it does not. `SOURCE-LEVEL`.
5. The reader reads the three asset kinds differently, and the differences are the whole of their treatment. Commands are user and project, one level, `*.md` only. Agents are project only. Skills are user and project, recursive to a depth limit. `SOURCE-LEVEL`.
6. A command has no frontmatter and its arguments are substituted, not appended. Both are the opposite of what the shared `commandAsSkill` path says, which is why this source writes its own. `SOURCE-LEVEL`. See `CURSOR_COMMAND_ARGUMENTS` for the substitution rule and `cursorCommandDescription` for the title extraction.

**What is deliberately not read** is named rather than passed over. Those things are the chat bodies, the MCP OAuth tokens under the CLI's data root, and the two decision lists beside them. The chat bodies live in the editor's SQLite `state.vscdb` and are keyed by a workspace hash this importer cannot recompute. The tokens are `existsSync` and nothing else. `readCursorStateDatabases` and `readCursorProjectData` only count and name, and each says why in place.

**Documents**

- `CursorDocument` has three states: absent, unreadable and document. The report prints a different sentence for absent and unreadable, and only one of the two needs an explanation. A file the user wrote that does not parse is a fact they need. A fold into "nothing there" is how a broken edit goes unnoticed for months.
- `readCursorDocument` reads one JSON object or says which of the other two things the path is. A parse result that is not a record counts as unreadable.

**Rules**

- `readCursorRules` walks the `.mdc` files under a rules directory recursively and names the `.md` files beside them. Recursion is the documented shape and not quite the documented claim. The official rules page shows a nested layout as its own example (`frontend/components.mdc`) and never says whether discovery stops at the top level. A recursive read is right if Cursor recurses. It is harmless if Cursor does not, because a nested file Cursor ignores is a file a user wrote and expects to come across.
- The two directories are walked apart because they are different things. `<project>/.cursor/rules` is Cursor's own and is loaded. `~/.cursor/rules` is a community convention that Cursor staff say is not supported. User Rules are a settings-UI feature with no directory of their own. Those files still import, because the user wrote them. Each one carries a report line that Cursor itself never loaded it.
- `CursorRule.name` is the file name with `.mdc` removed, so two subdirectories collide by name. The content is the file byte for byte, frontmatter included. `scope` is `project` or `user`. `notLoaded` is set exactly when Cursor does not load the file.
- The walk sorts each directory's entries by name, so two runs over one tree read the files in the same order.
- `CURSOR_PLAIN_MARKDOWN_IGNORED` is the sentence a `.md` file earns, and `CURSOR_USER_RULES_NOT_LOADED` is the sentence a user rules file earns.
- The entries are annotated `Dirent[]` rather than with the type `readdirSync` returns, which resolves to the `Buffer` overload. Every name then reads as a buffer. That is a false statement about the filesystem, and TypeScript propagates it into every use of `.name`.

**Frontmatter**

- `readCursorFrontmatter` parses frontmatter the way Cursor parses it: line by line, not as YAML. The rule is verbatim from the bundle (`Hs`, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`). Each line is trimmed. An empty line, or one that starts with `#`, is passed over. The first colon splits. The key is lowercased. The value after the colon is trimmed. Nothing is nested.
- This is the same parser `subagents.ts` `parseAgentDefinitions` uses on the target side. That is why an agent file travels byte for byte rather than rewritten. Every key Cursor honours that this build also honours is already in the same spelling.
- Cursor refuses a header with nothing after it (`if (0 === n.length) return null`), because the body is the agent's prompt and an empty prompt is not an agent. This reader returns `null` for the same case, and the file is named in the report.

**Commands**

- `readCursorCommandDir` reads the `*.md` files directly in a `commands/` directory and nothing else, from `loadCommandsFromDirectory` (build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`). The walk is not recursive, so a command in a subdirectory is invisible to Cursor. It is named rather than invented.
- Cursor itself drops a file whose name minus `.md` is blank (`if (!o.trim()) return null`), and this reader records the same skip.
- Non-`.md` files are passed over without a line, and that is the ordinary convention rather than a Cursor quirk. Every command tree in this repository filters on the extension, and a `notes.txt` beside a command needs no explanation. A directory is different. The extension of a directory is a filename, and Cursor's filter accepts it.
- The description Cursor derives is a source of surprise. `extractTitle` applies to the first line only. It falls back to that line verbatim when the line is not a markdown title. A command that opens with a frontmatter block is therefore described to the model as the three characters `---`. `cursorCommandDescription` does not reproduce that. It looks for the first markdown title anywhere in the file, which is the text the author meant. Then it takes the first line that is not a fence or blank. The final fallback is `the cursor command /<id>`.
- `CURSOR_COMMAND_ARGUMENTS` states the substitution rule. `$ARGUMENTS` is substituted, and so are `$1` through `$99` rather than `$1` through `$9`. The guard is `(?<!\w)`, so `$1` inside `$12` does not match on its own. An argument with no placeholder becomes the empty string rather than the literal `$7`. The arguments are appended only when nothing was substituted, and there they land after a blank line. With a placeholder, the substituted text replaces them. So "appends its arguments" and "substitutes `$ARGUMENTS`" are two different commands, and the file says which. A skill body is never substituted, so both behaviours are things this build does not have.

**Agents**

- `CURSOR_AGENT_EXTENSIONS` is `[".md", ".mdc", ".markdown"]`, lowercased first, so `AGENT.MD` counts. A reader that took only `.md` skips a file Cursor loads. An `.mdc` in an agents directory is not strange. `.mdc` is the extension Cursor's rules use, so a copy of a rule-shaped file produces one by accident.
- `CURSOR_AGENT_MAX_DEPTH` is 10, and this is the one place the importer's answer is not the source's. The source has no cap, because the ripwalk it hands to runs until the filesystem runs out. The cap exists for two reasons. A walk with no bound is a walk a user can make slow. Every other walk in this repository has one. The value is `CURSOR_SKILL_MAX_DEPTH`'s on purpose. Two walks in one product that both stop at ten is a coincidence. A match lets the report say "ten" once per kind. What the walk gives up on is named, so a tree deeper than the cap is a report sentence and not a silent omission.
- `readCursorAgentDir` reads one `agents/` directory, and it is only ever the project's. `computeAgentsDirs()` returns `[join(resolve(workspacePath), ".cursor", "agents")]` and pushes `.claude` and `.grok` only when third-party extensibility is on. Every one of those joins is off `resolve(this.workspacePath)` and `homedir()` appears nowhere in it. So there is no user-level agent directory. A `~/.cursor/agents` a user made by hand is not read here, and this reader does not look for one.
- The agent walk is recursive, unlike a commands directory's, and the source has no depth check of its own. A flat reader skips a nested agent Cursor loads. The depth counts from the directory itself, so a file directly inside it is depth 1. That is how the source counts too (`s` is 0 for the root it is handed and the guard is `if (s > 10) return`).
- The file travels verbatim, which is possible because Cursor's parser and the target's are the same shape: line-based `key: value`, lowercased keys, no YAML. The keys Cursor honours that the target does not read are named in the detail text rather than dropped.
- The report names this divergence from Cursor. The loader does `name: n.name || Vs(basename)`, where `Vs` is `basename(e, extname(e)).replace(/[\s_]+/g, "-")`. So Cursor shows the frontmatter's name when there is one, and otherwise a slugified basename, which is not the filename either. Two files called `code reviewer.md` and `code-reviewer.md` are the same agent to Cursor and two to this run. The target reads the filename, so the directory is named from it. The difference is the user's to see.

**Skills**

- `CURSOR_SKILL_MAX_DEPTH` is 10. `findSkillMarkdownFiles` recurses under `if (s > 10) return` and keeps only files named exactly `SKILL.md`. It resolves each directory before it descends on it, and the resolved path must stay inside the root in the project scope. That last check is not reproduced, because the walk never leaves the root on its own. `readdirSync` reports a symlink as a link rather than a directory, so a linked directory falls out of the walk. It is not descended into. The difference is only in which links get followed, and to follow none is the conservative side. A link is the one way a scan of a workspace tree reaches a directory the user keeps somewhere else.
- The name is the directory that holds `SKILL.md`, not the top-level one. That is a different rule from every other reader in this repository. `getSkillIdForPath` takes `basename(dirname(skillMdPath))`. Only when two directories share that name does the path come in, its segments joined with `-`. A nested `frontend/deploy/SKILL.md` is the skill `deploy`. The wrong first case names every nested skill after its top-level directory and merges unrelated ones.
- The third line of that rule is deliberately not reproduced. Its `taken` set spans every root of one load. This set spans one `skills` directory, and within one directory the rule above already yields distinct names. The bare name is used only when it is unique, and the relative path is unique by construction. So the loop cannot fire. A mechanism that can only be a no-op reads as if it carries weight. The case the source's suffix exists for is one id claimed twice. Both the user half and the project half claim it, and the home is also its own project. `collectFileWrites` catches that one layer up. It reports the collision and keeps the first. It does not start a second directory.
- The names are settled after the whole walk, not as each file is found. Whether a name is relative or bare depends on the set. `dupNames` comes first, then the ids.
- A directory more than 10 levels below the skills directory is a skip with its own sentence. Cursor stops its walk there too, so anything under it is not a skill to it.
- The rest of the directory travels with the file even though Cursor loads only `SKILL.md`. Those are different questions: what the model is told at load time, and what the skill may use when it runs. A skill whose body says to run `scripts/check.sh` needs that script, and Cursor keeps the script next to the `SKILL.md` it read. `readAttachments` is the shared walk, with the same size cap, the same binary and VCS exclusions and the same skip reasons in the report.

**Vendor trees**

- `CURSOR_VENDOR_TREES` is per asset kind, because the three kinds do not read the same trees. A flat list is wrong in both directions. A `.codex` entry for commands claims Cursor reads `~/.codex/commands`, which it does not. A stop at `.claude` for skills imports a second copy of every Claude skill the user has.
- The skill roots come from `$n`, which pairs a config directory with a subdirectory and a third-party mark, and `Wn(enabled)` is `$n.filter(t => !t.builtin && (enabled || !t.thirdParty))`. The config directories are `.cursor` and the four vendors `.claude`, `.codex`, `.grok` and `.agents`, at both the user and the workspace level.
- `.agents` is marked `thirdParty: false` in that table, unlike the other three. So Cursor treats `~/.agents/skills` as a first-class skill root. It is excluded here for a different reason and on a different warrant. The reason is not "Cursor marks it another tool's". The reason is "this repository's `agents` source imports it, and a second import files the same skill under two names". The exclusion is the same and the reason printed is not. A report with Cursor's reason asserts something the source denies.
- The agent trees are `.claude` and `.grok`, only when third-party extensibility is on, and off the resolved workspace path alone. There is no `homedir()` anywhere in that computation, so the two agent trees have no user half at all. That is why `scopes` is the one place the table says where each tree can be.
- Commands load from `.claude` at both levels, ungated, because the extensibility flag does not apply to commands.
- Each entry's `owner` is a `MIGRATION_SOURCE_IDS` member that really does read that directory for that kind. That was checked per entry rather than assumed from the name, because the same vendor id is not good for all three kinds.
- The flag that decides half of this is not modelled, and it does not need to be. `thirdPartyExtensibilityEnabled` defaults to true (`t?.thirdPartyExtensibilityEnabled ?? true`). No configuration key turns it off. The exclusion is right in both states. On, Cursor reads those trees, and a copy files a Claude skill under Cursor's name as well. So the user has two of every one and no way to tell which came from where. Off, the trees are none of Cursor's business, and the source that owns them brings them in its own run. So the answer is the same either way. A report sentence that branches on the flag branches on something with no second outcome.
- `readCursorAssets` takes the home and the cwd rather than the two `.cursor` roots, because of the vendor scan. The third-party trees are `~/.claude/skills` and `<workspace>/.claude/skills`, and their base is the home and the workspace, not the config root. On a machine that set `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`, the config root is somewhere else. A scan built on it finds the config and nothing else.
- The asset loader's own `userHomeDirectory` is the reason for that. It defaults to `homedir()` and is what `c$0`, `x7$` and `Dct` are handed for the skill roots, while `WI()` appears in none of them. So on a machine that exports `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` or `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`, the config files move and the three asset directories do not. `migrate.ts` passes `options.home ?? homedir()` here, which is the same value by construction. A read of the assets out of the config root is the same failure the config files had, one level down: silent emptiness.
- Each vendor tree is looked for only at the levels the source loads it. A `~/.claude/agents` is not a tree Cursor reads, so a line for it tells the user Cursor harvests a directory it does not touch. The `claude-code` source imports that directory in its own run regardless.
- The trees are named by their presence, not by a scan. That is the same call `opencode-read.ts` makes, and for the same reason. The point of the line is that the user learns another source owns the tree. A file count says nothing they can act on.

**MCP**

- `RawCursorMcpDocument` keeps the path, the `mcpServers` as Cursor wrote it, a `malformed` flag and an `unreadable` flag. `malformed` is set when the file is there and its `mcpServers` is not a table of servers. `unreadable` is set when the file is there and is not JSON at all. A file with no `mcpServers` key yields an empty map and no flag.
- Two documents are read, the global `~/.cursor/mcp.json` and the project's, and both may be present.

**The name lists, and the pillars of presence**

- `unaccountedNames` returns the names in a directory this importer does not read, sorted. A directory that is not there contributes none.
- `readCursorStateDatabases` is `existsSync` and nothing else for `state.vscdb`. The chat bodies are in it and this importer cannot carry them. The count is the part that earns its keep, because it is a fact about what is left behind. The global database and each `workspaceStorage` subdirectory that holds a `state.vscdb` are named.
- `readCursorProjectData` lists the CLI's per-workspace data tree rather than addresses it. The reason is that the slug is not computed, which the `cursor-home.ts` block comment states. `existsSync` is the only contact with any of the files. `mcp-auth.json` is a token store, and the other two are decision lists this importer cannot read. The workspace is the directory name reported as-is, and it is a slug of the workspace path. So it is already a name the reader cannot mistake for something they typed.
- The scan uses `Object.entries` rather than `Object.keys` and a lookup, because the lookup needed a non-null assertion. That assertion stood in for a lookup that cannot miss. The entries carry the value along.
- The files are sorted by workspace and then by name, so two runs over one tree print the same line. `readdirSync` order is filesystem order, which is not.
- `RawCursor.roots` carries the user root, the project root, the editor's user-data root and which of the config-root rules answered for the last one.
- `present` is wider than "does `~/.cursor` exist", for the same reason the `opencode` source's is. A Cursor install that a user opened and used leaves its state in the editor's user-data directory. A user who never touched the CLI's files still has a tree worth a name. It is narrower than "does any of them exist", because an empty directory has nothing to import.
- `present` gates on the sentences, not on the importable files alone. A document that is there and unreadable has a whole sentence written for it in the plan. A source that is not present prints none of them. The prompt list is the clearest case. It sits under the CLI's own home, three directories down, in a per-workspace subtree named by a digest of the cwd. So it is the one piece of evidence for a user who typed prompts and wrote no rule, no config and no server file. A gate that excludes it makes the report say "nothing migratable". That report sits on the very file the run exists to read.
- The asset directories, the vendor tree hits, the two name lists and the per-workspace tree are in the same disjunction. For the per-workspace tree the pillar does work the others cannot. Under the default data root, `projects` is an entry of the user directory. Once `CURSOR_DATA_DIR` points elsewhere, the tree is the only thing left of the CLI's own state. Without that disjunct, a user with nothing but MCP tokens behind it is told there is no Cursor here.
- The two name lists are in it for a related reason. A home whose only Cursor file is `permissions.json` is a home where the report has something to say. The sentence that explains it is the IDE's list and not the one in effect is the whole treatment. A gate on the importable files alone kept it out of the report.
- The three asset directory names join the accounted lists. Without them, one run prints both halves of a contradiction. One line says `commands` was imported. Another says `commands` is an entry this importer reads nothing out of. `rules` was in both lists before there were assets, and this is that shape.
- `projects` joins the accounted list for a different reason. It is never imported, but `readCursorProjectData` gives it a line of its own that says strictly more than the generic one. That line names the files in it and says the token store was not opened. A list entry prints "reads nothing out of projects" directly above the paragraph about the token in it. The first sentence is the one that reads as "there is nothing there".
- That entry is added at both roots, and only where it is the tree that reader walks. The first half is a fix. The list was extended for the user root and not for the project one. So a home whose project directory is also its home printed the contradiction anyway. A test written to catch it looked at the user list, which was clean, so it passed. The second half is why the entry is computed rather than written. With `CURSOR_DATA_DIR` at another path, a `projects` under either root is an unrelated directory. For that one, "this importer reads nothing out of it" is exactly true.
- The project data tree is kept apart from the state databases, because the two answer different questions. Those are the editor's SQLite files, and this is the CLI's tree, which hangs off `$CURSOR_DATA_DIR` rather than the editor's user-data directory. A `root` of `null` is the common case and is still worth a distinction from "there and empty".
- `promptHistory` carries the CLI's prompt list and which of the three config-root rules answered.

**Paths**

- Cursor's state sits in four directories, and they are easy to confuse. The CLI's own home is `~/.cursor/` unless one of two environment variables says otherwise, so it is not always under the home at all. The project half is `<project>/.cursor/`. The editor's user-data directory is VS Code derived, and it sits outside the home on two of the three platforms. The prompt list is `<cli home>/chats/<md5 of the resolved cwd>/`, one directory per workspace.
- The CLI home holds four files, and each one carries a different format. The global CLI config is `cli-config.json`, the global hooks are `hooks.json`, and the global MCP servers are `mcp.json`. The fourth is `permissions.json`, and it belongs to the IDE. Neither this importer nor `cursor-agent` reads it. All four paths are documented.
- The project half holds `rules/`, `mcp.json`, `hooks.json` and `cli.json`. The three asset kinds sit at `join(root, kind)`, for whichever half `root` is.
- The prompt list is the only Cursor path in this module that no official page documents.
- The names are deliberately asymmetric, and this module keeps them. The global CLI file is `cli-config.json` and the project one is `cli.json`. A symmetric guess reads a project file that does not exist and misses the one that does.
- Nothing in `cursor-home.ts` creates anything. Every function computes strings, and the two functions that answer "is it there" answer with `existsSync` and nothing more.

**User and project files**

- `CURSOR_USER_FILES` lists the three files under `~/.cursor` this importer reads. The first is `cli-config.json`, the CLI's global configuration: permissions, model, approval mode, sandbox and network. The other two are `hooks.json`, the global hooks, and `mcp.json`, the global MCP servers.
- `permissions.json` is left out on purpose. It is a third permission system and it belongs to the IDE. Its schema is different again, and the in-app command allowlist overrides it. Neither `cursor-agent` nor the file beside it reads it. A migration that reads it imports the one list the CLI never applied.
- `CURSOR_PROJECT_FILES` is the same shape for the project half. The project's `cli.json` is the CLI file, which Cursor documents as permissions-only. The project's `hooks.json` is version-controlled with the repository, and its `mcp.json` holds the project's MCP servers.

**Config root**

- `cursorConfigRoot` resolves the CLI home in three steps, in the order the source applies them: `$CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`, else `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/cursor`, else `~/.cursor`. This is source-level, checked against the shipped bundle: module `cursor-config/dist/paths.js`, function `WI()`, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`.
- Three details matter and the importer was wrong about all three before the check. The variable is `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR` and the middle one is `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, not the `~/.cursor` the importer used to hard-code. On a Linux desktop that exports `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` the config genuinely lives at `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/cursor`, and `~/.cursor` does not exist. Every read came back empty and the report said nothing at all: no file, no directory, no "not installed" either. The importer reported the source as absent while the evidence it wanted sat one directory away.
- The test is a truthy `value?.trim()`, so a whitespace-only value is not a value. `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR=" "` falls through to the next rule. It does not become a path under the current directory, which is what `join(" ", "cli-config.json")` produces. This is the same convention the other importers in this repo use.
- The override is returned verbatim, not trimmed. The source trims to test and returns the original. This module keeps that, so a value with a stray space at the end points where the source points.
- The default is `join(home, ".cursor")`, the same three files the official configuration page documents. The path is the documented one whenever neither variable is set. That is why the documentation-level claims about those files still hold once the root becomes conditional.
- `CursorConfigRootOrigin` carries which rule answered, and it is a union rather than a boolean. The first two rules can put the root outside the user's home. A reader who cannot tell "the default" from "you set `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`" has no way to check the importer's work. The two paths look the same on disk.
- `cursorUserRoot` is `cursorConfigRoot` without the origin. The wrapper is that way round because of scope. The origin is what a report wants. The three consumers only join a path, and to pass `{ root, origin }` through them is churn in files this batch does not own. A caller that needs to say which rule answered asks `cursorConfigRoot`. The `home` argument is the last fallback, so a path from this function is not always under it.

**Data root**

- `cursorDataRoot` is a different root from `cursorConfigRoot`. It is `$CURSOR_DATA_DIR` when set, else `~/.cursor`, and the source-level function is `ok()` at `cursor-config/dist/paths.js`, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`, with no `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` step.
- There is no `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` step here, so the two roots diverge in exactly the case batch 1 was about. Under the default both are `~/.cursor` and the difference is invisible. The divergence appears in one of two cases. Somebody exports `CURSOR_DATA_DIR`, which moves the data root and leaves the config root alone. Or somebody exports `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, which moves the config root and leaves the data root alone.
- Neither variable is documented on any Cursor page. A user who sets one has no way to learn that the two halves are addressed apart.
- What lives under the data root is `cursorProjectDataRoot`, the `<data root>/projects` tree from `m4()` in the same module, which holds the CLI's MCP OAuth tokens. So "this importer never reads the data root" is a statement about a choice rather than about emptiness.

**Project data**

- `CURSOR_PROJECT_DATA_FILES` marks `mcp-auth.json` as a credential and the other two as decision lists. That file holds `access_token` and `refresh_token` per MCP server, and the source-level class is `Yt` (exported `Q7`) at `mcp-agent-exec/dist/index.js`. Its `loadMcpAuth` reads the parsed object and keeps whichever shape it finds: a flat token entry becomes `{ tokens: <entry> }`.
- `mcp-auth.json` is the credential file: named, checked with `existsSync`, never opened. The other two are decision lists: which servers the user approved, and which the user switched off. To call a decision list a credential spends the one warning a user reads on a file with no secret. That is the same mistake the OpenCode importer records for its own `server.json`.
- The per-workspace directory name is deliberately not computed here, although it is a pure function. The source function is `r_()` at `utils/dist/workspace-paths.js`. Its slug replaces every run of non-alphanumeric characters with `-` and trims the edges.
- Two reasons keep the slug out. First, the importer cannot compute it: `Xq()` takes the git root where there is one, not the current directory. The approvals module passes `gitRoot ?? workspace`, so the slug is over a directory this importer never resolves and has no other reason to resolve. Second, a path built from the wrong root is worse than no path. A report that prints `~/.cursor/projects/<slug of cwd>/mcp-auth.json` when the file is not there teaches the reader to distrust every other path in the report. That is the one cost a "named, never opened" line is allowed to pay.
- So `readCursorProjectData` lists the tree instead, which finds the file wherever the git root pointed and needs no slug at all.

**Editor user data**

- `cursorUserDataRoot` is the one Cursor path with a hard platform branch: `%APPDATA%\Cursor\User` on Windows, `~/Library/Application Support/Cursor/User` on macOS, and `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/Cursor/User` or `~/.config/Cursor/User` elsewhere. All three spellings are the VS Code convention, which Cursor inherits as a fork.
- `%APPDATA%` is honoured when it is set and non-empty. An unset or whitespace-only value falls through to the platform default rather than to a path under the current directory, which is what `join("", "Cursor", "User")` produces.
- The report gives the directory a name, because a reader who looks in `~/.cursor` for the editor's state finds nothing. On Windows the place to look is the one place a POSIX-shaped importer never goes.
- `cursorStateDatabase` is a name and never an open. The chat bodies live inside the `state.vscdb` file, and they are the one part of a Cursor install this importer cannot carry. Cursor is a VS Code fork, so a chat is an editor state record rather than a transcript. The record is keyed by a workspace hash that cannot be recomputed.
- VS Code mixes the folder's creation time into the digest. The source is `src/vs/platform/workspaces/node/workspaces.ts`, and the call is `createHash('md5').update(folderUri.fsPath).update(ctime ? String(ctime) : '')`. So the widely repeated claim "it is the md5 of the folder path" is a loose description rather than a recipe. The report must not imply the hash is addressable, or a user will try to match it by hand.
- `cursorGlobalStateDatabase` is `<user data>/globalStorage/state.vscdb`, also named only. It holds the composer index rather than the messages.

**Detection**

- `cursorDetectionRoots` returns two trees: the CLI's root and the user-data root. The second one is why the CLI's home alone is not enough. A user who used the IDE and never ran the CLI has no CLI files at all. The command creates them, and the editor's state goes to the user-data directory. Detection on the CLI home alone reports Cursor absent on exactly the machine where a Cursor install most obviously exists.
- Both trees come back rather than the one that answered, because `detectSources` asks "is any of these non-empty" and a user may have either. The first is `cursorUserRoot`, so it moves with `CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR`. A user who relocated the root is detected through the relocated one.

**Prompt list**

- The prompt list sits at `<cli home>/chats/<md5 of the resolved cwd>/`, the per-workspace subtree the CLI keeps its own state in. This is source-level, checked against the shipped bundle: module `./src/state/index.ts`, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`, the three functions webpack calls `mh`, `wk` and `r7`. So this directory is the CLI home joined with `chats` and the md5 of the resolved cwd.
- Two md5 hashes sit in this file and they are not the same hash. Both are called "the workspace hash", and they are computed from completely different things. This one is `md5(resolve(cwd))` and nothing else: no timestamp, no inode, no creation time. Anyone can check it. That is why `cursorPromptHistoryFile` takes the cwd as an argument rather than a scan for a directory whose name it cannot predict.
- The `state.vscdb` hash is the other one. It mixes the folder's creation time into the digest and cannot be recomputed. The loose claim "it is the md5 of the folder path" describes that one, and this recipe describes the prompt list one.
- The two are kept apart on purpose. A reader who applies the recomputable rule to `state.vscdb` computes a digest that matches nothing and concludes the install is broken. A reader who assumes the `state.vscdb` rule applies here does not notice a prompt list that is not there.
- `cursorPromptHistoryPath` returns `<cli home>/chats/<md5>/view/prompt_history.json`, the CLI's up-arrow recall list, named whether or not it is there. This is source-level: module `./src/history/prompt-history.ts`, function `c(e)`, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`, called as `c("view")`.
- The importer used to look for `~/.config/cursor/prompt_history.json` and `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/cursor/prompt_history.json`. Both were guesses between two second-hand spellings, because no official page documents the file. Neither guess was a path the source ever writes to. The root is `cursorConfigRoot`, the same root as `cli-config.json`, and the `chats/<md5>/` segments were left out entirely. So the file was never found on any machine.
- The path is one of the four pillars of the source's `present` flag. A user who typed prompts and wrote no rule, no config and no server file reached the report as a user with nothing to migrate.
- The path comes back unconditionally, because "there is no list" is a report sentence. A report that cannot name the directory it looked in is a report the user cannot check.
- `pasted_text.json` is named and not read, in the same `chats/<md5>/` directory, built by the same `join(base, sub, name)` shape. The module is `./src/history/pasted-text-store.ts`, build `2026.09.26-dd393fe`. The bodies of pasted blocks are not prompts and are not imported.
- `cursorPromptHistoryFile` returns `null` when nothing is there, rather than a path to a file that is not. It carries which of the three config-root rules answered. So the report can name the rule rather than print a path the reader has no way to connect to their own disk.

### Antigravity

**The evidence**

- The `.gemini` tree is attested against the product, and the path claims come from two places that are cited apart. `dist/paths.js` in the unpacked Electron launcher (`asar-out/dist/paths.js`) is source, so every `join(homedir(), '.gemini', …)` is that file's expression, verbatim and at the cited line.
- `language_server.exe` is a stripped Go binary, so a path there is cited by the literal that carries it and not by a line. A literal proves the product writes that path. It does not prove what is inside the directory the path names.
- Nothing in the module calls `os.homedir()`. The `home` argument is the only source of a home directory. That is why a migration can point at a fixture instead of the developer's own machine. The one exception is `antigravityTreeHasContent`, and it exists for one reason. The reader has to choose between the two data roots, and this module refuses to make that choice.

**The roots**

- `~/.gemini/antigravity-ide` is the data root the separately installed IDE reads. `asar-out/dist/paths.js:50` exports it as `IDE_NEW_DATA_DIR` and comments it "User data dir for the separately installed IDE (destination for copy)". It is the app's own spelling of where the current build keeps its data, and it is first in `antigravityDataDirs`.
- `~/.gemini/antigravity` is the pre-split root. `paths.js:48` exports it as `IDE_OLD_DATA_DIR` and comments it "User data dir for the old IDE (source for copy)". "Old" is the app's word. This is the tree a build before the IDE split wrote to, and it is what the wizard copies from.
- `~/.gemini/antigravity-backup` is deliberately not a root. `paths.js:52` exports it as `IDE_BACKUP_DATA_DIR` and comments it "User data dir for backup (destination for backup copy)". The name is exported so a report can name the duplicate copy. This module never offers it as somewhere to read from.
- The data roots are ordered most authoritative first, and the order is not a guess. The app's own IDE-split migration copies the first from the second and never deletes the source. `doSetup` in `maybeShowIdeInstallWizard` copies old → new only when the new one is absent, then old → backup only when that is absent (`dist/ideInstall/wizard.js:114-131`). `downloadAndInstallIde` copies old → new again after a fresh IDE download (`dist/ideInstall/service.js:190`).
- The copy is a full recursive `fs.cp(source, dest, { recursive: true, force: true })` (`service.js:164-171`), so after it the new root is a superset of the old one. A conversation written after the split is only in the new one. That is why the new root wins and the old one is the fallback. The reader reads a file present in both once, from the copy the current build writes to.
- `antigravity-backup` stays out for a reason that is about the report. It is a third full copy of the same tree, made from the same source in the same function (`wizard.js:123-130`). A read of it imports every conversation twice, and the report calls the second copy a name collision.
- No environment variable moves any of the five paths. None of the expressions in `paths.js` consults one. A user who relocated the whole `.gemini` directory has a home the importer cannot be told about. That is a fact about the caller rather than something the function can resolve.
- The IDE's own app-data directory resolves to `antigravity` and is therefore not a third root, but not for the reason it looks like. `getAppDataDir()` is `join(homedir(), '.gemini', getAppDataDirName())` (`paths.js:24`), and `getAppDataDirName` is `app.isPackaged ? app.getName().toLowerCase().replace(/\s+/g, '') : 'antigravity-dev'` (`paths.js:17-22`).
- The packaged branch is the one that matters, and it yields `antigravity`. The shipped `package.json` carries `"name": "antigravity"` and `"productName": "Antigravity"` (`:2-3`), and the lowercase form of either is `antigravity`. The language server says so in its own words: "the app data directory is `~/.gemini/antigravity` (`%USERPROFILE%\.gemini\antigravity` on Windows)". That is the same string as `IDE_OLD_DATA_DIR`.
- So the derived app-data directory is a spelling of the old root, not a separate one. A third read of it imports every conversation twice. The argument is about what the value is. "A name a user never has" is false of the packaged build, which is the only build a user installs.
- `~/.gemini/config` is the customization root and the one directory under `.gemini` that is not inside a data root. The product's guide calls it the "Global Configuration (Machine-Local)" location. The skills, plugins, workflows, hooks and the global MCP document live under it. So a home can hold a complete Antigravity customization with no data root at all. A read of only the data roots finds nothing there.
- It sits beside the data roots, not below them: `join(homedir(), '.gemini', 'config', …)` (`paths.js:27`) and `join(homedir(), '.gemini', getAppDataDirName())` (`paths.js:24`) are two branches of the same parent.

**What a data root holds**

- The contents are quoted from the language server's own documentation rather than guessed. A data root holds `brain/`, one directory per conversation, and `sidecar_data/`. The latter is attested in three places as `<appDataDir>/sidecar_data/<sidecar-id>/…`, with `data/`, `logs/sidecar.log` and the sidecar's own code below it.
- Those three spell the parent as the app data directory, which is `antigravity`, the old root's own name (see the note on `getAppDataDirName`). So a sidecar written by a build before the IDE split is under `antigravity/`, not `antigravity-ide/`. That is the one place where the two roots are genuinely not interchangeable, so the import reads both and does not merge them.
- `<data root>/brain` is every conversation, one directory each. The binary carries a documentation block for an agent that reads a conversation's own logs, attested nine times in the product's own prose. It gives the path twice in full: `<appDataDir>/brain/<conversation-id>/.system_generated/logs/transcript.jsonl` and the same path with `transcript_full.jsonl`. Two more sites give the shape through the template the product substitutes, `{{ SystemGeneratedLogsPath }}/transcript.jsonl`. The directory beside the logs holds `swarm.md` (the multi-agent coordination file) and `scratch/`.
- An earlier draft looked for `<data root>/conversations`. The argument was that every hit for `conversations/` is the gRPC service table (`/v1/conversations`, `ListConversations`), the debug mux (`conversations/debug/pprof`) or a protobuf field. That argument was overstated and is corrected here, and the correction does not rescue the guess.
- There is a directory named `conversations`. Three prose strings in the binary name it as one, and the string reads `summaries_store: failed to stat / read / watch conversations dir %s: %w`. A fourth bare `conversations` sits in a settings-key table beside `settingEngine` and `SetSessionPin`.
- So the original conclusion held for a reason its argument did not support. A summaries store does keep a `conversations` directory, and it is not where conversations with transcripts live. The store is `brain/<id>`, and the gRPC service is a view of it.
- A reader that shipped `<data root>/conversations` finds a summaries store's directory or nothing at all on every real install. It tells its users they have no conversations. The lesson is narrow: a zero count was never the signal. Four attestations of a full path were, and their place in prose rather than in a literal was what made them easy to overlook. `conversations/` still has zero hits, `conversations` as a bare name has several, and the difference is the whole mistake.
- The two transcript tiers are the product's own and both are named. The compact `transcript.jsonl` comes with a `transcript_full.jsonl` for the steps whose `truncated_fields` say a value was cut. The order is the product's instruction rather than this module's preference, and it puts the compact file first: "Start with `transcript.jsonl` (compact). When `truncated_fields` is present, read only that specific line in `transcript_full.jsonl`."

**The MCP documents**

- `~/.gemini/config/mcp_config.json` is the global document, and it is attested. The product's own MCP documentation, carried inside `language_server.exe`, gives it as "Global Configuration: `~/.gemini/config/mcp_config.json` (applies to all sessions)". The second option is "Plugin Configuration: `plugins/<plugin_name>/mcp_config.json`".
- The file name is not a guess in either direction: `mcp_config.json` appears 14 times in the binary, and `mcp.json` and `mcp_settings.json` appear zero times each. A reader that reached for `.mcp.json`, the spelling every other tool in this repo's migration set uses, finds nothing. It reports that the user has no MCP servers.
- `<data root>/mcp_config.json` is inferred, and it is the weaker of the two locations. The product documents exactly two, and neither is this path. No literal in the launcher or the binary spells it. It is here because a data root is the other place a per-user document plausibly lives. A candidate that costs one `existsSync` recovers a file that is invisible without it.
- The cost of the inference is bounded and stated. The reader treats this path as secondary to the global one. A documented location is one the product reads, and an undocumented one is a guess.
- The order of `antigravityMcpConfigPaths` is the reverse of `antigravityDataDirs` on purpose. The data roots are ordered by which tree the current build writes to, and the MCP documents are ordered by which location the product documents. When both exist and both name the same server, the documented one wins. A file a vendor documents is a file its reader opens, and an inferred path may be a stale copy. The reader records the collision instead of a silent drop. "You have this server in two places and only one was read" is a sentence the user is owed.

**The named-only paths**

- `ANTIGRAVITY_NAMED_ONLY` is a `Record<string, string>` for the same reason `STEP_STATE_FILES` is one in `step-read.ts`. The reason lives next to the name. So a reader of the report and a reader of this file cannot disagree about why something was left alone.
- The keys are labels, not resolved paths. They are forward-slashed and `~`-rooted because the report renders every path it names with forward slashes. A Windows user recognises `~/.gemini/config/hooks.json`, while `%USERPROFILE%\.gemini\config\hooks.json` is a different string on every machine. The reader turns each key that exists into one `skipped` entry. Keys that do not exist produce nothing, so an install without them is not told about five directories it does not have.
- `hooks.json` is attested by the product's own customization guide as "Hooks | `hooks.json` | Lifecycle Event". Its event names and payload shape are Antigravity's own. This build's hook set is a fixed list of events with shell-command handlers only. A translation is a decision the planner makes with its own schema in hand. A reader that copied the block produces a report that claims a translation nobody wrote.
- `workflows.json` is attested twice, once as `~/.gemini/config/workflows.json` in the built-in `migrate-workflows` skill's manifest list. It is the manifest of which workflows exist, which the `.md` files beside it already say.
- `plugins/` is attested as `plugins/<plugin_name>/` with `plugin.json`, `mcp_config.json`, `hooks.json`, `rules/`, `skills/` and `agents/` inside. It is a whole tree of the user's own content. It costs one line to name it. Without it, a home full of Antigravity plugins produces a report that says nothing about any of them.
