- `sidecars/` is attested as `~/.gemini/config/sidecars/<sidecar…>`. Each sidecar is a separate Node process the user starts, with its own `package.json`, its own frontend and its own persistent data directory.
- `antigravity-cli/settings.json` is attested as the config of the `antigravity` CLI, a different product from this IDE. It sits in a third tree this source has no reader for.

**The memory documents**

- `~/.gemini/config/memory.txt` is attested three times in three distinct places. It appears as a bare path literal in the binary's string table. It is also a protobuf field that carries it as documentation beside a `path` field, and a protobuf field default, `def=~/.gemini/config/memory.txt`. The default settles it, because a default value is something the product reads rather than something it mentions. It is the machine-local memory file.
- `~/.gemini/config/GEMINI.md` and `~/.gemini/config/AGENTS.md` are attested in form rather than in path. The product's customization guide lists `GEMINI.md` and `AGENTS.md` as "standalone" rule files "relative to the customization root". It names the root as `~/.gemini/config/`. No literal spells either path in full.
- `~/.gemini/GEMINI.md` is not attested. No `~/.gemini/GEMINI.md` literal exists in the binary and no `config/GEMINI.md` one either. `GEMINI.md` on its own is documented as a workspace file discovered by a walk up from a file to the repository root. That is a per-project thing a user-level migration cannot enumerate.
- It is first in `antigravityMemoryPaths` because it is the spelling the rest of this repo's migration set already agreed on for a `<vendor>/<MEMORY>.md`. It is marked as the one entry with no evidence behind it. The reader reads all four. So a home that has none of the attested ones and does have this one still has its instructions carried over.
- No file under a data root is in the list. `GEMINI.md` is a customization-root file in every attestation, and nothing in either binary places one inside a data directory.
- `antigravityMemoryPath` is the first entry of the plural helper, so the singular helper and the plural one cannot disagree about what the memory file is. Read its doc comment before you use it as an answer. `antigravity-read.ts` reads the plural list and reads all four.

**The value documents and the workflow trees**

- `~/.gemini/config/config.json` is the user settings document, and it is JSON despite the `config` in its name. `paths.js:26-28` returns it from `getSettingsPbPath()`, and the reader at `dist/utils.js:63-84` opens it with `JSON.parse`. The function's name says `Pb`. Nothing about the file is protobuf. A reader that trusts the name looks for a `.pb` document and reports every setting as absent.
- The file has two writers. The Electron launcher reads it directly for the theme (`dist/utils.js:65`), and the language server owns it. The binary's own plugin-management prompt says "Never hand-edit `~/.gemini/config/config.json` to do this: writes that bypass the language server miss the live reload". A plugin enable goes through `JetboxWriteState {"userConfig":{"plugins":{…}}}` over its RPC, deep-merged. So the file is the settings store, written by the language server and read by both halves of the app.
- `~/.gemini/config/skills` is the global skills tree, one directory per skill.
- `~/.gemini/config/workflows/*.md` and `~/.gemini/config/global_workflows/*.md` are the two legacy workflow trees, deprecated by the product itself. The built-in `migrate-workflows` skill opens with a deprecation notice for workflows (`.agents/workflows/*.md` or `_agents/workflows/*.md`). It credits skills (`.agents/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`) with all the capabilities of workflows and more: first-class slash command support, semantic agent discovery, multi-file capabilities. It then lists both global locations among the trees it scans and converts to `~/.gemini/config/skills/<name>/SKILL.md`. The archive step is a rename to `<name>.md.bak`.
- The reader reads both anyway, for exactly the reason that skill gives. A user who never ran it still has real instructions in these trees. The change it makes is the vendor's own: a workflow markdown becomes a skill directory.
- The two trees are two spellings rather than one nested in the other. Nothing in the binary shows `global_workflows` inside `workflows`, so a home may have either or both, and both are read.

**The tree test**

- `antigravityTreeHasContent` is the same test and the same shape as `treeHasContent` in `step-home.ts` and `sourceHasContent` in `migrate-types.ts`. Three copies of a two-line predicate have to agree. Two of them decide whether a source is offered, and the third decides which root gets read. A directory that exists and is empty counts as absent, which is the answer detection gives too.
- It is the one function in this module that touches the filesystem, and it is exported for a reason. The choice between the two data roots belongs to `antigravity-read.ts`. That file reads a directory and can say which one it chose and what the other held. `antigravityDataDirs` returns both roots in priority order and does not choose between them. So this module never reports a path as the user's data directory. This module never answers "where does this source read from?", because the answer comes from both.

**The read side**

- The reader never reads a credential. Antigravity keeps OAuth tokens in the OS credential store, `wincred` on Windows and the keychain elsewhere, and no credential document sits under `~/.gemini`.
- The path `~/.config/gcloud/credentials.db` does appear in the binary, inside an agent prompt template, in a list of files the agent is told not to read. It belongs to gcloud, and nothing in this reader opens it.
- Conversation contents are not read and not parsed. The reader counts each conversation and measures one file inside it. A unit here is a conversation directory and a size, never a parsed turn. The history phase reads the transcripts later, on the same two-phase schedule every other source in this set uses.
- Nothing in the reader throws. Every read that fails becomes one `skipped` line that names what failed and why. A migration that stops on one damaged file loses every other source's import to make a point about that file.
- A source with no state produces an empty report rather than a line that says so. The `present` field is how a caller tells the two apart. It is true when `~/.gemini` exists and holds something.
- The `dataDir` field records which root answered. `null` is a real answer: customization with no IDE data, the shape of a user who installed the CLI side or deleted the IDE. It stops a read of `brain/` and `mcp_config.json` out of a directory this reader never established was theirs. The other candidate stays on `dataDirs`, because the fact that the IDE has two spellings for its data directory belongs in the report.
- The one decision this module makes about where to read is made by a question rather than by an assumption. The first root with anything in it wins. That is `antigravity-ide` on a machine that went through the IDE split and `antigravity` on one that did not.

**The theme**

- `dist/utils.js:63-84` resolves the theme in two steps, and the second is not an equality test. The value is `config?.userSettings?.themeMode`. The first test asks whether the value has `INHERIT` in it, and it answers `DARK` or `LIGHT` from `nativeTheme.shouldUseDarkColors`. The second asks whether the value has `LIGHT` in it. Everything else is `DARK`.
- Both tests use `String.prototype.includes`, and that matters. The value is an enum the product spells in more than one form, and this reader cannot enumerate the forms. A test for equality with `LIGHT` reads `LIGHT_MODE`, `MODE_LIGHT` or `light` as dark, silently and in the direction the user did not choose. The order is the app's too, so `INHERIT` is tested first and a value that names both is inherited.
- The `themeMode &&` guard is reproduced. On a value that is not a string, `themeMode.includes` throws inside `getThemeMode`, whose `catch` returns `DARK` (`:80-83`). So `"themeMode": 1` is a dark theme to Antigravity rather than an absent one. A reader that treated it as absent lets the planner invent a preference the app does not have.
- `inheritsOsTheme` is a reason for the report to say "follows your system theme" rather than a theme to write. `nativeTheme.shouldUseDarkColors` is a property of the machine Antigravity runs on.

**The settings document, and the one recovery**

- Both halves of the product use plain JSON. The Electron launcher opens `config.json` with `JSON.parse` (`dist/utils.js:70`), and the binary's JSON-shape check for MCP is a `jsontext.Value` unmarshal. No evidence shows either file is JSONC. The only document the product documents as JSONC is a plugin's `plugin.json`, which this source does not read.
- The retry is a recovery, not a format claim, and the report names it. When `JSON.parse` fails, the text goes through `parseJsonc` once. A non-empty object comes back with `recovered: true`, and every caller turns that into one `skipped` line.
- The line is a fixed phrase: `not parseable as plain JSON — read anyway with comments and trailing commas stripped, so anything the stripping removed is not carried over`. "Every setting came across" and "every setting came across from a file this build does not officially parse" are different claims. The user is told which one holds.
- One input makes the recovery wrong. A document whose body is nothing but a block comment parses as an empty object under both readers, so it reports as damaged. The cost is a settings file that held nothing.
- The failure line is never the parser's message. A `SyntaxError` from `JSON.parse` quotes the text it choked on, and that puts a fragment of the user's file into the report.
- The parsed document is `null` when it is unusable, never `{}`. The skip line says which of five cases applied. The cases are a directory where a file was expected, unreadable, not a JSON object, not parseable, and read anyway with comments stripped. An empty object lets a planner claim it read a document that held nothing.
- An absent file produces no line at all. A home that only installed the IDE never wrote settings, and that state is ordinary rather than a problem to report. `configPath` is always present in the record, as a path if never as a read.
- The reader asks with `statSync` before it tries to read. The two failures a caller must tell apart are absent and there but unreadable, and both arrive as exceptions from `readText` otherwise. Without the first question, a home that never installed Antigravity produces a report full of "unreadable" lines.
- A directory where a file was expected is its own case, and it is real. `~/.gemini/config` is a directory in this source, and the same name can be a file inside `~/.gemini`. A user who has it the other way round gets a sentence that says which.

**The credential guard**

- `scrubAntigravityCredentials` is the guard. Nothing in `config.json` is expected to be a secret, because the document holds `userSettings.themeMode` and similar. It is written as a guard rather than a step, because a guard that no reader can check is not a guard.
- Two tests drop key names. `looksLikeSecretName` matches `TOKEN`, `KEY`, `SECRET`, `PASSWORD` and `CREDENTIAL` as case-insensitive substrings (`migrate-types.ts:379-384`), which covers `apiKey`, `accessToken`, `refreshToken`, `clientSecret` and `privateKey`.
- The test is deliberately broad. It also matches a hypothetical `monkey`, and the importer drops a key by that name as a credential it is not. The cost is one line in the report, and that is cheaper than a token written into a file the user then shares.
- `authorization` is the one common spelling the helper misses, because none of the five markers is in it, so a second test catches it.
- The names go into `skipped`, and the values never leave the source. A token that a future version may put in the settings file never reaches a planner, a plan, a report or a written target file.
- The scan stops at a depth of eight, well past anything a settings document nests to. A pathological document cannot turn the scan into a walk of a megabyte-deep structure. A key deeper than that stays in place, which is the one thing the function can get wrong. The report states that fact and does not pretend it away.

**The MCP reader**

- The shape is the product's: a top-level object with an `mcpServers` map of server name to server object. The Go type is a struct whose `McpServers` field is `map[string]jsontext.Value`, tagged `json:"mcpServers"`. The failure the product raises when the field has the wrong type is the string `mcpServers field is not a JSON object, got %T`. So a value that is not an object is a real named condition, not a shape this reader invented.
- The report names an absent `mcpServers` instead of a silent drop. The one document where a user is likely to get it wrong is this one. Every other tool in this set puts servers at the top level. So `{"sqlite": {…}}` is a shape a user who comes from another tool writes by hand. The line says this source reads only the `mcpServers` field. Anything else invents a schema.
- Entries are handed over as they are. The Go map's value type is `jsontext.Value`, so any JSON value is legal where the product is concerned. This reader does not second-guess the value.
- Server values are copied without interpretation. `command`, `args` and `env` for a stdio server and `serverUrl` for an SSE one are the shapes the product documents. The planner decides what this build can reproduce from them.
- A server present in two documents appears once, from the earlier document in the priority order. `mcpSources` carries the path of each server, so the planner can print which document it came from. The map is provenance only, and the server's own configuration is on `mcpServers`. The collisions list names the kept file and the dropped file. `mcpConfigPaths` lists every document consulted, in priority order, whether or not it exists.

**The workflow walk**

- The walk is recursive, because the product's own discovery instructions write both trees as `*.md` globs and neither states a depth. A reader that assumed depth zero drops a nested workflow without a report.
- Four kinds of entry are set aside, each named with its own reason. An `*.md.bak` file is the product's own archive suffix. The built-in `migrate-workflows` skill renames each converted file to that suffix and does not delete it. So one of these is a workflow that was already converted. The line that names it is the difference between "already migrated" and "missed".
- A `README.md` is not a workflow, and every other reader in this repo refuses one for the same reason (`readCommandFiles` in `migrate-core.ts`). Anything that is not a `.md` is named rather than read. A workflow is markdown by the product's own definition, and a file that is not one is not a workflow this importer can translate. A directory is walked.
- A file can leave between the scan and the read. That race is named rather than passed over. A workflow the user can see and this walk cannot is not the same as one that is not there.
- The `detail` field on each result is what the planner needs to explain the copy. A workflow is a `.md` file, and a skill here is a directory with a `SKILL.md` and a `name`/`description` header. So the change is a rewrite and not a verbatim move, and the vendor's own migration does exactly that rewrite.

**The skills, the assets and the conversations**

- Skills come from `~/.gemini/config/skills/<name>/SKILL.md` and carry the files beside them as `attachments`. That is the shape `readSkillDirs` gives every other source, and it is the reason a skill's `references/` and `scripts/` travel with it.
- `readSkillDirs` passes over two things without a report. Both are worth a line here, because Antigravity defines a skill as a directory with a `SKILL.md` in it. A directory under `skills/` with no `SKILL.md` is user content in a directory the agent reads. A markdown file directly in the `skills/` root is not a skill in either shape the product documents. A read of it as a single-file skill cannot cite a schema.
- A workflow whose name a skill already answers to is left out and recorded in `nameCollisions` rather than dropped. The skill is the live shape, and the product itself retires the workflow in favour of it. So the skill is the entry to keep. The collision is a line in the report, so the user is told the second file was there.
- Workflows are added after skills. Two entries become one target directory, and which of them the plan writes is the plan's decision. A reader that dropped one leaves a report that says one file was imported where two were found.
- `kept` and `dropped` are this reader's words, not the plan's. Nothing is written yet, and the write step keeps the first of two writes to one target path. The record exists so the report can say a second file was found under a name already taken.
- A conversation is a directory under `brain/`, and its size is the size of the compact transcript inside it, from one `stat`. The compact transcript is the one the product tells an agent to read first, so its presence decides whether the conversation has anything to import. `transcript_full.jsonl` is the fallback for the steps whose `truncated_fields` point into it, not a second conversation. The conversation path is printed by the report and never opened.
- A file directly under the conversations tree is named, because the product puts one directory per conversation there. A conversation whose compact transcript is absent is named and counted with the reason. It is a conversation the user can see in Antigravity and this reader cannot account for.
- The scan keeps two answers apart: "the directory is not there" and "it is there and holds nothing". Those are different sentences to a user who asks whether their history came across.

**The skip list and the read order**

- The reader sorts `skipped` by name before it returns it. Two runs over one home produce the same report, rather than one that changes with the order the filesystem happened to hand back.
- A name in `skipped` is a label, not a resolved path. It is a bare name where that is unambiguous, and a forward-slashed relative label where it is not. A Windows separator inside one of those lines is a bug in the report, not a path. The convention is the same one `step-home.ts` and `step-read.ts` use for their skip labels.
- One of four things happened for a name to appear. The read failed, the parse failed, the file stayed unopened on purpose, or the importer read it and carried nothing because of a collision.
- The read runs in one order. The settings document comes first, then the theme that only it can answer and the MCP documents. The memory documents, the skills, the workflows and the conversations come next, and the named-only paths come last. Nothing short-circuits, so a home with a damaged `config.json` still yields its skills.
- Every path label under the home goes through `tildePath`. A skip line and a provenance line carry the same spelling on every platform.
- The named-only check is last and reports a path only when it exists. An install with none of them is not told about five paths it does not have. The label is resolved by a slice of the `~/.gemini` at its start rather than by a replace. A home whose own path has that text matches the wrong occurrence otherwise.
- An unreadable memory candidate is labeled with a `~`-rooted path. Four candidates with two of them in one directory mean a bare `memory.txt` does not say which one failed. The entries that come across keep the bare file name.
- The memory documents are one `memory` array rather than one string. A home can hold the machine-local memory file and the rule files at the same time. Skills and workflows share one `assets` array.
- The reader is pure with respect to everything outside the `home` argument. It resolves paths against that argument and no call reaches `os.homedir()`, so a fixture and a developer's own tree are the same code path. It does touch the filesystem, because a read must.

**The plan and the call site**

- The id is a plain literal with no cast, so the id has one spelling rather than eleven. The tables that key on the union are all in place: `MigrationSourceId` (`migrate-types.ts:33-48`), `MIGRATION_SOURCE_IDS` (`:69`), `MIGRATION_SOURCE_LABELS` (`:87`), `SOURCE_ROOTS` (`:135`) and its `detectionRoots` arm (`:287`).
- The call is one line in `migrate.ts:900`. This entry point takes the same arguments its neighbours do, so a call beside `planTrae` or `planStepCode` is a copy rather than a design.
- A source can be registered (id, label, the root tables, a reader and a planner) and still import nothing, because nothing calls the planner. Then `detectSources` finds `~/.gemini` and the report lists Antigravity. No setting, server, skill or rule from it reaches the target.
- The plan test, `migrate-antigravity-plan.test.ts`, calls this function directly and cannot see whether it is wired. So the call has its own coverage in `migrate-antigravity.test.ts`.

**The settings document, as the plan reads it**

- `~/.gemini/config/config.json` is a protojson document. Its top level holds one nested object, `userSettings`, and that shape is established rather than inferred. The launcher reads `config?.userSettings?.themeMode` out of it (`asar-out/dist/utils.js:71`), and the message behind the object is `jetbox_state_pb.UserSettings`. The only `GetThemeMode` accessor in `language_server.exe` belongs to it. Every `Get*` accessor the binary carries for `jetbox_state_pb.UserSettings` is a real key under `userSettings`, and the count is 44.
- The first set, `ANTIGRAVITY_CONFIG_HANDLED`, holds `userSettings` alone, and the reader reads it as the whole list. Everything else in the document is named by `planAntigravityConfigKeys` with no claim attached. That is the honest way to report a settings file whose schema this importer knows by key name alone. The product's customization guide lists Rules, Skills, Plugins, Hooks and MCP Servers as the customization surface, and names no setting beyond those.
- The second set, `ANTIGRAVITY_USER_SETTINGS_HANDLED`, holds `themeMode` and `permissionPreset`. The theme is mapped, because the launcher's own resolution of it is four lines of `String.prototype.includes` and a `nativeTheme` lookup. The preset is listed because it is accounted for with a line of its own, rather than by the catch-all. A key that earns its own sentence does not also belong in the catch-all.
- The report names the keys in two passes, because the document is two levels deep. That reporter, `reportUnhandledKeys`, prints names and never values, so the pass is safe over a document scrubbed only for credential-shaped keys. An install writes records like a `globalPermissionGrants` entry, an `allowedCommands` list, a `conversationWidth` or a `gcpRegion`, and each one arrives here as a name. The line beside it says this importer holds no rule for it and no note about it. The sandbox family gets no line of its own, because a name for each key asserts what that key does, and neither binary says. The honest sentence says that nothing is known about any of them.
- The planner uses the reader's resolution and does not repeat it. A second copy of a four-line function can drift from the one the reader runs, and the drift stays invisible.
- Three cases come out of that resolution. An `inheritsOsTheme` value gets a skip line, because `nativeTheme.shouldUseDarkColors` (`utils.js:73`) is a property of the machine Antigravity ran on. A resolve here answers for whatever machine runs the import. The nearest relative here is the `auto` theme, and that is a different resolver: the terminal's own background rather than the desktop's. So the reason is named and the choice is left to `/theme`.
- The importer claims a stated value. The launcher falls through to `DARK` (`utils.js:76`) for every value that is neither. So a `themeMode` of `SYSTEM` or `HIGH_CONTRAST` is a dark theme to Antigravity. A map to `dark` reproduces what the user saw.
- No stated value means the importer claims nothing and says nothing. The reader reports `declared: null` for three states: no theme stated, a document that does not parse, and a `themeMode` that is not a string. The app lands on `DARK` for all three, and for the third it does so through a `catch` on a `TypeError` from `(1).includes` (`utils.js:80-83`). To import that fall-through as a choice puts a value in the target's settings that nobody picked. The claim from `claimScalar` writes it over a theme this build already has. The `planT3RuntimeMode` planner makes the same call for an absent `defaultRuntimeMode`, and that key is not a security setting.
- The preset item is echoed verbatim rather than resolved to a name. The document is protojson, and this importer established the field, not the way a particular build marshalled the enum inside it. The report names the value rather than keep silent about it. A user who set `TURBO` sees that Antigravity held it and this importer declined it. That is different from a report that never mentions the most important setting in the file. The line tells the user to set the posture with `/mode`.

**The permission preset, the sandbox and the model**

- `jetbox_state_go_proto.(*UserSettings).GetPermissionPreset` is one of the 44 accessors, and its enum is `exa.codeium_common_pb.AgentPermissionPreset`. Each of `AGENT_PERMISSION_PRESET_UNSPECIFIED`, `_REQUEST_REVIEW`, `_DEFAULT`, `_VETTED`, `_TURBO`, `_AUTO` and `_NONE` is an exact literal in the binary, once each.
- Nothing in either binary states what the seven names do. There is no label, no description and no documentation for any of them anywhere in `language_server.exe`. The phrase "Request Review" appears zero times. The words "Turbo" and "Vetted" that do appear belong to `google.internal.cloud.code.v1internal.TurboModeSetting` and `exa.project_pb.SecurityPluginSettings.Vetted`, neither of which is this enum.
- Seven names with no stated behaviour cannot honestly become this build's three permission modes. The most likely wrong guess, `TURBO` as "runs without a prompt", is the one direction a permission import must not move.
- The message has no `GetSandboxMode`, so there is no user-level sandbox setting to pair with the preset. The reader reads a `sandboxMode` in one of exactly two ways. One way is per project, from a project file, through `GetSandboxModeForProject` on `CliSettingsStore`, `IdeSettingsStore` or `JetskiHubSettingsStore`, whose failure log reads `failed to read project file for %s in GetSandboxConfig: %v`. The other way is `AdminControls.AgentControls`, the administrator's control plane and not a setting the user chose.
- The `claimModePair` helper exists so one foreign value cannot set one axis and leave the other to read as deliberate. The preset fed to it writes a sandbox Antigravity never stated, so the importer claims the pair per field instead.
- The five keys beside the preset in `UserSettings` (`enableTerminalSandbox`, `sandboxAllowNetwork`, `sandboxProxy`, `internetAccessPolicy`, `nonWorkspaceFileAccessPolicy`) are named by `planAntigravityConfigKeys` and mapped to nothing. A family of keys is not one decision. Which of them matches which of this build's two sandbox values is not written down anywhere.
- None of the 44 accessors is a model. The binary's model lists are the server's own catalogue. The `GetCascadeModelConfigsRequest/Response` and `GetCommandModelConfigsRequest/Response` shapes are RPC shapes, and `custom_models_config` is a field of messages other than `UserSettings`. So there is no user-chosen model in this file to resolve against this build's registry, and the importer claims none.
- No credential sits anywhere in this source. The OAuth tokens live in the OS credential store, as the read side says at length. The only credential-shaped keys here arrive already removed from the parsed document and named in `skipped`. The one place a secret can still reach a written file is an MCP server's `env`. That value goes through the same hook every other source uses.

**The MCP planner**

- The transport is unambiguous in the product's documentation: a `command` means stdio and a `serverUrl` means a remote server. A server with neither is one Antigravity never connected. The two shapes are rebuilt rather than filtered, so a config that carries both is a stdio server here. That rule leaves a usable definition rather than one half of each.
- The importer drops a malformed entry inside a server, and the server stays. The reader hands server objects over uninterpreted, and the Go map's value type is `jsontext.Value`. So any JSON is legal where the product is concerned. This build's schema wants `args` and `env` to hold strings, because they are argv and an environment block. A server that works, lost over one number in place of a string, is the worse result. So the importer drops and counts entries that are not strings. No value is ever printed, only the count of drops and whether a variable name looks like a credential.
- An entry that is not a table is skipped as `not a server table`.
- The reader recorded which document answered for each name, already in the report's own path form. So the `from` label costs nothing and says which file the server came from. That is the difference between "this server was imported" and "this server was imported from the file the product documents". The lookup is `mcpSources[name]`, and the fallback is the first document in the priority order.
- A server whose only shape is a `serverUrl` goes through `urlCredentialProblem` first. There is no half to keep: an address with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` stripped points at nothing, so the server is not carried. The reason names the shape and never the value, and `containsSecret` is `true` although nothing was written. That is deliberate: the flag answers "was anything here a secret?" without a second derivation in the reader. No `markMcpSecret` call happens, because no file receives the value.
- The importer drops and counts arguments that are not strings. Environment variables whose value is not a string get the same treatment. Names only ever leave the env block, because the values are copied and never read back out. So a credential cannot reach the report through a count or a name.
- The rebuilt config goes through `McpServerConfigSchema.safeParse` before the downgrade notes are added. A failure is a skip. The stdio sentence says the command, arguments or environment are not a definition this build accepts. The other says the `serverUrl` is not an address this build's MCP client accepts.
- The key set, `ANTIGRAVITY_MCP_KEYS`, holds `command`, `args`, `env` and `serverUrl`, from the one documentation block headed `# MCP Servers (mcp_config.json)` in `language_server.exe`. That block gives `command` as required, `args` and `env` as optional, and `serverUrl` as required for the SSE transport. The example document above it carries both transports under one `mcpServers`. There is no `cwd`, no `headers` and no `type` in it. A key outside the set is named, not dropped, because a hand-written entry is where an undocumented key turns up. The downgrade note says the key is not one of the four Antigravity gives, and the names go through `summarizeNames`.
- A placeholder value in the config gets the shared placeholder note as one more downgrade. A server that names both transports gets a note: the command was taken as the transport.
- A name already in the target's servers is a skip with a note to use `--force` to overwrite. With `--force` the entry replaces the old server.
- The secret flag comes from the environment variable names, through `looksLikeSecretName`. The flag goes to `markMcpSecret`. The detail says the server was copied verbatim, and a second clause appears when the environment may hold a credential. That clause states that the reader read only the variable names.
- The reader merged the two documents in the priority order from `antigravity-home.ts`. The documented global document comes first, and the reader kept it. This planner says so. "You have this server in two places and only one was read" is a sentence the user is owed, and the merge stays silent. The item names the kept file and the dropped file. It also says Antigravity applies a global document and a per-data-root one in that order of authority.

**The rule names and the workflow copy**

- Two `GEMINI.md` candidates exist: one at the `.gemini` root and one in the customization directory. They are two different documents. The basename alone gives them one rule-file name. The write helper, `planMemoryAsRule`, guards only against a file already on disk. During a plan nothing is on disk, so the second file queues a second write to the first path and both report `map`. The write step then keeps whichever came last, and the report says both arrived.
- So the path is folded into the name when the basename is not unique, and only then. A single `GEMINI.md` gets the short name, and two of them give `config-gemini` beside `gemini`.
- One rule file is written per document, named for the document rather than the source. The `memory.txt` file is the machine-local memory file, and the only one of the four attested as a protobuf field default. The `GEMINI.md` and `AGENTS.md` documents are two different kinds of rule, so one `imported-antigravity.md` loses which document said what and drops whichever lost.
- A workflow markdown is written to `skills/<name>/SKILL.md`, which is where Antigravity's own `migrate-workflows` skill puts its conversions. So the destination is the vendor's choice rather than this planner's. The header is copied as it stands, and the frontmatter is not rewritten. Nothing in the launcher or the language server spells out the keys an Antigravity workflow header uses. A skill the model never finds is a worse result than a workflow left where it was. So the report names the check the user has to make: the file needs a `name` and a `description` before the skill is discoverable.
- Which assets are workflows is decided by the tree they came from, not by a field on the record. The tree path keeps its separator when the plan compares prefixes, so `workflows_old` does not match `workflows`.
- A workflow and a skill under one name get a report line rather than a drop. The skill is the live shape, the product retires the workflow in favour of it, so the skill is the entry to keep. The report names the second file and says nothing was merged from it.

**The leftovers and the plan signature**

- The count includes the conversations, and they never cross over. A conversation is a directory under `brain/` named for its id, so the reader can count them without a read. The count is the difference between a user who expected their history to come across and a user who never had any. The transcript inside is `.system_generated/logs/transcript.jsonl`, with the full log beside it for the steps whose truncated fields point into it. Nothing under the data root is written, so the conversations stay where Antigravity put them.
- When neither root held anything, the item says so: customization with no IDE data is a real state, not a failure. There were no conversations to count and no per-data-root `mcp_config.json` to read. It is what a user who installed the CLI side, or deleted the IDE, has.
- The root that did not answer is named. The app copies one tree out of the other and never deletes the source. So the reader reads a file in both once, from the current build's copy. Anything only the other root holds stayed there.
- The reader's own skip list is carried into items as it stands, one item per entry, with the reader's reason.
- The signature is TRAE's with `claimScalar` added. The added `claimScalar` argument serves the theme and nothing else. `existingMcpServers` and `force` cover a server the user already has. The writes list carries the skills, workflows and rule files. `mcpServers` and `markMcpSecret` cover the one place a credential still reaches a written file. There is no `claimModePair` and no `addPermissionRules`, because a claim here needs the sandbox half the header explains.

### OpenClaw

**Two settings documents, and the state directory has three spellings**

- OpenClaw has two unrelated settings documents, and a user's model can be in either. `<stateDir>/openclaw.json` is the product configuration. `<agentDir>/settings.json` is a forked Claude Code settings manager (`src/agents/sessions/settings-storage.ts:89`) that holds `defaultProvider`, `defaultModel`, `defaultThinkingLevel`, `theme` and the rest (`src/agents/sessions/settings-storage.ts:73-111`). A read of the first document alone reports "no model set" for a user who set one. That is the most common way a migration of this shape goes wrong.
- `$include` is how the product layers documents, and an import that skips it imports a document the product does not run (`includes.ts:25`). The merge rules are specific: arrays concatenate, objects merge recursively, and a primitive takes the source (`includes.ts:204-207`).
- The MCP server map is open-world. A retired key anywhere in it means the whole file does not load. `McpServerSchema` ends in `.catchall(z.unknown())` (`zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:154`). The retired aliases are rejected in a `superRefine` rather than by the schema (the comment at `zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:75-76` says so). A migrator that ignored them reports a healthy install where the product refuses to start.
- The state directory has three spellings and only one of them is the answer (`state-dir.ts:21-43`, `cli/profile-utils.ts:26-37`, `infra/config-dir.ts:7-20`). They disagree about `OPENCLAW_CONFIG_PATH` and about the `.clawdbot` fallback. The table is in `openclaw-home.ts`.

Nothing in this reader throws. Every read that fails becomes a line in `RawOpenClaw.skipped` that names what failed and why, for the reason `qoder-read.ts` states in its own header. No field holds a credential and no field holds a conversation. `scrubOpenClawCredentials` names the shapes that were removed, and the header of `migrate-core.ts`'s `urlCredentialProblem` covers the one that no key-name scan can see.

**The configuration read**

- A configuration document has three states on disk, and `recovered` records whether the file needed the JSON5 fallback. OpenClaw tries strict JSON first and JSON5 second (`utils/parse-json-compat.ts:48-55`), so a file that only parses as JSON5 is a real and common state. Its comments are stripped on write (`config/json5-comments.ts:24-34`), which is why this importer never round-trips a document through the product's writer.
- The report names the configuration **after `$include` resolution**, the document OpenClaw actually runs, or `null` when nothing was readable. The `null` rather than `{}` is on purpose. A present but unusable file must say which of "not a JSON object" or "not parseable" applies. An empty object lets a planner claim it read a document that held nothing. **This is the merged document, not the file.** An importer that read `openclaw.json` alone and stopped imports a file the product merged other documents over. That is the single most important thing this reader does beyond the choice of root directories.
- The report names the candidate paths because the choice is not obvious. An install can hold both spellings, and the modern one wins (`paths.ts:38-44`).
- Include failures are isolated rather than fatal. `resolveConfigIncludesForTopLevelKey` exists for exactly that (`includes.ts:637-646`), so a home whose include is broken still reports the keys it does have. The failure list names the files that did not load. Silence there is a report that claims a completeness it does not have.
- Retired MCP keys are named rather than counted. A whole-file failure in OpenClaw is not a per-key one, so the list is a diagnosis, not a partial import. The planner refuses the MCP map entirely when the list is not empty.
- The root document is first in the include file list, so `includeFiles.length > 1` is the test for an `$include` that pulled something in. The planner's report line uses that and not `> 0`.
- `$include` is a string or an array of strings (`includes.ts:25`). Anything else is a shape the product rejects, and this importer reports it rather than guess. A file already included by this document is a cycle the product detects and stops. It becomes a failure line rather than a second read.

**The merge direction**

- The direction of the merge is the opposite of the obvious one. The product returns `deepMerge(included, rest)` (`includes.ts:368`). `included` is the target and `rest` is the source: the keys of the document that declared the include, gathered as `siblingKeys` at `includes.ts:360-366`. Primitives take the source (`includes.ts:204-207`), so a key written in `openclaw.json` wins over the same key in an included file. An importer that merged the other way round inverts every override the user wrote in the file they actually edited.
- For an array of includes, the reduce at `includes.ts:336` makes each later include the source, so a later entry wins. That is the same rule one level down.
- An include path is resolved relative to the directory of the file that declares it (`includes.ts:415-418`), not to the root configuration's. The reader tracks the current document's base path for that reason. An included document's own includes resolve against its directory and merge into it before it merges upward.
- The bounds are the product's too: `OPENCLAW_INCLUDE_MAX_DEPTH` over the file chain and `OPENCLAW_INCLUDE_MAX_BYTES` per file (`includes.ts:26,31`). A chain deeper than the budget is a failure of that include rather than of the read. A document that does not load is worth less to the user than one that loads with a gap the report names.
- `stripJson5Comments` is hand-written rather than delegated to a JSON5 library, because the only JSON5 the product's writer produces is comments (`json5-comments.ts:24-34`). The string walk is the part a naive `replace` gets wrong. A URL in a config value holds `//`, and a strip to end-of-line truncates the document. A comma at the end of a sequence is handled here because the product names it. Its own comment on the fallback says it accepts JSON5 syntax, comments and a comma at the end of a sequence (`utils/parse-json-compat.ts:48`). A comma is removed only where the next non-space character closes a container, which keeps `"a", "b"` intact. A blanket `,}` replacement eats the comma out of an object value.

**Agents**

- The reader picks one agent. `agents` is a list whose entries carry an `id` (`agent-scope-config.ts:578-589` reads `resolveAgentConfig(cfg, id)`), and the product's store is laid out under `agents/<id>/agent`. A user with several agents has several stores, so this importer reads the first configured agent. That is the one the product calls `main` when none is named. To name one agent and say so is the honest choice: a reader that merged several produces a `RawOpenClaw` that describes no single install.
- An agent entry can state its own `agentDir`, and that path wins over the default layout. The store itself is `<agentDir>/openclaw-agent.sqlite`.
- Retired agent settings keys are four checks, and two of them are not top-level keys at all. `queueMode`, `websockets`, `skills` **as an object** (it is a `string[]` now) and `retry.maxDelayMs` are pushed by `requireSupportedSettings` (`settings-manager.ts:51-77`) and throw. A document that carries one does not load, so the planner says so rather than import a settings file that OpenClaw will not load. A top-level key name for `skills` or `retry.maxDelayMs` is a claim the user cannot act on.

**MCP, and the retired keys**

- Retired MCP keys come back from the **unsanitized** merge, for a reason that is not obvious and that a first draft got wrong. One of the nine rejected keys is `client_key`, and `looksLikeSecretName` matches the `KEY` inside it. The credential scrub deleted the key before the diagnosis ran, so the report named a configuration that OpenClaw refuses to load as clean. A diagnosis that reads the sanitized copy diagnoses the sanitized document rather than the user's.
- There are nine retired top-level keys: eight in the loop at `zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:77-86` plus `disabled` at `zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:102-113`. One more is nested under `codex` (`zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:95-104`). They exist as checks rather than as a strict object because the schema is `.catchall(z.unknown())` (`zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:154`). Without the `superRefine` the aliases are swallowed by the open-world catchall and accepted without a report. Such a file does not load in its entirety. So the importer reports the diagnosis rather than import the other keys, as if the file were healthy.
- `mcp.servers` is copied without interpretation, with every credential value removed. The map is keyed by a name the user chose (`types.mcp.ts:15-17`), and a server called `keyboard-mcp` is an ordinary thing to want. So the scrub walks **into** each entry and never deletes the entry. A `mcp` with no `servers` is an empty object rather than `null`, for the reason `qoder-read.ts` gives. The absence of the key is information, and an absent map reads the same as an empty one in a report either way.
- The credential **names** are separate from the servers, and that split exists for a reason. The scrub drops `env` and `headers` whole, so a planner that reads `mcpServers` sees no trace of them. It then reports a clean `map` for a server whose secrets the importer dropped. That is the "everything came across" report the credential rules exist to stop. The reader takes the names from the **original** entry, before the scrub runs. A read of the scrubbed copy finds only the keys the scrub kept. So a credential-shaped name such as `MY_TOKEN` or `authorization` is absent from the report that exists to name it. The planner can then say which names were left off and tell the user to set them again here. Never a value.
- `envNames` is what this source exists for. OpenClaw's own provider key list (`src/infra/dotenv.ts:43,61,62,76`) includes `KIMI_API_KEY`, `KIMICODE_API_KEY`, `OPENCODE_API_KEY`, `DEEPSEEK_API_KEY` and `MINIMAX_API_KEY`, so this block is a place where **other tools'** credentials live. An importer that copied `env` into this build's settings hands one tool's key to another file. An importer that copies nothing and **names what it saw** keeps that fact visible. The names come from the unsanitized merge, for the same reason as the MCP names. The scrub removed the values, so the scrubbed copy has no names left to report.
- `env` carries an index signature (`types.openclaw.ts:57-67`). A string value directly under it is the documented "sugar" for `env.vars`, and a record value is a structured block (`shellEnv` and friends). Both shapes are named, neither is read, and no value is ever returned.

**The credential scrub**

Four classes of thing are removed, and the fourth is the one that matters. The distinction is the same one `scrubQoderCredentials` draws. Read it backwards and a scrub deletes a user's servers.

- Slots the product itself registers sensitive. `mcp.servers[].env` and `mcp.servers[].headers` are wrapped in `.register(sensitive)` in the product's own schema (`zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:20-24,33-37`), every value, whatever it is called.
- Keys that read as credentials. `looksLikeSecretName` covers `apiKey`, `accessToken`, `clientSecret` and `password`.
- Named slots. `OPENCLAW_CREDENTIAL_SLOTS` covers the slots whose name is not credential-shaped but whose contents are. `clientCert` and `clientKey` are TLS key material. `authProfiles` and `tokens` hold login state.
- The URL. `mcp.servers[].url` is checked only as http/https (`zod-schema.mcp-server.ts:26`), so `https://user:token@host/mcp` is a valid server as far as OpenClaw is concerned. The credential sits inside a string that every scanner treats as a safe identifier.

The fourth is why the scrub records `looksLikeSecretName`'s miss rather than trusts it. **The key is called `url` and there is nothing to key-name-match on.** The guard that catches it is `urlCredentialProblem`, and it lives in the planner. The value cannot be dropped here and the server kept, because a URL with its userinfo removed points at nothing.

Two exclusions from the slot list matter. `env` is deliberately **not** a whole-drop slot, which was a first-draft mistake worth a note. It is not only a credential slot, because `env.shellEnv` is plain configuration (`enabled`, `timeoutMs`, `types.openclaw.ts:55-67`), and a whole-block drop lost it. `env` recurses instead: the importer drops `env.vars` whole, and a bare `FOO: "bar"` under `env` survives because it is not a credential. the importer drops `auth` as a whole, for the same reason: it is a mix of `authProfiles`, which is state, and profile references.

`OPENCLAW_NAME_MAP_KEYS` is why the scrub does not delete a user's servers, and the bug it avoids is not hypothetical. `looksLikeSecretName` matches `KEY` inside a longer name, with no regard for case, so a server named `keyboard-mcp` matches it. A walk that tests every key it reaches deletes that user's server, its command, its arguments and its current directory. None of those is a secret.

That is the failure `qoder-read.ts` documents in its own header, and a first draft of this module reproduced it exactly. When the walk reaches one of these keys it recurses into each entry and keeps every entry. It drops credential-shaped keys **inside** an entry (`mcp.servers.x.env`, `models.providers.y.apiKey`) rather than the entry. `authProfiles` is deliberately not in the set: it is login state rather than a user-named map, so the importer drops it whole.

**The agent settings document and the stores**

- The reader reads `<agentDir>/settings.json` separately and never merges it into the product configuration. They are different documents with different schemas: this one carries `defaultProvider`, `defaultModel`, `defaultThinkingLevel` and `theme` (`settings-storage.ts:73-111`), and the product configuration carries none of them. The planner reads both and reports both.
- The agent's SQLite store is existence-checked only, or `null` when absent. The **name** is what a report may print. Nothing in this importer opens it. `openclaw-session.ts` does, under its own guarded reader.
- `sessions.json` is the retired store, existence-checked only, or `null`. `state-migrations.legacy-session-store.ts:1` declares it retired and `openclaw doctor --fix` migrates it into SQLite. A read of it is a migration source rather than a current store, so it is named rather than parsed.
- The session count comes from the store, and this path reads no transcript.

**The workspace documents**

- `AGENTS.md` is the one bootstrap document with an equivalent here, or `null`. These live in the workspace, not in `~/.openclaw`. The policy resolves them against `workspaceRoot` (`workspace-bootstrap-policy.ts:51-57`), so the field is `null` for a home that never ran an agent in a directory it kept.
- The other five bootstrap documents are named and not imported. This build has no counterpart for a persona document, a device identity, a user profile, a first-run script or a root memory index. The report carries their names because "no equivalent" and "we did not look" are different claims, and only one of them is true.
- `TOOLS.md` is absent from `OPENCLAW_BOOTSTRAP_FILENAMES` on purpose, and it is not reported here either, for the same reason. `openclaw doctor --fix` folds it into `AGENTS.md`.
- `MEMORY.md` is resolved by the product only when it is a **real file and not a symlink** (`root-memory-files.ts:41-52`). The legacy spelling is skipped outright (`root-memory-files.ts:72`). Both facts change the answer, so both get a line rather than the flat answer "not present".
- The file read uses `lstatSync` rather than `statSync`, because the product needs `entry.isFile() && !entry.isSymbolicLink()` on a directory entry, which is `lstat` semantics. `statSync` follows the link and reports a symlink to a real file as a plain file, which is the case the product refuses.
- The workspace argument has no default, for the reason `readSources` states: a workspace is not something a reader may pick for itself. The environment block has no default either, so no test can leave it out and still run.
- The skills come from both managed roots, de-duplicated by folder name, managed first. A folder name answered by both roots produces a collision line, and the second was not read.

**Small rules**

- The credential scan stops at depth eight, with the same note as `qoder-read.ts`.
- The default agent id is `main` when the configuration names none.
- A skip label is a **label, not a resolved path**, the same convention `qoder-read.ts` uses. Nothing ever holds a value read out of a credential-shaped key.
- `openclawDirNames` returns the directories under a root, sorted, and an unreadable root is empty.
- The skip list is sorted by name before the function returns, so two reads of one home produce the same report order.

### Trae

**The evidence rule, and the two wrong paths**

- The citations in this source are documentation-level rather than source-level, for the same reason as `cursor-home.ts`. TRAE ships no public tree, so a claim is a dated second-hand statement. Each path says which of three kinds of evidence it rests on. The kinds are **vendor documentation** (`docs.trae.ai` / `docs.trae.cn`, read 2026-09-29), **third-party tools**, and **inference from VS Code's inheritance**. A `file:line` citation is not available for any of them and is not invented.
- A full re-check against the documentation on 2026-09-29 moved the balance a long way from the first draft of the header. Nearly every path this module touches is now **vendor-documented**, and the two that are not are named as such where they are used. The old header line said three of its paths were documented and one was not. That line came before the read of the skills, commands, hooks and memories pages. The correction is in the header rather than left to be believed.
- Two paths that circulate widely are wrong, and both were believed when this importer was first planned. **`~/.cursor/mcp.json` is not TRAE's.** The claim traces to one AI-generated news page about a v1.3.0 release that does not appear in TRAE's own changelog at all. The word "Cursor" appears nowhere in that changelog. The research note that repeats the claim is wrong on two more counts in the same paragraph. Nothing reads that file.
- **`~/.trae/mcp.json` is not it either**, which is the harder one to refute because it is the *tidy* answer. TRAE's own rules, skills, commands, memory and hooks all live under `~/.trae/`, so a global MCP file there looks obvious. It has no documentation and no code behind it, and the application resolves MCP from its VS Code *profile* directory instead. That distinction is invisible until you know it, and silent when you get it wrong. The check on 2026-09-29 found that `docs.trae.ai/ide/add-mcp-servers` names `<project>/.trae/mcp.json` explicitly. It describes the global one only as "the `mcp.json` file in TraeCode", reached through a **Raw Config (JSON)** button. So the project half became vendor-documented and the global half stayed undocumented.
- The reader reads the global MCP file at `<user data>/User/mcp.json`. That path is **community-checked and vendor-undocumented**: several independent tools hardcode it, two of them after a fix for an earlier wrong path. One checked it against a live editor, by a decompile of the bundle that showed the old path is never opened. Only macOS was checked against a live install, so the Windows and Linux spellings are inference by analogy. That is why `traeUserDataRoot` takes its platform from the environment and says which rule it used.
- What the vendor does **not** document, and this module does not invent: the workspace-id hash inside `workspaceStorage`, and the storage of custom agents. Two incompatible id formats are in circulation for the first, and neither is reproducible. The agent page (read 2026-09-29) describes how to create, share and import agents at length and never gives a path. They live in editor state.

**The edition split**

- The `-cn` directory is a real product split rather than a locale setting, because the two documentation sites print different directories for the same product. The Chinese rules page gives global rules as `~/.trae-cn/user_rules` on macOS/Linux and `%userprofile%/.trae-cn/user_rules` on Windows. The English rules page gives the *same sentence* with `~/.trae`, and it never mentions the China build.
- The string `trae-cn` appears **zero times** in every page fetched from the English site during the re-check. Those pages are the rules, skills, commands, hooks, hook reference, MCP, memories, agent and changelog pages. Its Chinese-language variant under `?_lang=zh` counts too. That is itself a useful fact. The two sites are not two translations of one document but two documents for two products. The `-cn` suffix is the vendor's own way to keep the two installs from a shared home directory.
- So the "the English page gets `~/.trae-cn` wrong" shape was looked for and is **not there**. Each site is right about its own product. What *is* worth a note is the mirror image: a reader who trusts the English site alone never finds a China install. That is why `traeEdition` decides by which directory exists rather than by a locale.
- `traeEdition` is one function because three call sites need the answer, and three copies of the rule are three chances for them to disagree. The reader, the detection roots and the label all have to name the same edition. Otherwise the report says it read `~/.trae` on a machine whose rules live in `~/.trae-cn`. The China build is preferred when its home exists, because the two are separate products. A machine with both directories is one where the China build was installed and the international one later removed. A read of the latter reads a tree nothing writes to any more.
- `traeGlobalRulesDir` is `~/.trae/user_rules`, a **directory**, and the only documented form. The name appears in the rules page only ever as this path: never as `user_rules.md`, and never under `~/.trae/rules/`. One third-party tool returns `<dir>/user_rules.md` from a function that claims to find it, and puts it under the OS-specific `Trae/User` directory rather than `~/.trae/`. That is a genuine unresolved disagreement between sources, and the documented form is what this module reads. A file named `user_rules.md` under the global root gets its own line in the report, as the thing the other source found.
- The re-check on 2026-09-29 left that verdict unchanged. The English page heads the section "Rule directories" and gives the path bare. The Chinese page calls the same path the "local root directory" in both the macOS/Linux and the Windows bullet. Neither page ever writes a file extension, and neither mentions `user_rules.md`. So the rejected form still has no vendor page behind it.

**The profile directory**

- `TRAE_PRODUCT_DIRS` holds more than one name, and each name is needed. These are separate products that share a codebase: the international build, the China build and the two SOLO builds. A reader that guesses one finds nothing on a machine that runs another, and reports TRAE as absent on a machine that has it.
- This is the weakest claim in the module and is labelled as such. It rests on two things of very different strength, and the difference matters more now that the rest of the module is on vendor documentation.
- The derivation is solid: a VS Code fork inherits VS Code's path rule. That rule is the app data path joined with `productService.nameShort`. So the directory name *is* `nameShort` and TRAE ships a different one per product. That is a stock line of VS Code (the user data path module, reached from `environmentService.ts`). It tells the *rule* and not any of the four strings.
- The four strings are not vendor-documented. The re-check found that TRAE's own pages name the profile directory exactly once, in the general-issues article. It gives a path under `Trae\ModularData\ai-agent\snapshot` on Windows and under `Trae/ModularData/ai-agent/snapshot` on macOS. That is a **vendor** page and it confirms `Trae` for the international build on two platforms, which is what the first entry rests on. The other three are **third-party**: the configuration reference at `agentsview.io` lists `Trae`, `Trae CN` and `TRAE SOLO CN` under each of `%APPDATA%`, `~/Library/Application Support` and `~/.config`. The bare `TRAE SOLO` is the weakest of the four. It is in neither the vendor page nor that third-party list. It survives only because an earlier check of `product.json` said so.
- A trap the same evidence exposes: the macOS bundle is `TraeCode.app` and the Windows executable is `TraeCode.exe`, while the user-data directory stays `Trae`. The install directory stays `Programs\Trae\`. The name a user sees in Finder or Task Manager is not the name of the directory. A read by the visible name finds nothing.
- The two builds do not share a directory under any of the five discriminators, which is why `traeUserDataRoot` probes rather than branches on edition.
- `traeProfileBase` is the VS Code profile directory TRAE inherits: `%APPDATA%\Trae\User` on Windows. It is not under `~/.trae/` on any platform, which is the point of the module header. `%APPDATA%` is honoured when set and non-empty. Otherwise the profile lands under `<home>/AppData/Roaming` rather than a path relative to the process's current directory. That empty-base join is what `join("", "Trae", "User")` produces and what a reader must never then go and create.
- `traeUserDataRoot` returns the first of `TRAE_PRODUCT_DIRS` that is actually installed, or `null`. To probe rather than choose is the whole point: the four names are four products, and a user runs exactly one of them. The difference between "TRAE is installed" and "TRAE is not" is a question with two wrong answers if a name is picked as the answer. The edition that answered is carried in the result so the report can name it. The rules directory is then read from the home of that edition, because `~/.trae` and `~/.trae-cn` are not interchangeable.
- `traeGlobalMcpFile` is `<profile>/User/mcp.json`, the global MCP document. It is the one path in this module with **no vendor citation**, named as such in the header. The evidence is three independent tools that hardcode exactly this path, two of them after a correction for an earlier wrong path. A bundle-level check also shows that the widely-repeated other paths are never opened by the application. The importer reads it, and the report says where it came from. A file a user cannot find is a file they will assume was skipped. The re-check left the verdict unchanged, which is worth a note because this is the one place a vendor page is most wanted.
- `traeProjectMcpFile` is `<project>/.trae/mcp.json`, and that half **is** vendor-documented. The page says you can create an `mcp.json` file in the `.trae/` directory under the project root. That file declares one or more MCP servers. The page also names the switch that turns the half on: Settings > MCP, enable project-level MCP, then confirm in a popup. That is why the report's sentence for a project document says the servers are named rather than merged. The feature is off until the user turns it on, and this importer must not look like the reason it did nothing.

**The trees named and not imported**

- `TRAE_OWNED_ASSETS` is the result of the full re-check on 2026-09-29 and it did not exist before it. The first draft of this module was written from the rules page alone and quietly assumed the rest of `~/.trae/` was empty of anything importable. In fact four more features are documented with paths. A user with any of them got a report that said nothing at all about them.
- They are named rather than imported, and the reason is a boundary rather than a verdict. To carry a skill, a command, a hook or a memory, this module needs the asset planner and the writer that live in this repo. It has neither. What this module can do without either is make the gap visible, which is what `traeCarriedByNoOne` is for.
- Every entry is **vendor-documented** on `docs.trae.ai` unless the entry says otherwise. Each carries the changelog version that introduced it, because "TRAE has this" is a claim with a date attached. A reader three years from now needs to know which release made it true. The reader probes each one and puts it in `notImported` with its own sentence. The sentences are built from `TRAE_OWNED_ASSET_REASONS` in `trae-read.ts`.
- The rows are `skills` (project and global, v3.5.24/25, 2026-01-23), `commands` (project v3.5.56, 2026-04-28, global v3.5.54, 2026-04-15) and `hooks.json` (both scopes, v3.5.66, 2026-06-10). The `name` field is a union rather than `string` so the two tables cannot drift. A row here without a sentence is a type error rather than a report line that renders as `undefined`.
- `traeOwnedPath` turns one row into a path. The rule is arithmetic: the documented basename under the documented root for that scope. It holds for every row, which is why the table is restricted to rows it holds for. Memories and `skill-config.json` are named by the reader instead. The first is a file one level under its directory, and the second is a file rather than a tree. Either one in the table breaks its arithmetic rule.
- `TRAE_COMMANDS_MAX_DEPTH` is the same limit as the rules one, documented **separately** on the slash-commands page. It says the project commands directory supports "up to three levels of nested directories", with the same example-tree markers. It is a second constant rather than a shared one because it is a second sentence on a second page. Nothing ties the two limits together except that they currently agree.

**The other paths**

- The rules depth is the tool's number and not a reader's choice. The prose says the reader reads the rules directories recursively up to three levels. The example tree marks level 3 "Deepest readable level" and level 4 "Exceeds limit (unreadable)". A file below it is one TRAE never loaded. It is named rather than imported, and the report says which level it was at. So the limit stays visible, rather than a reader who decides on the user's behalf what counts as too deep.
- `traeGlobalMemoryFile` is `~/.trae/memory/user_profile.md`, from the memories page, which gives the storage location in a table for macOS, Linux and Windows. That page also says the feature is off until the Memory switch is on, under Settings > Rules & Memories > Memory. That is why this is a *user profile* file and not a project one.
- `traeProjectMemoryDir` comes from the same page with one hole in it. Project memory is `~/.trae/memory/projects/{project_path}/project_memory.md`, and the page never says what `{project_path}` is spelled like. It is an absolute path, a slug or a hash. The importer has no way to reconstruct it, so the *directory* is named and the file inside it is not guessed at. A report that prints a made-up path is worse than one that prints the tree and says why.
- `TRAE_SKILL_CONFIG_FILENAME` is `<project>/.trae/skill-config.json`, the list of disabled project skills. The skills page says the product creates it after skills are disabled, and that it lists the disabled project skills. Disabled global skills do not appear in it. That last sentence is why the file is worth a name at all. It is the only record of which skills the user deliberately turned off, and it lives nowhere else.
- The project directory and the global directory share one spelling, `.trae`. The rule basenames are `user_rules` for the global directory and `rules` for the project one.
- `traeGlobalOwned` is the `~/.trae[-cn]/<name>` join, so every global-owned path agrees on the edition.
- `traeWorkspaceStorageDir` is `<profile>/workspaceStorage`, one directory per opened workspace. `traeStateDatabase` is `<profile>/workspaceStorage/<id>/state.vscdb`, and the `<id>` is deliberately not parsed. A VS Code fork carries **two** id formats: older workspaces are a 32-character hex digest, and newer ones are a numeric timestamp. Both appear in the same directory on one machine. A reader that pattern-matches one shape silently skips half of a real install, so every entry in the directory is checked and named.
- The directory name is a hash that also cannot be recomputed reliably. It mixes in the creation time of the folder, and older versions hashed differently. The report must not imply the id is addressable. The `workspace.json` beside it holds the real path, and this module does not read it. Nothing in this importer needs a chat, and a chat is the one thing in that database this importer cannot carry.
- The re-check found **no vendor page that names this file at all**. That absence is a fact about the evidence rather than a gap in it. The agent page describes how to create, share, import and delete custom agents in detail and never says where they are kept. The general-issues page, which is the one place TRAE names anything inside its own profile, points at `ModularData/ai-agent/snapshot` instead. So "custom agents are in editor state" is the best available answer, and it is an inference. The other option, a guessed path, is worse.
- `traeGlobalStateDatabase` is `<profile>/globalStorage/state.vscdb`, named only.
- `traeCrossToolSkillsDir` is `<project>/.agents/skills`, the cross-tool skills directory TRAE reads. It is named and never read. This build's own `agents` source reads `~/.agents/skills`, a different directory in a different tree. The reason this is named rather than imported applies to every such case. Two sources that read one file is how the same skill lands twice under two owners.
- The skills page describes it as "a convention-based directory specified by Agent Skills", and puts it behind a switch. You add the directory to the project *and* toggle **Enable the `.agents/skills/` directory** on under Settings > Skills & Commands. The changelog dates the feature to v3.5.44 (2026-04-02). The toggle matters for the report sentence, which is corrected to say so. An importer that tells a user TRAE reads this directory unconditionally describes a state the user's own settings may not be in.
- The name is `traeCrossToolSkillsDir` rather than the `traeProjectSkillsDir` it used to be called, because `traeProjectSkillsDir` now means TRAE's *own* skills directory. Two different "skills directories" in one module under names that differ by four letters is a trap for whoever reads it next.
- `traeDetectionRoots` returns the trees that mean "TRAE is here" when they hold anything. Three spellings for the same question, and all three are returned rather than the one that answered. The two global homes are the CLI-and-rules half, and only one of them can exist per install. The China build and the international build are separate products. The profile directory is the IDE half, and it is the one that catches the user who opened TRAE and never wrote a rule.
- The profile entry is the *product* directory rather than the `User` directory inside it, one level up for a reason worth a note. `User` is created on first launch and can be empty on an install that was opened once. A root that reads as empty is a root detection skips.
- The global root is worth more than it was when this was written. Since v3.5.21 (2026-01-13) it holds memories, since v3.5.24/25 (2026-01-23) global skills, since v3.5.54 (2026-04-15) global commands and since v3.5.66 (2026-06-10) `hooks.json`. A user whose only TRAE trace is a global skill has a non-empty root. This detection is what keeps that machine off the "no TRAE install" list.

### Grok Build

**The one home, and the tree the importer reads**

- Grok Build keeps its user state in one home that `$GROK_HOME` can put anywhere. The home holds `config.toml`, the skill, rule, agent and command trees, the plugins, and the sessions. `$GROK_HOME` wins when it is set, and `~/.grok` is the fallback. The resolved root travels with the data, the same as the dsh source. Every report line comes from the root rather than from a guess about `~`. The `present` flag marks whether the home exists.
- This is the largest reader in the set, and the only one that walks a plugin directory tree part by part. A plugin can carry hooks, MCP servers, skills, agents and commands at once. A scan that stops at the first part drops the rest without a report. That is why the plugin read returns all of them and the caller reports each.
- Only this tree is read, and that is the decision this file rests on. Grok itself reads `~/.claude`, `~/.cursor` and `~/.agents` through its compatibility table, and `/import-claude` writes those paths into `[paths] extra_skill_dirs` and `[paths] extra_rule_dirs`. The `claude-code` and `agents` sources of this build already own those trees. A second import from here produces two copies of every skill, so the compatibility trees are named in the plan and left where they are. The reader reads `claude_import_state.json` for its existence alone and reports it, because it is the plainest evidence that grok carried that tree across once.
- Credentials are touched by `existsSync` and nothing else. Grok keeps two at this level: `auth.json` for the account and `mcp_credentials.json` for MCP OAuth tokens. Both are named rather than opened. An earlier pass over this tree concluded that `auth.json` was the only one, because the config watcher special-cases it. That watcher only watches config files, which is a different question than what holds a secret.

**The instruction documents**

- Grok's own list has six names (`INSTRUCTION_FILENAMES`, `xai-grok-tools/src/types/compat.rs:309-316`): `Agents.md`, `Claude.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `CLAUDE.local.md`, `AGENT.md` and `AGENTS.md`. Three of them are Claude Code's spelling, and grok carries them for its compatibility cells. Grok also appends `.claude/CLAUDE.md` and `.claude/CLAUDE.local.md` when the claude cell is on (`agent_filenames`, `:376-382`). Those files live in `~/.claude`, which the `claude-code` source owns here. A read from this source lands a second copy of the same document. That is the scope decision this file makes everywhere else about vendor trees.
- The three names this source reads are grok's own: `Agents.md`, `AGENT.md` and `AGENTS.md`. `AGENT.md` used to be left out, on a comment that called the pair beside it "grok's own rather than a vendor's". That reason covers `CLAUDE.md` and does not cover `AGENT.md`, the singular spelling of grok's own name. A user with `AGENT.md` and not `AGENTS.md` had an instruction file grok reads and this importer walked past.
- `Agents.md` is not a typo, and the case matters twice. Grok carries the capitalised spelling first for case-sensitive filesystems. A user with one spelling and not the other has an instruction file grok reads.
- The reader reads all the names that exist, not the first one. Grok collects every name it finds (`compat.rs:309`, `read_file/mod.rs:268-275`). The documents are joined into one text, and the dedupe is by the text itself. Grok dedupes by canonical path (`seen_canonical`), and the case where the difference matters is a case-insensitive filesystem, where both spellings are one file. A second pass over that file puts the user's instructions into the imported rule twice. Text identity settles the case without a question about the kind of filesystem. The only other case it collapses is two files that say the same thing, which is a duplicate either way. Two different instruction files still both arrive.
- One interface comment on the `memory` field still says "the first of grok's own names that exists". The reader joins every name that exists. The field holds the joined text.

**The skills, and the switches**

- The `[skills]` keys decide which skills exist. `paths` are extra locations to load, `ignore` are path prefixes to exclude, and `disabled` are skill names that stay listed but never load (`prompt/skills.rs:21-48`). The last two keys, `server_skill_dirs` and `bundled_skill_dirs`, name directories that the launcher syncs in from the server and from the platform bundle. They are not the user's skills to move between tools, so the report counts them in `serverSkillDirCount` and `bundledSkillDirCount`, and the importer walks neither.
- Grok makes a distinction this build cannot. A `disabled` skill stays in the list and is merely not run, while an `ignore` path prefix removes it outright (`filter_skills`, `prompt/skills.rs:590`). Neither state exists here, because this build loads every skill it is given. So a skill either crosses over or gets a skip line in the report. A skill the user switched off must not cross, because it carries instructions the user had already decided against.
- `ignore` is matched against the path as spelled rather than as resolved. Grok canonicalizes both sides first. A reader that resolved symlinks to decide what to read reads files to decide what to read. The plain prefix is what a user who writes `ignore = ["~/.grok/skills/foo"]` means, and the report says that `ignore` was applied, so the rare difference stays legible.
- `grokStringList` reads a TOML string array. A scalar of another type is not a path and not a skill name.
- The skill walk is recursive because grok's walk is (`walk_for_skill_md`, `discovery.rs:114`). The other four sources keep a flat `skills/<name>/`, so the shared helper is one level deep. That helper here drops a skill that sits in a subdirectory, without a report. Two details are copied from grok rather than chosen. The walk does not stop at a directory that has a `SKILL.md`, so a skill may hold skills and both are loaded. The only bound is grok's depth (`MAX_SKILL_WALK_DEPTH = 5`), not a directory blacklist. Grok skips nothing, `.git` and `node_modules` included, and a walk that skipped them reports a different set of skills than grok loads.
- Every skill is named after its own directory. A `[skills] paths` entry is the exception in shape: it may be a `SKILL.md` file, a skill directory, or a directory of skills. Grok takes exactly the file spelling (`expanded.file_name() == "SKILL.md"`, `prompt/skills.rs:335`), and the skill name comes from the directory that holds the file. That is why the directory is what gets read. A source's own root is searched for skills inside it (`find_skill_paths`). A `[skills] paths` entry may itself be a skill (`find_skill_md_paths`, which checks the directory's own `SKILL.md` first).
- A `[skills] paths` entry inside a vendor tree is another source's to carry, so the report names it with its owner instead of a read. An entry that is not there at all gets a line too. Grok only warns about that one ("config path does not exist"). A config path the user deleted is the kind of thing a report must say out loud. `skillPaths` carries the `[skills] paths` entries as written, so the plan can say where it looked.
- A name grok accepts for a skill is derived the way grok derives it. `normalize_skill_name` lowercases the text, turns every other character into a hyphen, collapses runs and trims the ends. `is_valid_skill_name` then accepts what is left only if it is non-empty and at most 64 characters (`discovery.rs:316-341`). Both halves matter here. `My Command.md` is the skill `my-command` in grok. A stem that normalizes to nothing is a skill grok drops.
