An import under the other name presents a name the source tool never had, and in the second case a skill it never loads.

**The commands**

- Grok loads `.md` files directly inside a `commands/` directory as skills. `$GROK_HOME` is itself in the skill config-dir chain (`collect_skill_config_dirs_from_sources` adds `grok_home` at priority three, `skills.rs:192`). `find_command_paths` runs on every dir in that chain (`skills.rs:298-306`). So `$GROK_HOME/commands/*.md` is a real user asset, and one that a previous pass over this tree declared absent. Skills win name collisions against commands.
- Two differences from the shared command reader are deliberate. This one is flat, because grok's `scan_md_files` does not recurse. `commands/fix/bugs.md` is not a command there, so a flattened `fix-bugs` is a skill the source tool never had. And the name comes from the frontmatter when the file has one rather than from the path, which is grok's order (`parse_skill_frontmatter(content, fallback_name)`). A `README.md` comes across as the skill `readme`, which is what grok does with it.
- Only exactly `.md` counts, because grok compares the extension literally, so `NOTES.MD` is not one of its either. An absent or unreadable directory gives no commands, which is what grok sees too. A file with no name grok takes, or a name longer than `MAX_COMMAND_NAME` characters, becomes a skip with its reason, as does an unreadable file.
- The `provenance` sentence, when given, stays with the file. A command that came out of a plugin must keep that mark, because this build has no plugin to enable or disable it with.

**The plugins**

- A directory is a plugin to grok when it has a manifest, or any of the paths grok looks for (`discovery.rs:605-616`). The manifests are `plugin.json`, `.grok-plugin/plugin.json` and `.claude-plugin/plugin.json`, in the order grok looks for one. The parts are `skills`, `commands`, `agents`, `.mcp.json` and `hooks/hooks.json`. A directory with none of them is skipped by grok with a warning, so a read here imports something grok does not load.
- Grok keeps hand-installed plugins at `$GROK_HOME/plugins`, one directory each. `scan_plugin_dir` reads direct children, sorted (`discovery.rs:454`). Grok's other plugin sources are `~/.claude/plugins` and its marketplace registries. Grok explicitly does not scan a legacy `~/.grok/plugins` when `$GROK_HOME` points elsewhere. The reason sits in the source's own words. Trust, persisted data and install paths all resolve under `grok_home()`, so a legacy scan is half-initialized (`discovery.rs:197-200`). This reader follows that in both directions: the vendor tree is another source's, and the legacy tree is not grok's.
- Grok's marketplace install directory is `installed-plugins`, overridable with `[plugins].install_dir`. That gives the plugin read two roots, one fewer than an earlier pass assumed. The reader reads the hand-installed root one level deep, which is what grok does with it. The install tree is walked instead. Each of its entries is a repository, and a repository can be a plugin itself, hold one in a subdirectory, or, in a monorepo, both. The walk descends past a plugin, and that is deliberate. A plugin's own `node_modules` or `.git` is not a plugin, because it has none of the parts. The walk stops there on its own. `trusted-plugins` is not a third root: it is a file that lists which plugins the user trusts.
- A plugin can point its skills, agents or commands at other directories through its manifest, as a single path or a list (`PathOrPaths::resolve`). A reader that only looked at the conventional names reports such a plugin as empty while grok loads all of it. Paths are joined onto the plugin root and must stay inside it, which is grok's containment rule. The importer drops a `..` that escapes rather than follows it, and `join` normalizes it away before the check.
- Grok enables a plugin as a unit, and this build has no such unit. So the content that is just files is carried, each file marked with the plugin it came from. That is the skills, the agent definitions, and the commands, which grok loads as skills too. The report counts and names the content that changes what the process does instead: an `.mcp.json` and a `hooks/hooks.json`. A third-party plugin's hook must not enter a user's own configuration before the user sees it. `pluginSkills` and `pluginAgents` carry the lifted files, `pluginCount` counts the plugins found, and `pluginMcpCount` and `pluginHookCount` carry the counted pair.
- `disabled` is the one switch that reaches plugin skills. Grok marks them after the plugin skills are merged in, while `ignore` is applied before the merge and so never sees them (`list_skills_with_plugins`).

**The memory trees, and the switch**

- Grok runs one of two memory generations, and the two are isolated trees rather than two views of one. `MemoryStorage::new_for_mode` roots v2 at `memory-v2/` and the legacy pipeline at `memory/`. The module states it outright: v2 uses an isolated root and cannot see files under the legacy root (`xai-grok-memory/src/storage.rs:53-70`). The switch is `[memory_v2] enabled` (`xai-grok-config-types/src/memory.rs:107-109`, resolved at `:745-747`), with a server-side gate this reader cannot see on top of it.
- The reader reads the global document from the tree grok's own switch chooses: `<root>/memory/MEMORY.md`, or `<root>/memory-v2/global/MEMORY.md` when `[memory_v2] enabled` is true. A plain read of `memory/MEMORY.md`, which this importer used to do, carries over the document grok no longer uses when that switch is on. The other tree's document is recorded too, in `globalMemorySource`. It is the one that used to be in force, or the one that takes over. Either way the user keeps exactly one of the two. Neither is merged: the loser of the switch is named.
- The importer counts the workspace-scoped memory documents and never reads them, in `memoryWorkspaceCount`. Grok stores them as `memory/<slug>-<hash8>/MEMORY.md`, keyed by the git repository the workspace belongs to, plus a `memory-v2/workspaces/<hash>` tree. A carried document changes its scope, because this build's memory is user-global prose, so a project's notes become instructions for every project. The global document beside them is a different thing and is imported.

**The trees counted or named, and never read**

- The runtime entries are the caches, logs, binaries and pieces of machine state under the home. Every name was checked at its join site rather than inferred from a name that sounds plausible. `hooks.log` and `memory.log` live under `logs/`, so a top-level name for them reports a real artifact as absent. The present ones are named in the report, so their absence from the plan reads as a decision rather than an omission.
- The unimported trees are the user-authored trees grok reads that have nothing to land here. They are `personas` and `roles` (grok's own `.toml` definitions), `workflows` (`.rhai` scripts), `agent-memory` (per-agent memory) and `hooks` (the user's own hook scripts). This build has a hooks system, but a grok hook is a shell script wired through grok's own `hooks.json`. That is a different contract rather than a different spelling of this one. The report counts and names each tree, and only the non-empty ones appear.
- Five more things get a report line by existence alone, and no reader opens them. `<root>/bundled` holds grok's own shipped skills and agents, and `<root>/marketplace-cache` holds downloaded plugins. `<root>/lsp.json` is the language-server config, and `<root>/pager.toml` holds UI preferences. `<root>/claude_import_state.json` is grok's own record of a Claude import.
- The vendor trees are the directories outside `$GROK_HOME` that grok itself reads. Two independent reasons put a tree there. The compatibility table makes grok read the vendor directories under the user's home, and a `[paths]` or `[skills] paths` entry can name one outright. Both come back as bare directory names, because that is how the plan speaks about them, as `~/.claude`. The `home` argument rather than the grok root is deliberate and important. These trees live beside the user's home, not beside `$GROK_HOME`. A `$GROK_HOME` of `/srv/grok` must not have the directories beside it probed for `.claude`.
- The machine-policy layers are the config layers that are machine or organization policy rather than user preference. `xai-grok-config/src/lib.rs:4-8` lists the whole chain, lowest priority first: `/etc/grok/managed_config.toml`, `$GROK_HOME/managed_config.toml`, a policy file under `/etc/grok`, `$GROK_HOME/requirements.toml` (an Ed25519-signed cloud cache) and `/etc/grok/requirements.toml`. The two inside the home are checked here, into `machinePolicy`, and the three outside it are named in the plan. None is imported, and that is the point. A policy value moved into the user's own settings file must not survive there as the user's own choice. The user never set it.
- The importer counts the sessions under `<root>/sessions` in `sessionCount`, and the history arm reads them. `unimported` holds one entry per non-empty tree, with its count. `rules` and `agents` come from the shared tree readers over `<root>/rules` and `<root>/agents`. `commands` merges the home's own commands with the plugin ones.

**The configuration read**

- `config.toml` is parsed with `Bun.TOML.parse`, the same call the Codex reader makes. An absent file gives an empty config. A document that does not parse migrates nothing from this source, and the report says so through `configError`. Grok refuses to start on one, so there is a file for the user to fix. An abort of the whole run over it also drops the sources that are fine. The reason is a fixed phrase rather than the parser's own message, which can quote the line it choked on.
- One class of document is legal, readable by grok, and rejected by this parser: a dotted key path with a digits-only segment. Such a segment after a dot is legal TOML. `[model.grok-4.6]` is the path `model` to `grok-4` to `6`: one table nested in another, and grok reads it as such. `Bun.TOML` rejects the whole document over it. The parse is therefore retried with those segments quoted before the file is given up on. Those quotes are a no-op under the spec, so what comes back is what grok read. The paths are recorded in `configDottedKeys` rather than quietly rewritten. A `[model.grok-4.6]` user asked for an override of a model by that name and got a nested pair of tables instead.
- `expandGrokTilde` matches exactly two spellings, `~` alone and `~/…`, and passes everything else through untouched (`expand_tilde_in`, `prompt/paths.rs:48`). Grok's narrow rule is copied here on purpose. On Windows `~\x` looks like it ought to expand and does not, so an expansion here reads a directory grok itself never opens.

**Small rules**

- The report names skills the source's own config switched off as skips in `skillSkips`, with the key that switched them off. They get report lines rather than drops. A user who disabled a skill in grok still knows it exists, and a user who forgot notices its absence here.
- The plugin skips carry the plugin name in the reason, and the path entries carry the config path that named them.

**The plan, and the model tables**

- The plan module is the biggest one left after the split. It borrows the most from `migrate-core.ts`: `isRecord`, `tildePath`, `summarizeNames`, `placeholderNote` and the rest. That reuse is the point of the split rather than a cost of it. What is specific to Grok is what is left here.
- The report names `config.toml` through the resolved root. It does not use `~/${SOURCE_ROOTS[id]}/config.toml`, because `$GROK_HOME` can point anywhere, and that spelling names a file the reader never opened.
- An imported endpoint that states no `context_window` gets the assumed 128,000 tokens of `GROK_ASSUMED_CONTEXT_WINDOW`. The helper `firstGrokString` takes the first string from a value that may be a string or a list. That is the shape `env_key` has.
- The `[models]` keys reach the report rather than the import, and each line carries its own reason. A refusal that explains itself is worth more than a name in a list.
- The key `default_reasoning_effort` sets a per-model default for an effort setting that is session-wide here and set with `/think`.
- The picker keys are `allowed_models`, `hidden_models` and `disabled_models`. The first allows a picker list, the second hides picker rows, and the third removes rows from grok's own catalogue. That catalogue is not the one here.
- The feature-model keys each name the model behind one grok feature, and each feature runs on the session model here. Those keys are `web_search`, `session_summary`, `image_description` and `prompt_suggestion`. The last one is ghost text, which this build does not have. The key `agent_type` gives an agent-definition type for models that name none, and agent definitions here are markdown files.
- The request keys are retry and shape settings. The key `extra_headers` gives request headers for every model, and a provider entry here carries an endpoint and a credential variable rather than headers. The keys `max_retries`, `inference_idle_timeout_secs`, `rate_limit_retry_threshold` and `subagent_rate_limit_max_attempts` are retry policy, which lives in the adapters here. The key `max_completion_tokens` is a global output cap, and the output budget here is per model. The keys `temperature` and `top_p` are not configurable per model here. The key `stream_tool_calls` is a request shape some endpoints need for tool-call streams, and the wire shape here is fixed.

**The provider tables, and the credential rule**

- The plan reads `[models]`, `[model.<id>]`, `[model_providers.<id>]`, `[permission]` and `[mcp_servers.*]`, plus the rest of `config.toml`.
- The `[model.<id>]` table is grok's per-model override table. An entry becomes a provider here when something gives it an endpoint. That endpoint is its own `base_url` or `api_base_url`, or a `model_provider` that names a `[model_providers.<id>]` entry with one. The second spelling is easy to miss and was missed here, and grok's own table sits one level over from Codex's. A model that reaches through it has an endpoint while its own table shows none. An entry with neither only retunes a row of grok's own catalogue and stays behind.
- Grok resolves a model's connection through `with_provider_defaults` (`agent/model_providers.rs:170`). The model's own fields win, and a model that sets none of them inherits the provider's `base_url`, `api_base_url`, `api_backend` and `context_window`. It also inherits the provider's `env_key`, `api_key` or credential helper when it has no credential of its own. So `[model.<id>] model_provider = "gateway"` is an endpoint definition whose endpoint is one table over. A read of only `[model.<id>]` reports "no endpoint of its own" about a model that has one.
- The credential rule shapes this part of the plan. Grok accepts an inline `api_key` in these tables, and this importer does not read its value. The settings schema here holds a variable name and has nowhere to put a literal. So such an endpoint is registered with a variable named after it. The report says the key is still in `config.toml` and needs an export. A copy into `settings.json` puts a live credential in a second file, which is the one thing this importer never does.
- The set `GROK_MODEL_ENTRY_HANDLED` lists the keys of a `[model.<id>]` entry this planner reads. The rest of such a table is a list of retunings (`reasoning_effort`, `temperature`, `max_retries`), and each gets a line rather than a drop. The entry itself may be carried over, and a user who reads that line takes it for a table that came across whole. The reader reads the key `model_provider` against the provider table, because an entry that names one takes its endpoint from it. The importer reads the key `auth_provider` for presence, like `api_key`. A model that names a helper has a credential of its own, so it takes none of its provider's (`with_provider_defaults`, `model_providers.rs:205-215`).
- The set `GROK_PROVIDER_ENTRY_HANDLED` lists the keys of a `[model_providers.<id>]` entry this planner reads or names. The three header-shaped tables are `extra_headers`, `query_params` and `env_http_headers`, and this planner names them in one line rather than read. A provider entry here carries an endpoint, a credential variable and models. There is nowhere for request headers or URL query parameters to go. The keys `auth` and `auth_provider` are likewise named. Grok runs a command to mint the endpoint's token, and this build has no such mechanism.
- The provider map keeps `endpointProblem` beside the endpoint rather than inside it. An endpoint dropped at that table reaches the model loop as a provider that "defines neither base_url nor api_base_url", which is false. It defines one, and this importer refuses it. The field `endpointField` names which of the two spellings answered, so the model's line can point at the field rather than at the table.
- Key paths with a digits-only segment get their own lines. TOML spells a dot in a bare key as a path separator, so `[model.grok-4.6]` is a table named `grok-4.6` to nobody. Grok reads `grok-4` then `6`, and so does the reader. The user who wrote it meant a model by that name, which is a different override and is not what either parser has. So the path is named and not imported as what it looks like. The message tells the user to quote the segment if they meant one key, and the importer takes nothing from that path either way.
- The model loop names a `[model.<id>]` entry through `reportUnhandledKeys` before the endpoint test. The entries that carry no endpoint are exactly the ones most likely to carry something else. A key left off the report reads as a setting this importer forgot. An alias that exists only because a dotted key path was repaired is skipped when it has no endpoint. The path `[model.grok-4.6]` leaves a table called `grok-4` with one key, and the loop has nothing true to add. An alias that a real `[model.grok-4]` table also contributes to has an endpoint and is planned as usual.
- The endpoint is the same credential channel an MCP server's `url` is, under another name. It reaches a provider entry through two shapes: the model's own `base_url` or `api_base_url`, or the provider table's when the model states none. Both are checked after the merge, so neither spelling can drift past the other. There is no half to keep, because the same address with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` stripped is a different address that points at nothing. So the model is left off rather than written under a line that says nothing in it is a secret.
- The provider's values apply only where the model states none. That is grok's own merge: `or_else` for the connection fields, and a hard switch for the credential. A model with any credential of its own takes none of the provider's (`with_provider_defaults`, `model_providers.rs:205-218`).
- The note on each endpoint says where its credential comes from. The cases are a variable its `env_key` names, an inline key that was not read, and a provider's variable. The rest are a helper that mints a token and no recorded variable at all. The inline key gets the loudest sentence, because that key is still in `config.toml` and needs an export or the endpoint cannot authenticate. The same note covers a provider's inline key and a provider's credential helper.
- A provider no model resolves through is inert in grok. The table `model_providers` is consulted per model override and nowhere else. So it is named rather than imported as an endpoint the user finds in the target and wonders about.
- The merge itself reports the endpoints that reached the patch. An entry the target already defines is kept, and a "map" line for it says an endpoint moved when it did not.
- The key `models.default` becomes the session model when it can. An entry the source defines claims the pair `<alias>/<wireId>` under the provider the source defines. A name that resolves through the model catalogue claims the resolved reference. A name no model answers to becomes a skip that asks the user to register the endpoint by hand.

**The permission rules**

- The permission rules are the bulk of the module. Grok spells them in its own rule language: a compact tool table, verbose forms and a per-server section. The planner `planGrokPermissions` decides, per form, whether the target can say the same thing and what to report when it cannot.
- The set `GROK_COMPACT_TOOL_NAMES` is exactly `tool_name_to_filter` (`permission/rules.rs:244`), which is what the array form's parser consults and what it rejects everything else against. Two things are easy to guess wrong from the config reference. The spellings are PascalCase with no lowercase arm at all. There are no entries for `Any` or `WebSearch` as a filter beyond the ones listed. A `bash(...)` or `Any(...)` in that array is an `UnknownToolPrefix` warning and a dropped rule, not a rule in force. A carried rule of that kind puts a rule in this build's list that the user's grok never had.
- The compact map pairs `Bash` with `bash`, `Read` with `read`, and `Edit` and `Write` with `edit`. It pairs `MCPTool` with `mcp`, and `Grep` and `Glob` with `grep`. It pairs `WebFetch` with `webfetch` and `WebSearch` with `websearch`. The pair `AgentMessage` and `SendSubagentMessage` map to `agent_message`, and the legacy spelling `SendAgentMessage` still parses.
- The set `GROK_VERBOSE_TOOL_NAMES` is a different vocabulary entirely. That field is the serde lowercase name of `ToolFilter`, so `Any` and `Bash` (valid prefixes in the array form) do not deserialize here. A table with one costs grok every rule in it. The set is narrower than the compact one, and `ToolFilter` has no `websearch` variant. So `tool = "websearch"` is one of the values that fail the whole table rather than a search rule. A name this set does not know gets exactly that label in the report. The verbose map pairs `any`, `bash`, `edit`, `read`, `grep`, `mcp`, `webfetch` and `agent_message` with themselves. The `alias` on the same serde field adds `agentmessage`.
- The list `GROK_UNSUPPORTED_RULE_FORMS` holds the three prefixes grok itself refuses (`UnsupportedToolPrefix`): `EnterWorktree`, `NotebookEdit` and `NotebookRead`. Grok's message for each is that no rule was ever in force for it.
- The parser `parseGrokRuleText` mirrors `parse_permission_rule` (`permission/rules.rs:109`) down to the parts this translation needs. Those parts are the `Tool(...)` prefix and its content, the `:*` suffix, and the `domain:` marker. The suffix means the command starts with the text rather than a glob. One more part is the `.claude` `mcp__<server>[__<tool>]` spelling, which grok rewrites onto its own qualified names. A prefix grok refuses is refused here too, so the two cannot disagree about which rules exist.
- The interface `GrokRuleParts` holds one split rule: a tool, an optional pattern and the domain flag.
- The two shapes fail differently on purpose. A bad compact entry is warned about and dropped while its neighbours load. A bad entry in the verbose `rules` array fails the deserialization of the whole table. Grok then ends up with no rules from it, and the caller reproduces both granularities.
- The parser details are grok's own. The helper `indexOfUnescaped` finds the first target that is not backslash-escaped, because grok's parser is escape-aware (`is_unescaped`). So `Read(a\)b.md)` is one rule with a parenthesis in its pattern rather than a malformed one. The `)` that closes a rule is found after the `(` that opens it, as grok does. A `)` before it belongs to neither the prefix nor the content. The flag `last` chooses the search direction grok uses for the `)`.
- Whatever follows that `)` is not read at all. Grok takes the content up to it and discards the rest. So `Bash(ls) # note` is the rule `Bash(ls)` and is carried as one. A bare name grok does not know becomes a rule that matches anything, with the literal itself as the pattern. For `mcp__` names, `<server>__<tool>` is already the qualified name, and a server alone covers every tool on it.
- Empty content and a lone `*` both mean a tool-wide rule. The order of the three strips is grok's. The bash colon suffix comes first, then the `domain:` prefix, and only then is an empty pattern read as no pattern at all. The helper `unescapeGrokRule` is grok's own reverse of its escape step.
- The translator `grokRuleText` turns one grok rule into this build's rule text, or gives the reason it cannot become one. Three differences between the two engines drive this, and each stays silent if the pattern is copied across. Grok matches a `Bash` pattern against a command that starts with it. It also matches against a command the pattern globs, while a rule here is either an exact string or a glob. So a pattern with no wildcard gets a `*` at the end. That is exactly the prefix match and nothing wider. `Bash(sed:*)` in grok blocks `sed-custom`, and `Bash(sed*)` here does too.
- Grok's `edit` covers writes, and `Edit(...)` here does not match the `Write` tool. So one source rule becomes two. A file protection that covers half of what it did is not something to let a user discover. Grok spells MCP tools `server__tool`, and this build spells them `mcp__server__tool`. A rule written in the source's own `.claude` spelling is rewritten by grok before it is ever matched.
- The form `Any` is refused in both of its shapes. Bare, it is a catch-all, and grok drops exactly those from its own `--allow`. Patterned, it matches whatever text the call happens to carry, and a rule here has no form for that.
- Other tools are refused with reasons. A `grep`, `webfetch` or `websearch` rule has no reader here, because the engine here consults rules for `Bash`, `Read`, `Edit`, `Write` and MCP tools only. A `domain:` marker means nothing to a rule nothing consults. An `agent_message` rule has no counterpart here, so there is nothing for it to gate. A rule for any other tool has no counterpart here either.
- An MCP rule becomes `mcp__*` when the source wrote `mcp__*`, and `mcp__<server>__<tool>` or `mcp__<server>__*` otherwise. Grok's qualified name is `server__tool`, and the bare-tool form is how a rule for one is written here.

**The permission planner, and the verbose table**

- The planner `planGrokPermissions` carries `[permission]` over as this build's rules. The two shapes grok accepts do not merge, and the difference carries weight. When any of the compact `allow`, `deny` or `ask` keys holds an array, `parse_toml_permission_section` returns those and never looks at `[[permission.rules]]`. A file with both is therefore reported as the one grok reads rather than as their union. An import of the other half puts rules in force here that were never in force there.
- The verbose shape carries its own trap, and it is not the one the config reference implies. An entry with no `action` does not default to deny. It fails the parse and takes the whole table with it (see `readGrokVerboseRules`). That is what the report says, rather than a rule-by-rule account of rules that never loaded.
- The action `ask` has no tier here, exactly as Codex's `prompt` decision does not. The rules that carry it are named rather than turned into an allow or a deny. A compact key that is not an array makes grok warn and fall through to the verbose table rather than fail. Grok reads the compact keys in the order `deny`, `allow`, `ask`. When one rule appears as both an allow and a deny, deny wins here as it does in grok. The allow is left in place so the file still reads like the decision that was made.
- The reader `readGrokVerboseRules` reads the verbose table the way serde reads it and reports nothing. The key `action` is required in this shape, and that is the trap. The enum `RuleAction` carries `#[default] Deny` (CWE-1188), and `PermissionRule.tool` and `pattern_mode` carry field-level `#[serde(default)]`. The action field carries none, and the struct has no container-level default either. So the `Deny` default is what Rust code gets when it constructs a rule, and not what a key that is not there deserializes to. An entry with no `action` fails the parse. The call `try_into::<PermissionConfig>()` refuses the whole table when it refuses any part, so grok holds none of the table's rules.
- A value of that entry taken as a deny puts a refusal in force here that was never in force there.
- The reader is pure and called from two places on purpose. The rule translator needs the rules, and the policy reader needs to know whether this section loaded at all. A failed table leaves the policy at its type default too. One reader means the two cannot disagree about whether the user's rules ever applied. A `rules` key that is not there is the container's own `#[serde(default)]`, so an absent table is simply no rules. The first failure is the one serde reports, so it is the one carried.
- The function `planGrokVerboseRules` translates the table, and the granularity is the point. One entry grok cannot deserialize costs it every rule in the table. So the report says that instead of rules grok never loaded, and `unloadedTable` writes that one line. That strictness is the whole reason it is one line rather than a per-entry account. The call `try_into::<PermissionConfig>()` either produces the table's rules or produces none of them.
- The set `GROK_PERMISSION_HANDLED` lists the `[permission]` keys this planner reads or names: `rules`, `allow`, `deny`, `ask` and `prompt_policy`. The rest reach `reportUnhandledKeys`.
- The key `prompt_policy` is real in grok, but it does not come from this file. A session's permission config does carry a `prompt_policy`, and its `Deny` is grok's own dontAsk (`manager/mod.rs` logs `always-approve is active while prompt_policy is dontAsk (Deny)`).
- The `config.toml` loader keeps only the rule tables. The chain is `extract_toml_permissions` to `parse_toml_permission_section` to a `PermissionConfig` whose sole field is `rules`. The shell's own test `permission_prompt_policy_warns_as_unconsumed` pins that a `prompt_policy` written in `[permission]` reaches the user as an unrecognized key. The policy the session actually runs with arrives from the Claude compat layer. There `DefaultPermissionMode::effects` maps a `.claude` settings `permissions.defaultMode` of `dontAsk` to `Deny` and `auto` to `Auto`.
- So `prompt_policy` gets a line rather than a carried value. A carried value tells a user that a policy grok warned about and ignored is now in force under another name. The mode it has to become is precisely the one this migration may not invent on the user's behalf.
- The helper `describeGrokValue` names what a value is for a report line that may not print it. The helper `clipped` keeps a pattern echoed into a report line short, so the user reads the report in a transcript.
- The rules are planned from `planGrokPermissions` beside the call and not inside it, the way Codex's rules are. The list they append to is built in the dispatch, and a planner that reached for it takes the whole accumulator as a parameter.

**The MCP servers**

- The function `normalizeGrokMcp` rewrites one `[mcp_servers.<name>]` entry into this build's MCP shape. The transport is the same two shapes Codex uses, and grok reads its table untagged with `Stdio` first. So an entry with both a `command` and a `url` is a stdio server with an unused URL, exactly as it is there.
- Everything this refuses is a credential grok does not store. The keys `bearer_token_env_var`, `oauth_client_secret_env_var` and `env_http_headers` all name variables in the user's environment. The server is copied and the report says which variables it used to read. A read of them here, or an invented empty value, turns a server that works into one that fails at connect time. The source's own `oauth`, `setup`, `startup_timeout_sec`, `tool_timeout_sec`, `tool_timeouts` and `expose_image_base64` settings are named, and none has a counterpart here.
- A server grok reads as disabled is a skip, because there is no way to keep a server defined and switched off here. The keys `disabled_mcp_servers` and `disabled_mcp_tools` are named for the same reason. Tools hidden from the model on a server here are approved per project rather than listed in a file.
- A server whose `url` carries a credential is left off. The `url` is the one credential channel a name-based scan cannot reach. The token is inside the one string every importer treats as a safe identifier. It is not under a secret-shaped key. Grok supplies a credential without a stored one through `bearer_token_env_var` and `env_http_headers`, which name variables. So that is the other channel to point at here.
- There is no half to keep, because the same address with its userinfo or its `?access_token=` stripped is a different address that points at nothing. The flag `containsSecret` is `true` even so, and no `markMcpSecret` runs because no file receives it.

**The sections without a home, and the policy files**

- The sections in `GROK_UNMIGRATED_SECTIONS` get a line of their own rather than a translation. Every documented key the file can hold is there. The one thing a migration report may not do is leave a setting that looks simply passed over. The reasons are deliberately not interchangeable: each says what the section holds and what this build does instead.
- Grok's own machinery: `agent`, `auth_provider`, `harness`, `telemetry`, `privacy`, `feedback`, `endpoints`, `relay`, `storage`, `consent`, `diagnostics`, `doom_loop_recovery` and `grok_com_config`. The last is the sign-in settings for grok.com, the same table as `[auth]`, named rather than read. The key `auth_provider` names helpers that mint a bearer token for a model entry. A provider here reads its key from an environment variable instead. The key `agent` names the agent definition grok loads for the session, and agents here are files under `~/.labunbun/agents`.
- Deployment content: `announcements` and `campaigns` are payloads and patches the deployment publishes, not preferences the user wrote. The key `managed_mcps` names servers the deployment provisions at startup. The key `marketplace` names plugin marketplace sources, and the reader reads plugins here as files rather than through a subscription.
- Interface state is `dashboard`, `hints`, `ui` and `voice`. The key `dashboard` picks the panes grok's own dashboard shows, and `hints` picks the worktree offer a `/fork` or `/new` shows. The key `ui` holds grok's interface preferences, and `voice` holds dictation settings.
- The classifier mode is `auto_mode` and `default_auto_mode`, and no mode here does that. The key `default_auto_mode` starts sessions in that mode. The key `disable_web_search` drops grok's web search tool. Which tools may run here is decided by permission rules. The key `goal` names a goal applied to every session, and there is no such setting here.
- The keys `memory` and `memory_v2` are switches for grok's memory subsystem, and memory here is the rule files and `MEMORY.md` a session reads. There is no extraction pipeline to enable. The key `sandbox` holds filesystem profiles, and the shell here is gated by permission rules rather than confined by a sandbox.
- The key `session` holds the compaction threshold, which follows the model's context window here. It also holds `.envrc` injection into the shell, which this build does not do. The key `shell_environment_policy` says what grok's child shells inherit and are handed. Env in settings here is injected into this process, which is a different thing. The key `subagents` holds concurrency, depth limits and per-type model overrides, and the subagent pool here has its own defaults and no per-type model table.
- The key `tools` holds gitignore rules, media-generation caps and ZDR switches for grok's tool set. The key `toolset` holds timeouts, output limits, the file-edit scheme and web allowlists, and the tools here fix their own. The key `mcp` is the MCP client's own output cap, which the client here sets for itself. The key `path_not_found_hints` enriches path-not-found errors with suggestions, and the tools here report the error as it stands. The key `cli` holds the update channel, version pins and worktree preferences, and this build has no updater or version pin. The key `version_overrides` holds CLI version pins.
- The key `hooks` holds matcher groups with command handlers, and this importer does not translate them. A hook that guards or annotates a tool call is not in force here until it is copied by hand. The key `workflows` holds named workflows in config, and workflows here are agent definitions and skills. The key `worktree` says how grok lays out a session's worktree, and the worktrees here are git's own.
- The plan names the `[skills]` and `[paths]` entries that point outside grok. The keys `paths.extra_skill_dirs` and `paths.extra_rule_dirs` are directories grok's own Claude importer wrote, and they point at `~/.claude` and `~/.agents`. Those trees belong to the `claude-code` and `agents` sources, and an import from this source too lands a second copy of every skill. The reader reads the `[skills] paths` entries, and lists the skills under them with the rest. The importer counts and never walks the keys `server_skill_dirs` and `bundled_skill_dirs`, because they are not the user's to carry.
- The keys `skills.disabled` and `skills.ignore` shape what loads here. The config line says the switches were applied, and each removed skill is listed with the key that removed it. That half is what a user who forgot about them needs.
- The plan names the `[compat]` section, which holds grok's compatibility toggles and the trees it found. Those belong to the tools that own them, and grok's own `claude_import_state.json` shows that an earlier migration read those trees once already.
- The plan names the `[plugins]` keys as flags this importer cannot apply. The keys `disabled` and `enabled` hold plugin ids of the form `<scope>/<hash>/<name>`, and this importer cannot recompute the hash from a directory. So a plugin the user switched off there is still read and imported as the user's own files. The key `enabled` names project-scope plugins that default off. The key `paths` holds extra plugin directories that this importer does not walk. The reader reads only the two roots under `$GROK_HOME`.
- The policy files beside `config.toml` are not a second copy of the user's preferences. The file `requirements.toml` is where `disable_bypass_permissions_mode` lives, and `managed_config.toml` is pushed by an administrator. A copy puts an admin's requirement in the user's own settings file. There it stays in force as the user's own choice after the machine is no longer administered. The user never chose it. So the report names the machine-policy layer as policy rather than as a setting. A requirement copied into the user's settings outlives the policy that imposed it.
- A `configError` from the reader becomes a skip that says the settings, permission rules and MCP servers were not read.
- The dotted-key lines say a bare digits-only segment after a dot is a key path and not part of a name. Grok reads it as a table named `<leaf>` inside `<parent>`, and so does this importer. The message tells the user to quote the segment if they meant one key, and the importer takes nothing from that path either way.

**The assets planner**

- The asset planner `planGrokAssets` covers the files the user wrote, then the things this importer walks past and names. It does not use `planAssetTrees`, for the reason that helper's own doc gives in reverse. That helper renders the memory label from `SOURCE_ROOTS[source]`, which is a guess at a directory under home, and `$GROK_HOME` can be anywhere. Every path here comes from the resolved root, so a label always names a file the reader opened. The planner `planDeepSeekAssets` became a module of its own for the same reason.
- The second half matters as much as the first. The user reads that as an oversight when the importer skips a tree. The user's next move is to look for it in the target and conclude the migration was broken. So every tree that holds something and has no home here gets a line. The two credential files get one each that states outright that they stay closed.
- The skills, rules, agents and commands go through the shared writers. Rules keep their original file names, as `planClaudeCode` does. A rule is a document the user named, and a `grok-` prefix puts it in a different sort order than where they left it. The command label names each plugin's `commands/` directory when plugins contributed any.
- The report names skills the source's own config switched off as skips in `skillSkips`, with the key that switched them off.
- The importer reads the instruction document from every spelling grok reads and joins the copies into one (`GROK_INSTRUCTION_FILES`). The label names one. The line does not treat the others as absent. The label is `AGENTS.md`, and the sentence notes that grok reads `Agents.md` and `AGENT.md` too and joins whichever exist.
- The global memory document in force comes from the tree grok's own switch chooses: `memory/MEMORY.md`, or `memory-v2/global/MEMORY.md` when v2 is chosen. The other tree's document is named in a skip. Grok keeps two memory generations isolated, so exactly one is in force and a user with both keeps one of them. The decision is grok's own switch, and that is what the line states. A v2 enablement that arrives from the server makes the other document the live one instead, and this importer cannot read that arrival.
- The report counts and names workspace-scoped memory documents in `memoryWorkspaceCount`, and the importer never reads them. Each one belongs to one repository, and a rule file in this tree loads in every workspace. A carried document does not move a note but widens its scope, which is a decision for the user.
- Plugins are named as a unit this build does not have. The skills, commands and agents above are now plain files that load whenever skills do, and each line names the plugin it came from. The importer counts a plugin's `.mcp.json` and `hooks/hooks.json` and never reads them, because the files change what a session does, not what it knows. A third-party plugin's hooks do not enter a user's configuration before the user sees them.
- Grok's own shipped content and its download cache (`bundled`, `marketplace-cache`) are named only, because neither is the user's to carry. The files `lsp.json` and `pager.toml` are named the same way. Nothing here registers a language server, and this build's interface is not configured from a file.
- The file `claude_import_state.json` gets a line for its existence alone. It is grok's own record of a completed `~/.claude` import. It is the plainest evidence that an earlier migration carried the trees across. That is why this importer leaves them to the `claude-code` and `agents` sources.
- The module counts the unimported trees so their absence from the report reads as a decision. The trees are the prompt machinery of grok, memory that its agents wrote, and the scripts it runs around a session. The runtime entries are logs, caches, downloads and machine state grok writes as it runs, so they are not configuration at all.
- The two credential files get one line each, by name: `auth.json` for the account's tokens, and `mcp_credentials.json` for MCP OAuth tokens. The helper `existsSync` is the only thing either one is ever subjected to. These two lines are all the text the report gives about them. There is no size, no mtime and no content.
- A repository's own `.grok/` is deliberately outside this planner's reach. The report says so on purpose. The tree looks like this source's own, so a user who does not find it mentioned will read the silence as a bug. The permission rules in its `config.toml` are the part most worth a line. They are the one thing in there that is dangerous to move even when a reader can read the file. Those rules apply only after the user trusts the repository, and they live with the repository rather than under `$GROK_HOME`. So this importer neither reads nor moves them.

**The mode**

- The planner `planGrokPermissionMode` reads the key `[ui] permission_mode`, and the two keys that spell the same decision, `approval_mode` and `yolo`. The translation is short because grok's own is. The routine `parse_permission_mode_canonical` knows `always-approve`, `auto` and `ask`, treats `default` as a spelling of ask, and sends everything else there too.
- A user who wrote `permission_mode = "plan"` in `[ui]` expected the Claude Code vocabulary. That user ran with a mode that never auto-approves. A carried name of that kind hands the user a mode grok never applied. So this planner imports the mode grok resolved and names the string it resolved it from. That is the only translation that cannot be a lie in either direction.
- Two details of the routine `permission_mode_from_ui_if_set` decide the answer. The precedence is type-gated: `permission_mode` counts only as a string, `approval_mode` only as a string, and `yolo` only as `true`. A key present in a shape grok does not read does not decide. It falls through to the next key. The presence of any of the three pins the answer. An explicit `yolo = false` resolves to ask rather than to "unset", so an account default cannot win. That is why the importer claims a value of ask as an explicit pair here instead of a silent pass.
- One key is two axes here. The value `always-approve` becomes the pair that asks nothing and confines nothing: `agent` under the open sandbox. That pair is the one Claude Code's `bypassPermissions` already maps to in `claude-plan.ts`. The value `ask` becomes a confined pair, because grok states no confinement beside the mode. Its `[sandbox] profile` is a separate section this importer reports and does not read, so that half is this build's default. The claim line says in those words which half was chosen. The value `auto` is a classifier, and no mode here is one.
- The set `GROK_PERMISSION_MODE_NAMES` holds the names grok recognises: `always-approve`, `auto`, `ask` and `default` as an alias of ask. A name grok does not know is the case worth spelling out. The file says one thing and the session did another. The user is the only one who can say which of the two they want from here on.
- The value `auto` becomes a skip: it is a classifier that approves the calls it judges safe, and no mode here is one. The value `ask` puts a person in the loop instead of a classifier, and the value `agent` runs everything instead. Either choice changes the posture the mode names, so the session stays with what it starts in otherwise.
- The other spellings grok reads past are named only when one of them changes the decision. A second key that says the same thing is not worth a line. The key `yolo` counts only as the boolean `true`, and the key `permission_mode` only as a string. A value in another shape falls through to the next key instead.

**The handled-key sets**

- The set `GROK_CONFIG_HANDLED` lists the `config.toml` keys that are imported above or named by a line of their own. They are `model`, `models`, `model_providers`, `permission`, `mcp_servers`, `disabled_mcp_servers`, `disabled_mcp_tools`, `skills`, `paths`, `compat`, `plugins` and `ui`, plus every key of `GROK_UNMIGRATED_SECTIONS`. Anything else reaches the report through `reportUnhandledKeys`, so a key the user set always appears somewhere.
- The list `GROK_COMPACT_KEYS` pairs each compact key with the action it carries, in the order grok reads them: `deny`, `allow`, `ask`.

### MiniMax Code

**The layout, and the two history generations**

- MiniMax Code keeps a session as one dated directory under `<root>/v2/sessions`. One session directory holds a `manifest.json`, the history files, and the `snapshots/` and `reports/` directories beside them.
- The history comes in two generations, and a reader has to know both. The current one is `messages.jsonl`: one canonical envelope per line, `{message_id, turn_id, message, turn_config?, history_artifact?}`, written by `CanonicalHistoryJsonlDataSource`. The released one is `snapshot.json` plus an append-only `ledger.jsonl` of `message.*` events. The current build replays that pair and then materializes it into `messages.jsonl` the first time it opens the session. A machine whose sessions were all opened since then holds only the first form. A machine that has not run that build holds only the second.
- The canonical file is a context, not a journal, and that is the single most important thing about it. When MiniMax compacts, it replaces the file's contents. The new first record is a `compactionSummary`, or, in the older Archon spelling, a user message that carries an `archonCompaction` marker. The messages before it are not in the file any more. So there is nothing to rebuild, but there is something to say: the import starts with a compaction marker. A reader of it can see that the conversation came from an earlier one rather than a fresh start. A migration that drops the marker presents a compacted session as a complete one.
- The shapes: `MinimaxListing` carries the sessions and a notes list of `{reason, count}`. `MinimaxEntry` is a message or a compaction marker with its summary and `preTokens`. `MinimaxSessionFile` carries the id, the paths, the history kind, and the row fields. `cwd` is `""` when only the metadata row knew it and the row is gone. `startedAt` is the manifest's `createdAtMs`, which the tool refuses to work without. `kind` and `sessionType` and `parentSessionId` are `""` with no row.

**The read path, step for step**

- The read follows the tool's own read path (`readSnapshot`, `canonical-history-provider.ts:321-353`), a two-step answer that this module reproduces step for step. The difference between the steps is the difference between a refusal and an import.
- Step one: decode strictly. `readEnvelopesStrict` is `readJsonl` with no observer. A malformed line is fatal rather than skipped, and the envelope decoder is exact about the envelope's keys. A file that fails here is refused whole, with the line named. Half a conversation is worse than a reported failure, because the absent half is invisible.
- Step two: repair the tool protocol, refuse the rest. `repairCanonicalHistory` (`canonical-history-recovery.ts:57-84`) drops exactly the tool rows whose disposition is deterministic. Those are an orphan result, a result that does not follow its call, a call with no identity, and an interrupted round. Everything else stays. `assertRecoveryInvariants` still fails closed on "ambiguous identity, compaction, and turn-config corruption". This reader draws the line in the same place. An identity used twice refuses the session, and so does a boundary that is not the first record. A `turn_config` on the wrong message refuses it, and so does a `history_artifact` past the first envelope. The tool pairs go to `@labunbun/ai`'s `repairToolPairing`, whose verdict, not just its count, is what this module returns.
- A final assistant message whose tool results never arrived is kept, the tool's own answer for a read. `allowPendingToolCallTail` retains the final in-flight round and reports no issue for it. A read that is part of a run removes it. A transcript is a read, and the text in that message belongs to the user.
- What this reader deliberately does not read follows. `display.jsonl` and the SQLite rows behind it are the UI's views of the conversation, with compaction lifecycle and review frames mixed in. The tool falls back to them only after both file sources are gone. A migration that raises a transcript from `display.jsonl` imports the UI's view of a conversation as the conversation. `snapshots/` and `reports/` are the durable pre-compaction artifacts that `history_artifact` points at, not conversation. `v2/chats` holds the pre-`v2` draft ledgers, which the tool's own migrator moves into `v2/sessions` before a read as history. Unsent composer drafts (`v2/mcode/drafts`) are text the user never sent.
- The SQLite database is opened read-only and only for metadata: title, project directory, archived flag and internal kind. The walk over the manifests decides which sessions exist, exactly as the tool's own discovery does. Nothing here writes anything, and no file outside the sessions tree and that database is opened.

**The walk, the rows and the sort**

- `INTERNAL_SESSION_KINDS` holds `peek`, `task`, `channel` and `cron`. `eligible()` in `projects/sidebar/predicate.ts:30-56` is the sidebar's own filter. It is the closest thing the product has to a definition of a conversation the user had. The test is: no parent session, not archived, not hidden, and a kind that is not one of these three. `cron` joins them from the shared list default (`sessions/query/query-service.ts:236-238`).
- The walk is the authority on what exists, and it is the walk the tool's own discovery makes. `listManifestPaths` descends exactly four levels (`session-history-location.ts:186-202`). The manifest is what proves a directory is a session. A directory whose JSON does not state a string `sessionId` and a safe-integer `createdAtMs` belongs to nothing this reader can name. The reader counts it rather than guesses.
- The metadata row is a lookup, never the authority. It answers for the fields the manifest does not carry: title, project directory and archived flag. It also answers for the three the tool's own list filters on. The notes state what that costs. When the reader cannot open the database at all, it lists every walked session, as they are real, and it classifies none of them. A hidden or internal session is then imported rather than skipped. When the database is readable and one row is absent, that session is listed and counted. A directory with history in it is a conversation whatever the absent row means.
- The importer counts a session directory whose history files are both gone and skips it. A manifest-only session has nothing to import.
- The sort follows the tool's own list: `updated_at_ms DESC`, and the session id breaks ties (`idx_local_runtime_sessions_recency_v2`). A caller's history limit then means the sessions the user last used.
- `readManifest` reads `<dir>/manifest.json`, or null when it is not a manifest this reader can name. `isManifestIdentity` is the tool's own guard (`session-history-location.ts:334-342`). It wants a session id that is a string and a creation time that is a safe integer, and nothing else. The layout and the paths block are a convenience. A manifest whose layout and paths block are wrong still names the session it is for.
- `locateHistory` follows the tool's own priority, `canonical > legacy > manifest` (`candidateHistoryKind`, `session-history-location.ts:271-283`). That priority comes from which files exist rather than from the manifest. The manifest lists every path whether or not the file was ever written. `messages.jsonl` therefore wins when it is there, and the released pair answers when it is not.
- `readSessionRows` states the discipline. `new Database(path)` creates a database that is not there. So the file is checked before it is opened, and the handle is closed in a `finally`. That is the discipline `zcode-db.ts` already sets out for the other SQLite source. Every failure becomes no metadata, never an aborted migration: an absent file, a locked database, a schema newer than this query. The sessions are still there and still importable. They just cannot be titled, scoped or classified. A row half-read before a failure is not metadata, and nothing is claimed.

**The canonical decode**

- `readCanonical` follows `readJsonl`'s line rules. One empty line at the end is the writer's final newline and is dropped, and every other blank line is malformed. `parseJsonLine` is a plain `JSON.parse`. The tool replaces the parse error with a reason and does not quote the line back. A history line holds whatever the user typed, so this reader does the same and names the line number only.
- The envelope's keys are exact, the tool's own `ENVELOPE_KEYS`: `message_id`, `turn_id`, `message`, `turn_config` and `history_artifact`. A turn configuration may carry `TURN_CONFIG_KEYS`: `system_prompt`, `model` and `tools`.
- `decodeEnvelope` is `decodeCanonicalHistoryEnvelope` (`canonical-history-jsonl.ts:308-338`), and two of its properties decide how much this reader trusts. The envelope's keys are exact, and an unknown key is not ignored: it means a writer this reader has not seen. The three named keys are required, and the two optional ones are decoded to the shape that makes them recognizable. A record that cannot be accounted for is refused rather than imported. The message is not exact, and deliberately so. `decodeMessage` reads `role` and `timestamp` and spreads the rest through as written: content, usage, stopReason, provider, model and errorMessage. That is where a transcript lives, so the tolerance is the point.
- Beyond the shape there are four rules. `message_id` must match `msg-.+` or hold only digits. `turn_id` must be a non-empty string. `turn_config` is allowed only on user messages, and `message.timestamp` must be finite unless the role is `compactionSummary`.
- One place is deliberately looser than the tool. Since the reader carries none of a `turn_config`'s own body across, it reads only as far as the test above. A record whose config is malformed but whose message is intact is imported with the config dropped and counted. The tool's strict reader refuses such a file. Its tolerant reader drops the whole line. Both readings exist upstream, and this is the one that loses less of a real conversation.

**The compaction boundary, and the two Archon forms**

- `checkCompactionSummary` is `decodeCompactionSummary`: a minimal `{role, summary}`, or the full form that also states how large the context was, `{role, summary, tokensBefore, timestamp}`. The two come together, and the minimal form may carry nothing else. `tokensBefore` must be a non-negative safe integer, and `timestamp` must be finite.
- `compactionBoundary` is `isCompactionBoundary` (`canonical-history-jsonl.ts:1056-1076`), which accepts the native summary and the two Archon spellings and refuses a marker it cannot read. Both Archon forms are still on disk. `schemaVersion: 2` is the generation form: a `generation`, and a `parentSnapshot` that names the generation it replaced, which must be exactly one less. `version: 2` is the recap form: the summary, the recent user queries it was built from, and the todo list it kept. A query carries a text and an optional timestamp. A todo item carries content, status and priority. Anything else with a `summary` string is `schemaVersion: 1`, the original marker, which carried no structure worth a check.
- `isHistoryArtifact` shapes the marker on the first envelope of a context the tool produced by compaction. It is generation n plus one, recorded with the parent snapshot's id and revision so the line of descent can be checked. None of it is carried into the import, because the compaction marker already says a context was replaced. A file whose lineage is malformed is a file the tool refuses, so it is refused here. The revision must match `sha256:` and 64 hex digits, and the parent generation must be exactly the generation minus one.
- `validateSequence` is `assertRecoveryInvariants` (`canonical-history-recovery.ts:86-128`). The tool runs the sequence validator over the records with every tool-protocol row projected away. Tool calls are stripped from assistant content, and tool results are rewritten as empty assistant messages. It then checks tool-call identity on its own. What survives that projection is exactly what is checked here, because everything else has a deterministic repair.
- The rules: an identity used twice makes a message unaddressable. A tool call identity used twice makes a result ambiguous. A compaction boundary that is not the unique first record contradicts the file's own layout, because a compaction replaces the file. A `turn_config` that is not on the first user message of its turn drifted off the message it describes. It is a system prompt, and on the wrong turn it changes what the model was told. A `history_artifact` past the first envelope claims a compaction the records around it do not show.
- `assistantToolCallIds` skips a block without an identity, because that is a repair (`invalid-assistant-tool-call`) and not an ambiguity.

**The released history: snapshot and ledger**

- `readLegacy` follows `released-session-history-reader.ts:68-119`. `snapshot.json` is the state at a watermark, and `ledger.jsonl` is everything after it. The read is: the snapshot's `piHistory` first, then each event. An append adds, a replace or a retracted turn swaps the whole list, and a delete empties it. Events are sorted by `seq` and deduplicated. Duplicates that are the same are dropped, and a conflict is fatal. The tail after the watermark must extend the sequence with no gap. A half-written last line is cut before the parse, because an append that died mid-write is not a corrupt ledger.
- The snapshot is a cache, and that is the part worth a plain statement. When it is corrupt, or its tail will not read, the tool does not give up on the session. It replays the complete ledger from sequence one and fails only when that fails too or holds no conversation record at all. The same is done here, because the ledger is the record and the snapshot is derived from it.
- A session with both files and no conversation in either answers with an empty transcript rather than an error. Nothing is wrong with it. It just never had a conversation.
- `readLegacySnapshot` acceptance rules are the tool's own guards (`released-session-history-reader.ts:353-397`). The snapshot needs `schemaVersion: 1` and the session id this reader asked for. It needs a non-empty `snapshotId` and a finite `createdAtMs`. Its watermark must name the same session with a non-negative `lastSeq` and a byte offset that is either absent or a non-negative integer. Its payload must hold `displayMessages` and `piHistory` as arrays, with a boolean `piHistoryFacts` and `deleted`. A snapshot that says it was deleted carries no messages.
- `readLedger`: the tool seeks to the watermark's byte offset, because a live session may append while a turn starts. A migration has no such reader, so the import reads the whole file and drops the events at or below the watermark afterwards. The watermark's consistency is still checked. One that points past the end of the ledger is a state the tool refuses to read and recovers from, so it recovers here too.
- An absent ledger is not an error and not a cause for recovery, whatever the snapshot holds. The tool's `readLegacyLedgerTail` answers `undefined` for `ENOENT`, and its caller reads that as no events (`?? []`, `released-session-history-reader.ts:62-71, 154-161`). A session whose ledger was never written, or was cleaned up, is replayed from its snapshot alone.
- `committedLedgerText` cuts a half-written last line before the parse. `PI_HISTORY_FACTS` holds the kinds that decide whether a session had a conversation at all: `message.pi_history_appended`, `message.pi_history_replaced`, `message.turn_retracted`, `message.state_deleted` and `session.deleted`. `isAuthoritative` says a session is one only when something in it says so.
- `recoverFromCompleteLedger` wants a sequence that starts at one and is unbroken. The ledger must hold at least one conversation record, or there is nothing to recover. The failure sentence carries the cause and the failed recovery.

**Legacy entries as canonical records**

- `legacyRecords` handles two kinds of entry. A legacy entry is either a bare pi message or an envelope an earlier generation wrote. `isCanonicalEnvelopeShape` is `message_id` and `turn_id` and a message. The two are told apart by those keys. A bare message has neither identity nor turn, so both are derived the way the tool derives them. The turn id is the record's own, or the sequence of the event it came from. A snapshot has no events, so its records take a count that opens a turn at every user message and at the first one. The message id is the message's own `msg_id`/`id`/`messageId` when it is spelled as a canonical id may be. Otherwise it is a digest of the record's own bytes, stable for the same input. A re-run of the migration then produces the same transcript rather than a second set of identities.
- Every one of these records then goes through the canonical decoder, which is what the tool does too (`legacyEnvelope`). A legacy message that cannot be decoded fails the session in the tool's own migration too. So it is a refusal here rather than a message imported with a guessed shape.
- `legacyNotAMessage` reuses the decoder's refusal for an entry that is not a message at all. The tool does not skip such an entry. `legacyEnvelope` hands it to the canonical decoder whatever it is. The decoder refuses anything that is not a plain object ("message must be a plain object"). That refusal fails the whole read. A skip here imports a transcript the tool cannot open, and does so without a report.
- `normalizeLegacyEntry` is `normalizeLegacyHistoryTimestamp`: a legacy stamp made into the finite number the canonical shape needs. Legacy messages came from a generation that allowed a string stamp (`legacy-history-timestamp.ts:8-19, 68-75`), and the canonical decoder rejects one. The fallback is the time of the thing the record came from, the snapshot's `createdAtMs` or the event's. A record with neither is left alone, and the decoder refuses it. This reader does not make up a time.
- `legacyMessageId` takes a legacy message's own id when it has one in a spelling a canonical id may take. `timestampMs` reads epoch ms from a stamp written either way, and answers `undefined` when it is not a time. `eventRecords` gives the turn a legacy event's messages belong to. That is its own `turnId` when it has one, and the sequence otherwise. A message with no turn still gets a stable and obviously derived identity. Only the three message kinds reach it, and each is about a list of messages. One without a list is a ledger the tool refuses too.

**Records to messages**

- `foldRecords` turns the records into the target's own messages. The translation is close to an identity, and the differences are the whole content of the function. `arguments` becomes JSON text. A block of type `thinking` loses its signature. A compacted context becomes a marker instead of a message. The importer counts every record that is not a message the model saw and leaves it out. That covers the harness's own turn configuration, a role this build does not carry, and a call or a result with no identity. The target's `Usage` has no `cost` and no `totalTokens`. Both are derived numbers in the source and are dropped.
- The validator has already established that a boundary is the first record and that there is at most one. A compaction replaces the context, so the marker stands where the conversation now starts.
- `compactionSummary` reads the summary of a boundary record in whichever of its spellings it was written. The first is the native `summary`, and the second is the Archon marker's `summary`. A generation marker need not carry one. Then the text of the user message it is attached to answers, because the context was rebuilt around that text. `compactionTokens` answers how large the context was before it was compacted, and zero when the source did not say.
- `toMessage` maps the `user`, `assistant` and `toolResult` roles, and counts anything else.
- `assistantRecord` carries the blocks, the provider, the model, the four usage buckets, the stop reason, an optional `errorMessage` and the timestamp. An image or video the assistant produced is content the target cannot re-request, and a URL is not an image until it is fetched. Counted, not fetched, and never carried as a placeholder the model reads as fact.
- `thinkingContent` handles a redacted block, whose payload lives in the signature field and belongs to the provider that issued it. A signature replayed against a provider that did not issue it is the first thing an API rejects. So every reader here counts such a block and leaves it out, and this one does the same. Text is carried without its signature for the same reason. The target has a `signature` field, and an empty one is honest where another provider's blob is not.
- `toolCall`: pi stores `arguments` as a JSON object and the target stores the text of one, because its adapters never parse partial JSON. A string is already text, which is how a writer stores arguments it cannot parse. An absent or unencodable value becomes `{}`, which is what a call with no parameters takes. A call with no identity is dropped, the tool's own repair of an `invalid-assistant-tool-call`. A call the target cannot name is a call whose result it cannot match, and the request is rejected outright.
- `toolResultRecord` drops a result that names no call, because the target refuses a transcript that holds one. Content is text and images, and a result with no content gets the placeholder "(no output recorded)".
- `imageContent` carries an image block when it holds its bytes rather than a place to fetch them. pi stores the base64 payload inline, and anything else, a URL or a file path, must be fetched. A transcript is not the place to start network I/O on the user's behalf. An image with no inline data is counted.
- `stopReasonOf`: pi writes five of the target's seven reasons, so those cross over as they are. The same holds for the two the target added and a newer writer may already record. Anything else is from a vocabulary this build has not been taught: it is counted, and the answer comes from the content instead. A tool call block means `toolUse`, and otherwise `stop`. That is the derivation the readers of every source that records no reason use.
- `readMinimaxSession` reads one session end to end and returns `{ error }` when it cannot read the transcript as a whole. The module header says where that line is drawn and why.
- `usageOf` keeps the four token buckets the two builds share. `stableJson` gives key-order-stable JSON, so two spellings of one event can be compared. `safeJson` gives JSON text for a value whose exact spelling the target will parse again. `bump` counts one reason, and the notes come out in first-seen order.

**The product, and its three directory names**

- MiniMax Code is `mcode`, the npm package `@minimax-ai/code`, and its internal codename is `mavis`. Its directory has three historical names, and only one of them is live. The current default is `~/.minimax` (`packages/config/src/data-dir.ts:5-6`, `docs/installation.md:75-90`).
- The older name is `~/.mavis`, the same tree renamed into `~/.minimax` by `resolveDataDirPair`, the first time a build that knows the new name starts. A refused rename leaves a junction link instead (`packages/config/src/data-dir.ts:5-6, 287-382`).
- Its older variable name, `$MAVIS_DATA_DIR`, is still read after `$MINIMAX_DATA_DIR`, as an override of the very same tree. So both names point at one directory, and the only question about them is which one is live on this machine.
- This is not a second product's tree, unlike `kimi-cli`'s `~/.kimi`, which is a different tool's directory. It is this tool's own history. A migration that read `.mavis` without a link test imports one tree twice.
- The third name, `~/.minimax-code`, is where the macOS, Linux and WSL installer unpacks the npm package (`MCODE_INSTALL_DIR` changes it). It was also the data directory of earlier source builds, which the current default neither moves nor merges (`docs/installation.md:85-87`).
- A migration that read `.minimax-code` reads the tool's program files rather than sessions, so the name is reported and never resolved into a read. Named here so a report can say why a tree full of program files was not read as sessions.
- Credentials live inside the same directory: `auth/`, `credentials/`, `cli-auth/`, `codex-auth.json`, `local-runtime.auth.json`, and BYOK keys in `config.yaml`.
- So the reader works from an allow-list of paths it knows how to parse, and never from a walk of everything under the root. Every credential path is named for the report and never opened.
- Everything here computes strings, and the one legacy question that cannot be answered from a name alone stats a path. Nothing is created. No file outside the caller's own data directory is read, except to answer whether a legacy directory is a link to the current one.

**The state tree, and the two spellings of the root**

- The rule decode lives in this reader rather than in the planner, because it is a read problem and not a decision. MiniMax writes a rule as an escaped string, and the string has to come apart before anything is planned from it.
- MiniMax Code keeps one tree, in one of three places: `$MINIMAX_DATA_DIR`, else `$MAVIS_DATA_DIR`, else `<home>/.minimax`. The `minimaxRoot` rule owns that, and it trims the value and refuses to expand `~`.
- The predecessor tree `<home>/.mavis` is a second question rather than a second source: it is the same tree under its older name. `minimaxLegacyDataDir` answers "separate directory, or a link to the one we already have" the way the vendor answers it.
- Which tree the reader ends up with follows the vendor's own resolution (`resolveDataDirPair`, `packages/config/src/data-dir.ts:286-382`). Its move of the legacy tree is a rename and never a merge. Two trees on one machine are one tree's worth of decisions plus a backup of the other.
- The test that decides is a content test and not an existence test. An `.minimax` that is present but empty is exactly the state that moves `.mavis` over it (`:310-330`). An existence test reports the source as empty while its data sits under the older name.
- Three corners come from that function rather than from a guess. A legacy tree answers only when it is a directory or a link to one. A plain file at `.mavis` is never read and is only renamed onto (`:306-308` and `:342`). A primary directory this process cannot list is not empty and does not fall back (`:311-315`). The one pair that falls back to nothing is "empty here, empty there", and there the vendor keeps the current name (`:318-321`).
- An explicit `$MINIMAX_DATA_DIR` skips the pair entirely, which is why the fallback is open to the default path alone.
- The `legacyRead` flag is true when the rules above produced `<root>` from the legacy tree. The vendor renames that tree into `.minimax` on its next start. On a machine where a current build never ran, it is the only tree there is.

**The root's rules, and the profile**

- The `minimaxRoot` value is trimmed, and an all-whitespace value is unset (`process.env.MINIMAX_DATA_DIR?.trim() || process.env.MAVIS_DATA_DIR?.trim()`, `packages/config/src/config.ts:1316-1319`). The TUI resolves it the same way (`readDataDirOverride`, `packages/tui/src/runtime/data-dir.ts:36-45`).
- This is the opposite of the sources beside it, where `$KIMI_CODE_HOME` is used verbatim and `$DSH_HOME` counts pure whitespace as a value.
- The audit this module was written from describes the value as used `as-is`, and the source disagrees. The source wins: `  /data/mcode  ` names `/data/mcode`, and a value of `"   "` names nothing and leaves `$MAVIS_DATA_DIR` to decide.
- A `~` at the start is not expanded, and a relative value is not resolved. The vendor returns the value from `getExplicitDataDirArg` to its callers unchanged. So `~/elsewhere` names a directory literally called `~` under the process's own directory, exactly as `$KIMI_CODE_HOME` does. A reader that resolved it reads a tree nobody wrote.
- The newer name wins even when both variables are set, because the two are one setting with two names. The shipped build documents the newer name (`docs/installation.md:83-84`).
- Three sources of the value are deliberately not read. The `--data-dir` and `--profile` flags (`cliArgValue`, `config.ts:1220-1228`) are the argv of a live process. The internal `__MAVIS_RUNTIME_DATA_DIR` and `__MAVIS_RUNTIME_PROFILE` are its environment, read by the vendor only when `__MAVIS_ALLOW_LEGACY_RUNTIME_ENV === "1"` (`config.ts:84, 1214-1219`). This module reads neither.
- Git auto-detection (`detectGitPortInfo`, `REPO_NAMES` at `config.ts:1247`) fires only inside the vendor's repositories, and it picks a per-branch profile. That is a tree this module can name, but it cannot detect one without the same questions put to git.
- The profile root is `<home>/.minimax-<profile>`. The vendor builds the name as `base + "-" + profile` with no separator of its own (`packages/config/src/data-dir.ts:36-38`). A profile reaches the tool as `--profile` or through the internal runtime environment. The branch-isolated profile of a git auto-detected run has `/` replaced by `-` (`config.ts:1400-1420`).
- An empty profile is the unscoped tree rather than a directory named `.minimax-`.

**The legacy pair, and the content test**

- The `minimaxLegacyDataDir` function answers whether `<home>/.mavis` is a different tree from `<home>/.minimax`. The vendor's own migration renames `.mavis` into `.minimax`, and the rename can leave a link behind. That link points at the new directory (`createCompatLink`, `data-dir.ts:83-90`).
- So on a machine where any build of this tool ran once, the old name is not a second tree. It is the same tree under both names, and an import from both imports every session twice.
- The function answers the way the tool answers. It resolves the link, compares the resolved paths, and calls it a separate tree only when the comparison fails.
- A `null` means do not read this path. A returned path is a `.mavis` directory that still holds its own data. The vendor left this machine alone so far, so a read of it is exactly the read the vendor makes. A later rename by the vendor moves that data to the same place.
- The comparison is the module's one filesystem touch, and it is a `stat`, not a read. Nothing inside either directory is opened.
- The `sameDirectory` function follows a link at the candidate. It canonicalises with `resolve` and strips the Windows `\\?\` prefix. It folds case on `win32`, and accepts either an absolute link target or one relative to the link's own directory (`data-dir.ts:61-81`). Both spellings appear in the wild, and `fs.symlinkSync` writes the relative one.
- The `MinimaxDataState` type is the content test MiniMax itself applies before it moves a legacy tree in. A directory with no entries, or with nothing but an empty `workspace/`, counts as empty (`contentState`, `packages/config/src/data-dir.ts:110-125`).
- The reader reads through a link, because that is what the vendor does with one. A legacy link's content is its target's (`linkTargetDirectoryContentState`, `data-dir.ts:301-308`), and a primary link never gets a content test of its own (`:372`). So the question whether MiniMax finds data here has the same answer on either side of a link.
- The `other` state is a path that is neither a directory nor a link to one. The vendor migrates onto it and never reads it as data. The `unknown` state is a directory this process cannot list, which the vendor refuses to treat as empty (`:311-315`), and neither does this. The `missing` state is also what a path this process cannot stat returns, and the caller treats it as a path with no data.

**The paths, and what each one is for**

- The user-global instruction document is `<root>/AGENTS.md`, and only the global file has one spelling (`join(dataDir, 'AGENTS.md')`, `static-prompt-reader.ts:99,109`).
