A project file is read as `CLAUDE.md` first and `AGENTS.md` second (`static-prompt-reader.ts:124`, where the first name is spelled with hex escapes). That is a rule about a project's own directory rather than about this tree, and the caller that walks projects owns it.
- The sessions root, `<root>/v2/sessions`, holds one dated directory per session, four levels deep: `<year>/<month>/<day>/<HH-mm-ss-SSS>-session_<base64url(sessionId)>/`. The four-level shape is the tool's own: its discovery walks exactly these depths (`listManifestPaths`, `session-history-location.ts:186-202`). Its writer refuses a relative directory that is not four non-empty segments (`validateRelativeDir`, `session-history-paths.ts:120-139`).
- The timestamp segment is UTC for every session the current build creates, and local time for the earlier `v1` writer. So nothing here parses the directory name back into an id. The `manifest.json` states `sessionId` and `createdAtMs`, and that is the only source that survives both writers.
- The live database is `<root>/v2/sqlite/runtime-state.sqlite`, and it is the only place a session's title, project directory, archived flag and internal kind are written. The manifest carries identity and paths and nothing else, and it is written once, when the session directory is created. The row in `local_runtime_sessions` is the record the sidebar reads (`projects/sidebar/predicate.ts:30-56`).
- A reader may open it read-only for that metadata, and it must not create it. Every entry point checks for the file first.
- The drafts directory `<root>/v2/mcode/drafts` is unsent composer text rather than history. `resolveTuiDraftRecoveryPath` (`composer/draft-recovery.ts:498-513`) keys a draft by `sha256(resolve(workspaceDir)).slice(0, 24)` and, per session, by `sha256(normalizeSessionKey(sessionKey)).slice(0, 24)` under that workspace's own directory. A draft is text the user never sent, so an import puts words in their mouth in a session that has no turn for them.
- The user's own MiniMax skills live in `<root>/skills`. The tool also reads three borrowed skill trees, `~/.claude/skills`, `~/.codex/skills` and `~/.agents/skills` at user scope and `.claude/skills` and `.agents/skills` beside a project (`packages/local-runtime/src/skills/roots.ts:60-125`, `skills-config.ts:1-25`). They are copied from, not owned: those trees belong to the sources this migrator already has, and an import here imports every skill twice. Two more roots inside this tree are not the user's either. One is `skills` under an agent directory, and the other is the builtin assets seeded into `<root>/.builtin-skills` (`skills/roots.ts:19-22`).
- The user-level MCP servers live in `<root>/mcp.json`, and it is the only file MiniMax connects from. The runtime's own path is `join(dataDir, 'mcp.json')` (`filePath`, `mcp/runtime/local-mcp.service.ts:1027`).
- The reader reads `<root>/mcp/mcp.json` beside it, but not as a second source of connections. The older spelling fills only the set of configured names, for the deprecated feature that resolves skill references to MCP tools (`readConfiguredMcpServerNames`, `mcp/runtime/config-file.ts:6-11`). Its one caller is `local-runtime/src/api/host.ts:945`.
- So a reader that found only the alias must name it rather than pass it off as this file. A reader that takes servers out of it must say what the source itself does with them.

**The documents: `config.yaml` and the two `mcp.json` files**

- The `config.yaml` error is a fixed phrase rather than the parser's message. The parser's message can quote the line it choked on, and that line is often `apiKey`. The same reason and the same words hold in the harness reader.
- Both MCP files are read: `<root>/mcp.json` and the older spelling `<root>/mcp/mcp.json`. Unlike kimi's bare `{name: server}` map, both files are the `{"mcpServers": {…}}` wrapper this build's own `.mcp.json` uses, so the values travel as they stand.
- The reader reads both no matter what the first file holds. A name in the second that the first does not define is a server the user wrote and never sees again.
- Each parse failure is reported against its own path. A report that names `mcp.json` for a broken `mcp/mcp.json` sends the user to a file this run never opened.
- Which of the two answers is the planner's decision, because it depends on what MiniMax does with each.

**The rules file, and the store the source itself refuses**

- The `permission.json` document holds the user's allow, deny and ask decisions in one of two generations (`local-runtime/src/permissions/rule-codec.ts`). The v1 generation is a Claude-Code-shaped `{allow, deny, ask}` of `Tool(pattern)` strings. The v2 generation is `{version: 2, allow, deny, ask}` of `{tool_name, matcher}` records, and a matcher is `tool`, `command` or `path`. Only the `path` matcher carries an `actions` list, because the other two are already scoped by the tool.
- The two engines are close relatives. MiniMax's v1 grammar is Claude Code's, and this build's `Tool(specifier)` grammar is the same family. So a rule carries as text with its tool name translated. The exceptions are the rules the two engines do not agree on. Its `notCarried` list names them one by one rather than leave them to be discovered.
- Every way the file can be unusable is a refusal over there rather than a partial read. A corrupt document, a root that is not an object, and one malformed v2 entry all raise `LocalPermissionStoreUnhealthyError` (`local-runtime/src/permissions/rules.ts:214-262`). So does any explicit `version` other than two. The callers turn that into "ask about everything" rather than "allow everything".
- "v1" is the shape without a `version` key rather than the shape that spells `1`. The test `record.version === 2` picks the v2 decoder and `!== undefined` is the refusal, so what is left is v1. So a file that writes `version: 1` is refused over there as well, which is the distinction a hand-edited file gets wrong.
- The comment at the first of those refusals says what the callers do with it: `Treating it as an empty rule set could drop a persisted deny and silently authorize a command`. The store is treated as unhealthy and the user is asked. A rule out of such a file is a decision in force nowhere. An import of the allows out of it puts permissions into effect that the user's own tool refuses to honour.
- The `ask` list is validated before it is counted, because that is what the source does. It is carried as a count rather than a rule, because this build's settings hold allow and deny only. The `configV2ToRules` function maps all three behaviors through the same reader (`rule-codec.ts:143-153`). So a malformed `ask` entry makes MiniMax refuse the store exactly as a malformed `allow` entry does. A count before validation reports `N ask rules here have no equivalent` for a store the source reads as empty.

**The rule decode: names, patterns, and two reasons**

- A tool name in a MiniMax rule is not the name this build's engine knows for the same tool. MiniMax's rule strings are Claude Code's syntax (`parseRuleString`, `rule-codec.ts:166-181`), and this build's `Tool(specifier)` is the same shape. MiniMax's names are lower case and this build's are not. A rule that names a tool that does not exist where this build's engine reads it is a rule that silently does nothing. The `MINIMAX_TOOL_NAMES` table holds the translation.
- The `action` of a tool is the capability MiniMax assigns to it (`permissionInputAction`, `rule-match.ts:78-85`). The capabilities are `read`, `write`, `delete`, `execute` and `network` (`rule-codec.ts:44-51`). The reader reads it only for `path` matchers, the one matcher kind that carries its own `actions` list. The `fs` name is the umbrella MiniMax puts over the file tools. It expands to every one of them whose action the rule asks for.
- This build consults a specifier through `inputMatchesSpecifier` (`packages/agent/src/permissions.ts:213-245`). It has a case for `Bash`, one for the file tools and one for `mcp__…`, and a default that answers `false`. A rule for any other name in that position is written to no effect: the file holds it and nothing reads it. That is the one outcome a migration must not produce quietly, which is what `MINIMAX_SPECIFIER_TOOLS` names.
- A rule string is split the way MiniMax's reader splits it. The spellings are `Tool(pattern)`, `Tool`, or a bare name, and an escaped `(` does not open a pattern. The record of a rule that did not come across spells it as the source did (`splitMinimaxRuleString`, `formatMinimaxRule`).
- One entry of either generation decodes in one of three ways.
- The rule carries. Its tool name is translated and its pattern is kept as written. The two grammars agree on the shapes that matter: a `/**` subtree, an exact path, and a `*`-suffixed command prefix. One exception follows below.
- The rule is left behind because MiniMax does not consult it either, and then nothing is lost. A name no MiniMax tool has is one case (`permissionToolMatches`, `rule-match.ts:87-91`, is an exact comparison). A `path` matcher whose `actions` exclude the only action that tool does is the other (`:63-67`).
- The rule is left behind though MiniMax does consult it, and then something is lost. This engine reads a specifier for four tool names and the MCP family. The rule `glob(/etc/**)` is live in MiniMax, where a glob's target is the pattern it was given. The same rule sits unread here. This one is a real loss, and the direction of the loss depends on the behavior.
- The exception is a Bash pattern that ends in `:*`. MiniMax reads that suffix as its own word-boundary command prefix (`matchesCommandPrefix`, `rule-match.ts:141-148`), a shape this build's engine does not have. A Bash specifier here is a glob over the whole command line (`packages/agent/src/permissions.ts:213-222`).
- To keep the rule as written is to keep a rule that does nothing here. That is worse than a rule that means something slightly different. A rule like `Bash(sed:*)` reads as the glob `sed:*`, which matches no command a user ever runs. A deny written that way protects nothing after the migration.
- So the rule is rewritten to `Bash(sed*)`, the closest one-rule equivalent the two engines both understand. The rewrite is recorded in `widened`, where the report picks it up. The two ways that is wider are named in the report rather than left in the code.
- What the decoder does not try to reproduce is the matcher kind of a v1 entry. Over there the kind is inferred from the shape of the pattern. A `/`, `~/` or `./` at the start of a pattern, or a drive letter, makes it a path matcher. Anything else makes it a command matcher (`inferLegacyMatcher`, `rule-codec.ts:276-292`). The two kinds read the pattern differently. A reproduction of the guess here means this build decides what the user meant by a pattern MiniMax already guessed at. So the pattern travels as written. The caveat the caller writes for the rules it does carry says the two grammars differ.

**The borrowed trees**

- `readExternalUserSkillRoots` (`local-runtime/src/skills/roots.ts:129-137`) adds Claude Code's, Codex's and the shared agents home's skill directories to MiniMax's own skill search. The `DEFAULT_SKILLS_CONFIG` list (`packages/config/src/skills-config.ts:62-76`) has all three on. On a default install MiniMax already shows the user those trees.
- They are borrowed rather than MiniMax's. The `claude-code`, `codex` and `agents` sources each own one of them, and an import here as well puts every skill in the collection twice.
- The `skills.external` switch is on by default and so is each of its sources. A source switched off in `config.yaml` is a user decision this reader honours. To name a tree as borrowed when the user switched it off is to tell them something about their own config that is not true.
- A source is named by its current key or by the spelling it had before the rename. The current one wins when both are written. The `parseSkillsConfig` function looks for `user-cc` first and falls back to `user-claude` (`LEGACY_SOURCE_KEY_BY_KIND`, `skills-config.ts:41-46, 110-121`).
- To miss the legacy spelling is the exact mistake this function exists to avoid. A user who switched the source off under the old name is told it is on. A value that is not a table leaves the default in place, which is how the source reads it (`parseSource`, `skills-config.ts:96-101`).
- The project-level half of the same list is about a session's own directory rather than about this tree. It covers `<workspace>/.minimax/skills`, `<workspace>/.claude/skills` and `<workspace>/.agents/skills`, with a walk up to the repository root when `walkUp` is on. It is named in the plan rather than looked for.

**The assets, and what is only named**

- A MiniMax agent is a directory: `agents/<name>/` holds `agent.md` (the system prompt), `config.yaml` (its model choice), and optionally `PERSONA.md`, `skills/`, `memory/`, `crons/` and `daily/`. This build's subagent is a single markdown file, so `agent.md` is what travels.
- `PERSONA.md` is deliberately not folded into it. The two are separate prompt assets over there (`getPersona` and `getSystemPrompt`, `agent-files.ts:866,906`). For a custom agent only the system prompt becomes `agentSystemPrompt` (`agent-profile.ts:502`), and the persona travels beside it as its own field. A concatenation here is a document neither tool has. So the persona is named in the agent's note instead, and the user can paste it in if they want it inline.
- A `skills/` tree under an agent is scoped to that agent alone (`resolveConfiguredSkillRoots`, `local-runtime/src/skills/roots.ts:40-50` gives every agent its own root). A skill here is global and loaded into every session, so the report counts and names those trees, and the importer does not carry them. That is the same line the grok source draws at its per-workspace memory.
- An agent directory with no readable `agent.md` is named and skipped. The reason is that MiniMax lists no agent of that name either: its own roster skips such a directory (`inspectCanonicalCustomAgent`, `agent-files.ts:167-186`).
- The importer counts the copies MiniMax ships under `agents/.builtin` and never reads them. They are vendor-written, and the source's own updates are what keeps them current. The importer counts the vendor's own skills root `.builtin-skills` (`packages/config/src/config.ts:2078`, `builtinSkillsDir`) and never reads it for the same reason.
- The importer counts the plugin directories under `plugins` and never walks them. The importer counts the plan documents under `plans` and never reads them, and it does the same for entries under `memory`.
- The `review-rules` tree is named and not imported. MiniMax splices the user's own reviewer instructions into a review prompt as `<user-review-rules>` (`local-runtime/src/review/preparation.ts:80-82`). The reader reads a rule there in every session, and a review turn wrote this one.
- The user-global instruction document is `<root>/AGENTS.md`.
- Two directories under `v2` are named and left. The `chats` tree holds the pre-`v2` session ledgers, and `mcode/drafts` holds the composer text a user typed and never sent. Both are the user's own words and neither is a settings document.
- Entry names under the root that match `credential`, `secret`, `token`, `auth` or a `.env` suffix are reported by name and never opened. The names that hit are things like `auth`, `credentials` and `cli-auth`. Directories this reader does not account for are counted, and an unreadable root contributes nothing. The installer's own directory `~/.minimax-code` is named when it exists and never read.

### T3 Code

**The state read, and the two-phase schedule**

- T3 Code keeps its state in one directory. The reader reads `settings.json`, `client-settings.json` and `desktop-settings.json` from it, and it lists the threads from the database `t3StateDatabase` names.
- Nothing here throws. Every file is optional, every JSON document may be malformed, and the database may belong to a build with a different schema. A T3 release that renamed a column costs this importer one query, not the whole run. The failures land in `RawT3Code.skipped` with their own reasons. So a report can say what was not read rather than leave a user to work out whether the migration was partial.
- No credential enters a read. T3's sensitive environment values are not in `settings.json` at all, and the `secrets/` directory they do live in is never opened.
- The `T3Skipped` list carries the things seen and not read, each with a reason. Values are never here, and a sensitive environment entry contributes its variable's name. A path is allowed only where the thing is a path and has no other name. The two entries about state directories name themselves that way, because a user who ran a dev build recognizes `~/.t3/dev` and nothing else.
- Sessions are not in `skipped`. The reader reads them through `t3SessionRows` and `t3SessionMessages`, on the two-phase schedule the rest of this migration uses. That is: list the candidates, let the user choose, then read only what they chose.
- A `T3SessionRow` is one conversation thread, as the list phase sees it. It deliberately carries no message text. The list phase runs before the user makes a choice, and a list that reads every conversation makes the picker cost the whole history. This is the one input in this migration the architecture reads once, and only for the sessions that were actually chosen.
- The `RawT3Code` result carries the state directory, the three documents and the skip list. A document is `null` when it was absent or unparseable. A JSON file that was not read gets a reason: `not in the state directory`, `could not be read` or `not valid JSON`.

**The two spellings of absence**

- Absence is spelled two different ways in `T3Settings`. The spelling a field uses is part of its contract rather than an accident of the read. A field read out of the document with `text` is a `string`, and an empty one means the file stated nothing. A field read as a shape is `| null`, and `null` means the document held nothing of that shape.
- The two are not interchangeable, and the difference carries weight for `runtimeMode`. It is spelled out in `t3-plan.ts`.
- They are kept apart because a `string | null` that is only ever `null` or a non-empty string is a lie the type tells about the value. It invites `value ?? fallback`, which on an empty string does not fall back, so the fail-open branch a reader writes to be safe silently never runs. That is not hypothetical. An empty string is what T3's own absence decodes to here. The one setting where an imported default is wrong is a `string`.
- The file can be silent because T3's writer strips defaults before it persists (`stripDefaultServerSettings`, `apps/server/src/serverSettings.ts:387-419`, called at `:550` against `PERSISTED_SERVER_SETTINGS_DEFAULTS`). That default is the decoded schema default, so a setting left at T3's own default is simply not in the file.
- The `defaultModelSelection` key is the one field where the reader cannot tell absent from present and null. Both are `null` here, and the map is the same either way, so the distinction is not carried.
- The `runtimeMode` key reads `defaultRuntimeMode`, or `""` when the file states none. The `modelSelection` field reads `defaultModelSelection`, already resolved from T3's legacy shape. Its `textModelSelection` reads `textGenerationModelSelection`, which wins over the default for text turns.
- The `theme` key reads `defaultTheme`, or `""` when the file states none. T3 stores an id, not a palette.
- The `environment` key holds the non-sensitive environment variables from every provider instance. The `projectOverrides` field holds the per-project model overrides, keyed by project id. It is carried so the report can say how many projects had one. It is not applied. The target has no per-project model setting, and a project id in T3 is not a path this importer can resolve to one.
- The `settingsKeys` list holds every top-level key `settings.json` has, whatever became of it. It is carried so the report can account for the rest of the file by name rather than leave a settings document half-migrated and unexplained. The `settings` field alone cannot do it. It holds the seven keys this importer found something in, and the sixty-odd it did not are the ones a user notices vanish.
- The `readSettings` reader returns the keys the target has an equivalent for. Everything else in T3's schema does not apply here. It belongs to a desktop app with its own process model. The list is worktree cleanup policy, storage cleanup, response streams, device hosts, observability and Tailscale. The rest of the file gets one aggregate note in `t3-plan.ts` rather than a claim about each key.

**The sensitive values, and the secret store**

- A `sensitive` entry in a provider's environment is deliberately not carried. T3 does not persist the value of one. It writes the six-dot marker `SECRET_REDACTED = "••••••"` (`apps/server/src/serverSettings.ts:147`, applied by `redactSecret` at `:159`) and keeps the real value in the secret store under `<stateDir>/secrets` (`apps/server/src/config.ts:163`). It swaps the value back at every settings read (`materializeProviderEnvironmentSecrets` at `serverSettings.ts:699`, wired into `getSettings` at `:1127` and the change stream at `:779`).
- So a sensitive entry read here holds a marker, not a value. A write of that marker into the target's settings produces an environment variable that looks configured and silently fails. The entry is skipped with the reason `sensitive value, kept in T3's secrets directory rather than in its settings`, and the `secrets/` directory is never opened.
- The redaction is also why an `environment` array read from disk can be trusted to hold real values on its non-sensitive entries. The marker is written instead of a value, never alongside one.
- Each entry carries the variable's name and the value when the entry is not sensitive. It also names the provider instance and the driver slug that instance used. The slugs include `codex`, `claudeAgent` and `antigravity`.
- The `providerInstances` map is `Record<ProviderInstanceId, ProviderInstanceConfig>` (`packages/contracts/src/settings.ts:1292`). Each entry has a `driver` slug and an `environment` array (`packages/contracts/src/providerInstance.ts:104-110`: `name`, `value`, `sensitive` and a `valueRedacted` flag). The per-driver `config` blob is `Schema.Unknown` by design, because each driver registers its own decoder with the runtime registry. This reader does not look inside it for a key or a token, and a blob that happens to hold one is never copied.
- The reader also reads the legacy single-instance `providers` map (`settings.ts:1279`). It is the same information in an older shape, and a skip drops the environment of any user whose T3 predates the instance split.
- The current map is keyed by a user-chosen instance id, and each entry names its own driver. The legacy map is keyed by the driver slug itself, so there the key stands in when the entry does not repeat it.

**The model choice, and the legacy shape**

- Two shapes exist on the wire, and `T3Settings` normalizes them. `ModelSelectionWire` is `{ instanceId, model, options? }` (`packages/contracts/src/orchestration.ts:75-79`). Choices persisted before the driver and instance split carry `{ provider, model }` instead.
- T3's own schema absorbs the legacy shape. Its transform runs before the decode and takes `provider` as the key when `instanceId` is absent (`:92-107`). This reader reads the slug the same way. A choice written by an older build is not dropped for a field the current one no longer has.
- The key T3 routes a choice by is an instance id, or the legacy driver slug it was promoted from. The model id is what T3 stored, before any registry lookup. A flag says whether the key came from the legacy `provider` field.
- An `instanceId` wins over a `provider` when both are present, which is the order T3's own transform uses (`orchestration.ts:99-107`). The current field is authoritative, and the legacy one is the fallback for payloads written before the split.
- That transform passes the legacy slug straight through as the key. Its comment names `defaultInstanceIdForDriver` (`:148` in `providerInstance.ts`) as what the slug stands for, and the transform never calls it. This reader does the same, which is all a reader outside T3's registry can do with it.

**The threads, and the two filters**

- `t3SessionRows` returns every conversation thread, without the messages. It is not T3's own thread list, and the two differences are deliberate. T3 has two: `listThreadRows` takes every row (`orchestration/Layers/ProjectionSnapshotQuery.ts:562-601`), and `listActiveThreadRows` drops what the user archived or deleted (`:610-652`).
- This query is the first with two filters layered on it. Each filter is about what a migration brings back rather than what a session picker shows.
- A soft-deleted row is excluded on both sides, because the user threw it away and an import puts back something they removed.
- An archived row is not excluded. T3 hides it from the main list. The second view, `listArchivedThreadRows` (`:701-...`), covers the same rows, and an archived conversation is still one the user paid for. An exclusion adds a filter about the layout of T3's sidebar to a migration.
- The join to `projection_projects` is not optional. The thread row has no directory of its own, and `workspace_root` is the only other place one can come from.
- The order is `created_at, thread_id`, T3's own tiebreak for a list (`:601`). The second key carries weight. The key `created_at` is not unique. An unstable order makes a session picker show two different lists for two runs of the same state.
- The query returns `[]` for a database that is absent, unreadable or from a build whose schema differs. A T3 release that renamed a projection table costs this importer its conversations and nothing else. The reason is not returned, because this runs inside the list phase, which reports its own notes. The settings read is unaffected either way.
- The `id` field is `projection_threads.thread_id`, which is what T3 resumes by. The `title` field is the thread's title, or `""` when T3 left it empty. The `startedAt` field is `created_at` in epoch ms, and `0` when T3 stored something unparseable.
- The `cwd` field is the effective directory the thread ran in, or `null` when neither column gave one. The `worktree_path` column wins over `workspace_root` (`005_Projections.ts:11` and `:27`). A thread opened in a worktree ran in the worktree and not in the project it belongs to. There is no `cwd` column anywhere in the schema, and that was checked against the DDL rather than taken on report. So this join is the only place a directory can come from.
- The `toolCount` field is how many tool-lifecycle activity rows the thread accumulated. It is carried so the report can say what a transcript will not hold rather than leave the user to find out. T3 records a tool call as an activity row (`projection_thread_activities`) rather than as a message, and the row is shaped for T3's own interface. Its columns are `activity_id`, `thread_id`, `turn_id`, `tone`, `kind`, `summary`, `payload_json`, `created_at` and a `sequence` added by `008_ProjectionThreadActivitySequence.ts`. That is a one-line summary of what happened and an opaque payload, with no argument list and no result paired with it. A rebuild of a tool call from that is separate work with its own failure modes. So this importer carries the conversation and reports the count instead.
- The `toolActivityCounts` reader is best-effort and separate from the thread query on purpose. The activities table is the one most likely to be absent or renamed. It is the newest of the three projections and the one this importer cares about least. To add its failure to the thread query lets a T3 build that renamed it take the user's conversations down with it. That loses the thing they wanted in exchange for the count of the thing they did not.
- The `t3SessionMessages` reader carries the messages of one thread in T3's own order. The roles are `user`, `assistant` and `reasoning` rather than the two roles a transcript usually holds. The `reasoning` role is one T3 records in this same table, and it is a real part of what the thread holds. The `system` role is not carried, because it is the desktop client's own frame. The conversation now lives in a different client, and a replayed system preamble looks like something the assistant said.
- A message holds the role from `projection_thread_messages.role`, the text, and `created_at` in epoch ms, zero when unparseable. The text is the whole message for this schema.
- The order is `created_at ASC, message_id ASC`, T3's own order (`ProjectionThreadMessages.ts:225`). The second key carries weight. The key `created_at` alone is not unique, and without it the order differs from the one the user's own client shows.

**The state directories, and the attachments**

- `noteOtherStateDirs` names a state directory that holds content and was not the one read. `t3Root` reads the production directory when both exist. The `t3-home.ts` header says why that is the right tree. So a dev build's state stays where it is. The name is in the report because `the migration ignored a directory full of my conversations` and `there was no dev tree` look the same from the outside until you go and look.
- The other state directory, when it holds nothing, produces nothing. `There is a dev directory holding nothing` is not information a migration report needs.
- The `noteAttachments` reader names the attachments directory when there is one. An imported conversation carries its text and nothing else. So a message that points at a file leaves a path in the transcript that resolves to nothing once the migration is done. That is worth one report line rather than a find the user makes while they scroll back through a conversation they just imported.
- The files themselves are not copied. They are conversation-scoped binary blobs with names T3 chose, and a target history entry is a text transcript. To put the bytes somewhere and rewrite every reference to point at them is a different migration. Half of it is the worse outcome. The originals stay where T3 put them.
- T3 stores timestamps as ISO strings, and anything else reads as `0`.

**The plan, and the two absences it states**

- T3 Code has no credentials on disk to migrate. The report says so as part of what this source maps rather than as a footnote. Two independent facts hold that true. The first is why the report can promise a migration needs no re-auth: the values are not in `settings.json`. A `sensitive` provider entry is written as the six-dot marker, and the real value lives in the secret store the importer never opens. So the entry is skipped with a reason, by name only. The second is why a model reference cannot come across at all: the key T3 routes by does not port. A model choice names an instance id that resolves inside T3's own provider registry (`packages/contracts/src/orchestration.ts:75-79`), and nothing in that registry exists here. A model that happens to share a name with one of ours is a coincidence rather than a match.
- T3 persists no rule files, no skills, no subagent registry and no MCP server list. A provider instance's `config` blob is `Schema.Unknown` by design (`packages/contracts/src/providerInstance.ts:104-110`, because each driver registers its own decoder with the runtime registry). So an MCP configuration inside T3 lives in a blob this importer does not parse. It is not in a shape the target can use even if the importer does parse it.
- The report says T3 has none on disk this importer can read, which is the true and the weaker claim. T3 can talk to MCP servers. It does not keep the list where a migration can pick it up.

**The runtime modes, and the one row that does not map**

- The `T3_RUNTIME_MODES` table maps three of T3's four runtime modes and leaves one out. The names come from the labels T3's own composer renders (`apps/web/src/components/chat/runtimeModeConfig.ts:8-28`). A mode name read cold is exactly the kind of string that maps to the wrong thing. The first two are `approval-required` (Supervised: ask before commands and file changes) and `auto-accept-edits` (Auto-accept edits: auto-approve edits, ask before other actions). The others are `auto` (Auto: supported providers approve routine actions, others still ask) and `full-access` (Full access: allow commands and edits without prompts).
- `auto-accept-edits` maps to `ask`, which is narrower than the label says. This build has no mode that applies edits while everything else asks. An import that silently widened writes to unasked is the worse of the two errors. The same call `CLAUDE_PERMISSION_MODES` makes for Claude Code's `acceptEdits` and `MINIMAX_PERMISSION_MODES` makes for MiniMax's. The report line says so rather than call it a rename.
- The `auto` mode is absent from the table. It means a classifier decides, and the nearest mode here, `ask`, means the opposite: a human decides. To carry it over under another name is a lie about what the session does. The Claude Code and Kimi Code mappers make the same call for their same-named mode.
- The `full-access` row is the one that maps to the unconfined sandbox, and for the same reason it is `bypassPermissions`' row in Claude's table. The single value does two jobs, never ask and no confinement. To claim only the mode half leaves the sandbox at whatever another source decided.
- The table is exported for its row count rather than its values. A row added here without a line in the mapper test's table is an import whose claim nobody checked.

**The mode claim, and the empty string**

- An absent `defaultRuntimeMode` is T3's own default, which is `full-access` (`DEFAULT_RUNTIME_MODE`, `packages/contracts/src/orchestration.ts:135`, and `packages/contracts/src/settings.test.ts:82` decodes an empty document to it). T3's writer strips defaults before it persists (`stripDefaultServerSettings`, `apps/server/src/serverSettings.ts:387-419`, called at `:550`). So a user who never touched the setting has no `defaultRuntimeMode` in the file at all.
- So the mode is claimed only when the file states one. The opposite choice looks more faithful and is worse. To import the default is to write `agent` and `danger-full-access`, no prompts and no confinement, for a user whose file said nothing. The grounds are that the source's schema supplies it. To fail open on a security posture is the one direction a settings import must not move in. To omit it leaves the session at this build's own default, the stricter of the two. The report says what was left alone rather than report a mode nobody chose.
- A present key means the user, or a build whose default differed, actually said something, and the importer claims it. The `full-access` row is not dead for that reason. A hand-edited file reaches it, and so does any T3 build older than the one whose default it is.
- The absence arrives as `""` and not as `null`, and the guard is `if (!mode)` for that reason. To write `settings.runtimeMode ?? "full-access"` instead looks like the same fail-open behaviour and is not. The `??` operator does not fire on an empty string. So the branch meant to widen a file that said nothing is dead code, while it reads exactly like a deliberate choice. The type is a `string` so that mistake cannot compile.

**The two model keys**

- T3 keeps two model keys because it routes different turns to different models (`defaultModelSelection` and `textGenerationModelSelection`, `packages/contracts/src/settings.ts:1145` and `:1244`). This build has one `model` setting, so the importer claims exactly one of the two, and write order does not settle the choice.
- The default wins, for two reasons that point the same way. It is the setting a user reaches for first, and the one a migration report line is expected to carry. The text key is, in a stock install, the schema's own default rather than something the user wrote (`settings.ts:1244` supplies `{ instanceId: "codex", model: DEFAULT_TEXT_GENERATION_MODEL, … }`). To prefer it often imports a model nobody chose over one they did.
- The key that did not win is named whether or not it resolves. So a user who set both is never left to wonder where the second one went.

**The theme check**

- T3 stores a theme id, not a palette, and none of its ids is a theme name here. The five it ships are `t3-chat`, `grove`, `ocean`, `ember` and `iris` (`packages/shared/src/themePalettes.ts:1`), plus whatever a user published. This build's eight are `dark`, `light`, `high-contrast-dark`, `high-contrast-light`, `deuteranopia-dark`, `tritanopia-dark`, `spiderman` and `splatoon` (`packages/tui/src/themes/`). The sets do not intersect.
- The two ids that come nearest, `light` and `dark`, cannot be a T3 theme id at all. The `EnvironmentThemeId` pattern rejects them, because a published `dark.json` otherwise captures every client whose stored preference is the stock `"dark"` (`packages/contracts/src/server.ts:510-512`). The `t3 theme set` command refuses them again through `UNPUBLISHABLE_THEME_IDS` (`apps/server/src/cli/theme.ts:337`).
- So this is a membership check that in practice never passes, and the branch is kept anyway. The file is decoded leniently and outlives the build that wrote it, so a hand-written or legacy value can hold anything. A check expected to fail is still the correct thing to write rather than a skip.

**The environment, scoped and global**

- Every entry of the provider environment is scoped to one provider instance in T3 and becomes global here. Every line is a `downgrade` rather than a copy. In T3 these variables are injected into the process that talks to one provider (`packages/contracts/src/providerInstance.ts:104-110`). A target `settings.env` applies to every tool call, Bash included. To import an `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` meant for one provider and let it reach a shell widens the scope the user asked for. The report scores it as one.
- Two instances that disagree about one name are not merged. The target holds one value per variable, so to choose one silently discards the other. The report line for the discarded one still says it was imported, which is the worse outcome. Instead the name is skipped with both instances named, which is the only outcome that leaves the user able to fix it by hand.
- The same name from two instances with the same value is not a conflict, because there is nothing to choose. The importer claims it once, and the line names both.

**The per-project overrides**

- T3 keeps a `projectSettingsOverrides` entry per project (`packages/contracts/src/settings.ts:1157`, a `Record<ProjectId, ProjectSettingsOverrides>`), and this build has no per-project setting to put one in. The second half of the reason is the one that matters. A T3 project id is an opaque record key, not a path, so nothing here turns it back into the directory it stands for.
- The count is carried, because that is what tells a user with twelve overrides that twelve decisions did not come across. It is the difference between a migration that is incomplete and one that claims it finished.

**The two settings files beside the one**

- Neither `client-settings.json` nor `desktop-settings.json` is imported, and the reason is the same for both and is a fact rather than a shrug. Every key in each is a presentation or a host concern. T3 Code is a GUI with a web, a desktop and a mobile client. Most of its settings describe how that looks and where that runs. This build is a terminal application with a host and not a client surface. So no setting in either file has an equivalent to be lost in.
- The `client-settings.json` document is `ClientSettingsSchema` (`packages/contracts/src/settings.ts:298-340`): notifications, diff colours, chat width, panel animation, and the in-app browser's viewport, zoom, appearance and capture options.
- The `desktop-settings.json` document is `DesktopSettingsDocument` (`apps/desktop/src/settings/DesktopAppSettings.ts:98-116`): window geometry, the update channel, the Tailscale service, how far the machine is exposed, and the WSL backend.
- The keys each file holds are named in the report. So the paragraph above can be checked against the user's own file rather than taken on trust. A settings file that is silently not migrated is a worse experience than one the report explains.

**The final aggregates**

- The `T3_SETTINGS_HANDLED` set holds the keys of `settings.json` this mapper accounts for. The rest are named by one aggregate at the end rather than one line each, and the two lists are read together. A key added to the set without a mapper behind it is a setting that vanishes with no report line. That is the failure the aggregate exists to make visible.
- The `planT3UnhandledSettings` container is rebuilt from the key list rather than carried whole. The reader hands over the keys so the plan can name what it did not handle. To keep the parsed document as well is a second copy of the same thing for no reader.
- The skipped entries replayed by `planT3Skipped` are the ones the read phase did not turn into anything. They are a file that was not there, a document that was not JSON, and a provider environment value T3 keeps in its `secrets/` directory. Each carries a name, never a value, and the sensitive-entry path records the variable's name and stops there. A report is something a user may paste into an issue.
- The `planT3Code` signature takes no `mcpServers`, no `existing`, no `existingMcpServers` and no `force`, and each absence is deliberate rather than an oversight. T3's MCP configuration is not readable from disk, and there is no per-project model setting to collide with. To take parameters this source has no use for reads as a gap a later change can fill by accident. To leave them out makes the absence the type system's problem.

### Step Code

**The state directory, and the pre-rename tree**

- The fallback directory name has two exported spellings: `STEPCODE_DEFAULT_DIR` and `STEP_DEFAULT_DIR`, both `.stepcode`. Every source here names its fallback `<PRODUCT>_DEFAULT_DIR`, and the settings half of this one named it after Step's own variable, `STEPCODE_CONFIG_DIR`. Both names are exported because both are imported, and a second literal is a second rule, free to drift apart.
- The directory Step Code used before the rename is `.step-harness`, exported as `STEPCODE_LEGACY_DIR`, and Step's own name for it is `LEGACY_RENAMED_CONFIG_DIR` (`step/environment.ts`). Step reads it in two narrow places and never as a live root. The `step/auth.ts` reader imports a credential out of `<retired>/agent/auth.json` or `<retired>/auth.json`. The `step/session.ts` reader treats a session path under it as legacy and copies the file into the canonical tree before it opens it. So a user who never launched the renamed build keeps everything under `.step-harness`, where no Step reader looks. That is the case this fallback exists for.
- The reader reads the canonical tree, or `.step-harness` when the canonical one holds nothing and no environment variable moved it elsewhere. The fallback is guarded by the overrides on purpose. A user who set `$STEPCODE_CONFIG_DIR` or `$STEP_CODING_AGENT_DIR` said where the tree is. To read a directory they did not name, the one the previous release used, is to import a tree they may want left alone. Without an override the canonical path can only be `<home>/.stepcode`, so an empty one beside a populated `.step-harness` has one explanation.
- The has-content test is the same test detection applies to what this returns, because `sourceHasContent` lists the directory. So the two cannot disagree about whether the source is present.

**The config root, and the two agent-directory spellings**

- The config root is `<home>/<config dir>`, and the parent of `$STEP_CODING_AGENT_DIR` when that is set. `config.toml`, `auth.json` and `.credentials.json` sit there. It is deliberately not the agent directory, which is a child of it: the files sit next to the agent directory rather than inside it. A reader that joined them onto the agent directory looks for `config.toml` one level too deep.
- The port of `resolveStepConfigRoot` carries two details that read like accidents and are not. The override is `resolve()`d first. A `..` joins onto a relative path as text, so without the resolve a relative override puts credentials beside the process's own directory. The resolve runs against this process's own directory, which is what Step does too. It is the one place where a relative override is ambiguous. Both sides read it the same way, because each is a process with its own directory.
- The second detail: a filesystem root has no parent to be the config root. So it keeps the files inside the agent directory itself rather than write outside the namespace the user named.
- The agent directory has two spellings. The session side, `stepAgentDir`, uses `$STEP_CODING_AGENT_DIR` verbatim, and a `~` at the start is not expanded. So `$STEP_CODING_AGENT_DIR=~/elsewhere` names a directory literally called `~` under the directory the process runs in. To expand it here is to read a tree that does not exist and to report the user's real one as absent.
- Step's two spellings disagree only for a `~`-prefixed value, and the readers that matter split along the same line. The session machinery calls `resolveStepAgentDir`, while settings, skills, prompts, extensions and `models.json` go through `getAgentDir()`. The migration keeps both spellings rather than pick one and be wrong for half the tree. The session one is `stepAgentDir`, and `stepAssetDir` is the other, the `getAgentDir()` spelling that expands `~` and falls back to `<home>/<config dir>/agent`. The files `models.json` and `settings.json`, and the `themes/`, `prompts/`, `skills/` and `tools/` directories, are all reached through this spelling.
- The tilde rule: `~` alone is the home, `~/` and on Windows `~\` prefix it, and a `~` anywhere else is an ordinary character. Both of Step's copies of the rule agree (`packages/coding-agent/src/utils/paths.ts:89-90` and `packages/agent-core/src/harness/env/nodejs.ts:53-55`).
- The expansion runs against the home this module was handed rather than `os.homedir()`. That is the only difference from Step, and it is not observable on a machine where the two agree. Where they disagree, a `$HOME` that is not the system's own home, this reader still reads the home the migration was told to read. That is the point of the argument.

**The session root, and the three inputs**

- The session root is the environment override, else the `sessionDir` setting, else `<root>/agent/sessions`. Step's startup chain folds three inputs into one: the `--session-dir` flag first, then `$STEP_CODING_AGENT_SESSION_DIR`, then the setting (`main.ts:1018-1021`). That is this order without the flag, for which a migration has no argv.
- The function that looks like it disagrees does not: `resolveConfiguredSessionDir` tries its `sessionDir` parameter before the variable (`step/session.ts:153`), and that parameter is the folded answer. The flag its help text names as the winner is the argv one (`cli/args.ts:675`). The settings half (`getSessionDir`, `core/settings-manager.ts:723-725`) reads the setting from `settings.json`, and it arrives here as an argument.
- The setting is tested for truthiness rather than blankness, so a whitespace-only `sessionDir` is a very odd directory name rather than an absent setting. Only the absent case falls through.
- Where both are set, the environment's directory is read, and that is the one the user set for this process. The test `test/step-home.test.ts:174-182` pins it, and the report names the directory it read either way rather than imply the read was complete.
- Both overrides are tilde-expanded and then used as they stand. Step resolves a relative value against the directory of the session it opens, which is per-session knowledge a reader of finished sessions cannot have. So a relative value resolves against the source home, and the report names that. A guess at the session's directory looks under a project while the sessions sit elsewhere.

**The walk, and what was passed over**

- The walk is the one Step's own `listAll` makes (`core/session-manager.ts:1657-1716`). Each session directory is read, and every `.jsonl` file directly inside it is a session. Every subdirectory is a `--<cwd>--` project whose `.jsonl` files are sessions.
- Nothing else is a session, and a name that is not a `.jsonl` file is passed over without a report. Step reads no other name as one, so a note about it is a note about a file the user never had as a session.
- The suffix is case-sensitive, because Step's is (`f.endsWith(".jsonl")`, `core/session-manager.ts:641`, `:825`, `:1686`, and the `${fileTimestamp}_${sessionId}.jsonl` name at `:954`). This reader must not present a `.JSONL` file as a session when Step itself never shows it, and must not hide one Step does show.
- Step's own resolution names one session directory (`resolveStepSessionDir`, `step/environment.ts:60-62`), and the trees are two. The list holds the session root of each tree, in read order: first the tree `stepRoot` chose, then the pre-rename `.step-harness` tree.
- Step writes sessions under `<agent dir>/sessions` into a `--<cwd>--` bucket per directory (`getDefaultSessionDirPath`, `core/session-manager.ts:476-481`). When a session directory is configured the file lands directly in it (`SessionManager.create`, `:1521-1524`, and the flat read at `listSessionsFromDir`, `:812-826`). The reader reads both layouts from every entry, because the same directory can be either.
- Step reads the retired tree too. A session path under it is recognized as legacy and copied into the canonical tree before it is opened (`isLegacyPiSessionPath` and `relocateLegacyPiSession`, `step/session.ts:168-172`, `:191-220`). So a user who never launched the renamed build has real sessions there, and one who did may have them beside the new ones.
- The retired tree is second in the order, so the reader reads a session present in both trees once, from the canonical copy. A tree that was copied keeps its session ids, which is what the walk de-duplicates on.
- Two cases collapse the list to one entry, and both mirror the tree rule. An environment variable already said where the tree is, or the retired tree is the chosen tree.
- What the list cannot honour is the `sessionDir` setting in `settings.json` (`core/settings-manager.ts:140`), which the CLI's `--session-dir` flag shares. It belongs to the settings reader, which is handed the value with the rest of the planner's work. A user who set it has sessions read from the default location instead, and the report gives the name of the directory it read.
- The reader reads each session file once, and only its head: one bounded read of 64 KiB, never the file. Only the reader that imports a session reads it end to end. So a scan of a home with a hundred sessions costs a hundred heads rather than a hundred conversations. Step's own header scan gives the first line 4096 bytes. It then reads up to 1 MiB for oversized metadata (`SESSION_HEADER_READ_BUFFER_SIZE` and `MAX_SESSION_HEADER_SCAN_BYTES`, `core/session-manager.ts:491-494`). The 64 KiB budget covers the same line in a single read, and a longer header is one neither tool parses.
- A head holds its header, something else, or nothing that parses. A blank or malformed line is stepped over rather than disqualify the file. Step's own header scan does the same (`parseSessionHeaderCandidate` returns `"keep scanning"` for both, `:564-570`). So a file whose first line is damaged but whose next line is a header is a session to Step and to this reader. The two failures stay apart because the report says which one happened. The `none` case is a head with no JSON at all. The `other` case is a head whose first JSON line is not a session header.
- The report names everything session-shaped that is not one, each with its own reason. The reasons are a head with no JSON, a first entry that is not a session header, and a file that does not open. Also a header with no id, a directory with no session file in it, and a session already read from an earlier directory. The empty directory is an ordinary state rather than a broken one. Step creates the bucket before the first turn is saved (`getDefaultSessionDir`, `:483-489`).
- The `stepSessions` function returns the scan's sessions, for a caller that wants the files and not the tally.

**The header, and what the path does not say**

- Every field comes from the header line, and nothing is recovered from the path. That is a decision rather than an omission.
- The id is the header's, which is the id Step uses (`SessionHeader.id`, `core/session-manager.ts:32-39`). The file name carries the same id as a suffix, `${fileTimestamp}_${id}.jsonl` (`:954`). To read it back is to pattern-match on a name Step never promises to keep. A session whose header states no id is one Step itself refuses to open. The `loadEntriesFromFile` reader returns no entries unless the first entry is a `session` with a string `id` (`:551-553`). Those are skipped with a reason rather than given a made-up id.
- The cwd is the header's too, and the directory name cannot replace it. The bucket name starts as `--<cwd>--`. Its first separator is stripped, and every `/`, `\` and `:` maps to `-` (`getDefaultSessionDirPath`, `:476-481`, and again at `step/session.ts:105-110`). That map loses information, since a directory whose own name holds a `-` is indistinguishable from a deeper path. There is no decoder anywhere in Step. Every reader takes the cwd from the header (`getSessionHeaderCwd`, `:626-629`), and that value is what the session test matches on (`sessionCwdMatches`, `:631-633`). A `cwd` of `""` is not a directory, because that test treats it as absent, so the report gives `null`.
- The `startedAt` value is the header's `timestamp` in epoch ms. It is `0` when the header states none it can parse, which is the value `buildSessionInfo` gives it (`:743`).
- The `version` field is `null` for a v1 session, which is what "v1 sessions don't have this" (`:34`) leaves behind. The reader, not this scan, turns that into the entry list of a v1 file.
- A session file's directory is the `--<cwd>--` bucket it sits in, or the session root itself when the file was written flat.
- The reader reads directories and entries in name order so the file list does not depend on `readdir`. An unreadable directory contributes nothing rather than abort the walk over the ones that are.
- A file that went away, a directory that looked like a file, a permission denied: the caller reports it and the walk goes on.

### Kimi Code

**The journal, and the fold it mirrors**

- A Kimi Code session lives under `$KIMI_CODE_HOME/sessions`. Each conversation is a directory, and each agent inside it has one `agents/<agentId>/wire.jsonl`, an append-only journal of everything that shaped the agent's context.
- The transcript this reader takes is the journal, not the context, and the two differ. That difference is the whole difficulty of this module.
- The context is what the model was sent. `context.apply_compaction` throws the prefix away and keeps a summary. `context.undo` takes a rewind back. `context.clear` empties it.
- The journal keeps every one of those records plus the messages they discarded, in order. Kimi's own reader therefore rebuilds the context from a fold of the journal (`agent/replayBuilder/fold.ts`), not from a plain read.
- This module mirrors that fold rather than invent a read of its own. The boundary an imported session resumes from has to be the boundary Kimi itself resumes from. Otherwise the import is a summary of a conversation the user never had.
- The fold's rules are reproduced one for one. A message appended while a tool result is still open waits behind it (`appendMessage`'s deferral). The journal can deliver a user message mid-tool-run, and the context never holds it in that order.
- A step's shape is the fold's own: `step.begin` opens an assistant message, `content.part` fills it, and `tool.call` adds a call to it. An assistant turn is several records.
- A call with no result by the time the step ends is closed with an error result (`TOOL_INTERRUPTED_ON_RESUME_OUTPUT` there). That keeps an interrupted turn's pair whole.
- The `context.undo` record walks back over real user inputs, skips injections and stops at a compaction summary. The messages it removed leave the journal too.
- The `context.clear` record empties the context and leaves the journal alone, so a cleared session still imports with its whole transcript.
- The `context.apply_compaction` record replaces the context with the summary, plus the legacy kept tail when the record is in the old shape. It binds that summary to the `full_compaction.begin` record already in the journal.
- The omissions are as deliberate. The importer counts a `think` part that arrives only encrypted and drops it, because this build cannot vouch for the signature.
- Audio and video parts are left behind, and so are the records of what a tool showed on screen. The internal records go too: `turn.prompt`, `llm.request`, `usage.record`, and the goal and permission records. The model never saw any of them as content.
- A journal whose records cannot be folded at all is refused rather than half-read. The example is a `content.part` for a step that was never opened. A reader that guesses there imports a prefix the source itself dropped.
- Nothing here writes, and nothing outside `<root>/sessions` is opened.

**The list, and the three skips**

- The `listKimiSessions` function offers every session Kimi's own list shows. The walk is the authority on what exists, and `<root>/session_index.jsonl` is not.
- The index is an append log of `{sessionId, sessionDir, workDir}` lines, and it holds deletions too. As the tool's own reader notes, some entries point outside the sessions tree.
- So the reader reads the index only to attribute a cwd, and only for a line whose `sessionDir` is the directory the walk actually found. A stale line must not place a conversation in a project it never ran in.
- Three things are skipped, each for its own reason. A child session (`custom.child_session_kind === "child"`) is a subagent conversation spawned for a task rather than one the user had. Kimi writes it beside its parent, with both keys, so the marker is exact.
- Every agent's journal but the main agent's is skipped, counted as subagent threads. A session's `agents/` directory holds one wire per agent, and each of them imports the same conversation again.
- A directory that is neither a session nor meant to be one has no state file and no `agents/`. It is passed over without a report, because a note about something the user never had is a note about nothing.
- A directory with an `agents/` directory but no readable state is a broken session, and the importer counts it rather than passes it over.
- The walk skips two directories of this tree by name, and every dotted name by a rule. `.index-cache` is a scan cache and `.index-dirty` is a journal of dirty marks, and both sit in this very tree. Kimi's own walk skips those two by name.
- The dot-prefix rule is this reader's own, and it is safe for the same reason. A workspace id can never begin with a dot, because the tool builds every one as `wd_<slug>_<hash>` (`encodeWorkDirKey`). A dotted directory there is the product's own files, whichever release wrote it.

**The fields of a session**

- The `listKimiSessions` result fills each field from the state file first. The cwd comes from `cwd`, then `workDir`, then `custom.cwd`, which is the tool's own recovery order.
- The index and the wire head are the fallbacks for the cwd. A session with no directory is one this importer cannot scope to a project.
- The start time is `state.createdAt`, with the wire head as its fallback, and the updated time falls back to the created one. The reader reads a numeric or string stamp, and an unreadable one is zero.
- A title falls back to the last prompt, and a session with neither gets a line with no title rather than a made-up one.
- The reader reads the state file at `state.json` and then at `session-meta/state.json`, the legacy path. The archived flag and the `forkedFrom` value come from the same file.
- The archived flag marks a session Kimi filed away rather than left in its list, and it still imports.

**The agents of a session, and which one is the conversation**

- The `listAgents` function takes the agents of a session from two directions: what `state.agents` names, plus any directory under `agents/` it does not. Both directions are needed.
- A state file written before an agent was registered leaves a journal nobody lists. A state file that lost its agents map hides every journal on disk. The tool's own reader calls the second case the empty-inventory case.
- An agent id passes the tool's own guard (`isSafeAgentId`): letters, digits, dot, underscore and dash only, and never `.` or `..`.
- Both sources feed one map. The type comes from the state file when it is `main`, `sub` or `independent`. The map gives an unrecognised agent the default Kimi's own reader uses. An agent named `main` defaults to `main`, and every other name defaults to `sub`.
- The `chooseAgent` function resolves which journal is the conversation. The tool resolves it as the literal `agents/main/wire.jsonl`. The engine registers exactly that id as the session's `main` agent (`sessionLifecycleService`), and its own readers name nothing else.
- Those readers are the CLI's replay (`agents['main']`) and the viewer's route, which defaults to `main` and refuses when there is none. So the agent named `main` comes first here too.
- The rest of the order exists so a session whose main journal is not on disk is imported rather than lost. It is an order by kind rather than by knowledge.
- An `independent` agent has no parent and its journal is a conversation of its own. The viewer's own disk-only inventory labels every non-main agent that way when `state.json` is unreadable. A `sub`'s journal is a thread of the session's.
- Last comes the only agent with a journal at all, for a session old enough to predate the field. Everything else is a sub-thread, and the count travels to the report rather than stay unstated. A user who ran subagents deserves to hear that their transcripts stayed behind.
- The importer counts a session whose chosen agent has no wire on disk as a session with no transcript.

**The index, and the head**

- The `readSessionIndex` function maps `sessionDir` to `workDir` for the entries the walk can vouch for. An append log holds lines for sessions that are gone, since a deletion is a line too.
- It also holds lines a hand edited, and lines for a session copied into another workspace. This tool's own legacy migration copies session directories between buckets, which is the one way two lines can name one id.
- A prompt history imported under the wrong project is worse than one left behind. So an entry is used only when its `sessionDir` is the directory the walk actually found.
- An entry whose basename is not the id its line claims is refused outright. That is stricter than the tool's own reader. That reader uses the index to find a session, so it has to accept any line whose directory exists under `<root>/sessions`.
- The key is the directory itself, which is what this reader can afford. It already knows which directory it reads, and it only wants the project name that goes with it.
- The `readWireHead` function reads what the first records of a wire say about the session. A state file written by an older release can carry no `cwd`. The wire's own `config.update` is then the only place the project is named.
- The reader takes only the first `WIRE_HEAD_BYTES` (64 KiB). The record is written when the session starts. A wire that names its directory later gets a report line with no directory, and the list does not read to the end for it.
- The head window can end mid-line, and half a JSON object is not one. The fragment reaches the parse loop and the same parse drops it there, as it drops every other line the reader cannot read.
- The wire's `metadata` record gives the `created_at` fallback for the start time, and `config.update` gives the `cwd`.

**The fold, record by record**

- The `readKimiSession` function reads one session end to end, and it returns `{ error }` only when the journal cannot be folded. That case is a record that names a step the journal never opened, which is a journal the tool itself refuses to replay.
- Every other surprise inside the file is a line this reader counts and leaves behind. That keeps one unknown record from the loss of a whole conversation.
- A step can be left open three ways. The process died before its `step.end`. The journal itself ends there. A `context.clear`, `undo` or compaction dropped the step while its records stayed.
- The source says nothing about how any of those ended, so `sealOpenSteps` derives the reason from what the step holds. The ZCode and dsh readers make the same call.
- A `step.end` record carries the step's own usage and how it ended. Without the usage an imported turn looks free.
- The end reason is the only place the source says whether the model stopped, ran out of context or was cut off. The fold has no field for it, and a transcript that says `still streaming` about a turn finished months ago is simply wrong.
- A call whose result never came is closed as an error result with the text `INTERRUPTED_OUTPUT`. That is the fold's own stand-in for a tool result the journal never recorded.
- A message that arrives while a tool result is still open is deferred. The deferral flushes when no call is open. The journal really does deliver messages in that order when a user types during a tool run.
- A tool result the journal cannot pair is not part of the context, and the fold returns without a word there. The count is this reader's own, and without it the line disappears without a trace.
- The `openCompaction` function finds the compaction a summary still waits for: the `full_compaction.begin` placeholder a later `context.apply_compaction` fills in.
- The fold patches only the last record of its replay, and it gives up when a message was appended between the two records. That loses the summary from its replay, though the context keeps it.
- To search back for the open placeholder instead binds the summary to the record that opened it. That is the placement the fold's own patch tries to achieve.
- A compaction that began and never reported a summary is not a boundary, because it replaced nothing the model saw. The importer counts it and removes it from the transcript.
- A cancelled compaction (`full_compaction.cancel`) is removed and counted. A completed one (`full_compaction.complete`) needs no record of its own.
- The importer counts a loop event this build does not know rather than guesses at it. The refusal for an unopened step is the one case that returns an error, and it names the record and the step.
- A step the journal opened and never filled is an assistant message with nothing in it, and the target cannot hold one. The reader drops it before the repair pass, with its own count.
- The repair pass drops such a step as an unpaired tool call, which is a different fact and a wrong note to hand the user. So the drop happens first, and the count names the reason.

**The boundaries, and the repair**

- A compaction boundary keeps its place among the messages. Each one is carried as the number of messages that came before it. The messages are laid out in one pass, so that index is exact.
- If the repair pass dropped anything, every boundary moves to the end instead of a place mid-transcript. A boundary whose suffix no longer exists is worse than a late one, and the ZCode and dsh readers make the same trade.
- Two boundaries can share a position. The records of a compaction land where the records of one before it already sit. A rewind between them removed every message that did separate them.
- They are inserted in the order the journal wrote them, and each position takes the count of messages before it plus the boundaries already placed. So the newest is the last.
- The newest is the one a resumed session starts from. The target reads its context from the final boundary onward, and the newest summary stands for everything the older ones described.
- The `fillToolNames` function fills a tool result's tool name from the call it answers, in place. The journal records the name on the call, and a result that renders unnamed is one the user cannot recognise.
- In place rather than as a new array, because the messages are already held by the transcript's entries. A new array leaves the imported entries on the unnamed originals.
- A tool name the source did not record gets the stand-in `unknown`, and `fillToolNames` replaces it when a call names it.
- The output of `repairToolPairing` is what goes in, and its dropped count is reported. A call whose result never came, and the result whose call never did, is a pair the provider rejects on the next request. Such a session lists but cannot be resumed.
- Every other record is not conversation: prompts, model requests, usage, goals, permissions, the tool store, and the agent and workspace records. None of them can add, remove or rewrite a message, so the report counts none of them as a loss. A count of every record in a long session buries the real skips.
- A session with a `forkedFrom` value imports whole, and the report says whose words are in it. The inherited prefix cannot be told apart from the turns the user typed. A fork copies the parent's records into its own journal, ids and all.

**What a real user input is**

- The `kimiMessage` function turns a `ContextMessage` into a labunbun message, or returns null when nothing can carry it.
- The origins the tool itself calls real user input are the ones imported. They are what the user typed, and the two slash-command records typed at the prompt (`skill_activation` and `plugin_command`).
- Everything else the harness appends to its own context is a message the target renders for itself on resume. Those include injected reminders, shell lines, task notifications, cron and hook output, system triggers, and the summary the context starts from. To import the source's copy sends two of them.
- The report counts the skipped ones by what they were. So the report says what happened to the user's `!` commands rather than act as if they were never there.
- The `isRealUserInput` function is the tool's own predicate, and it is what an `undo` counts. An origin the journal did not state counts. An old record omits it, and a message the user typed is more likely than one the harness appended by accident.
- A slash-command record counts only when the trigger says the user typed it (`user-slash`). A model-invoked skill is the model's own step.
- The importer counts a user message with no body as an empty user message. A harness-appended message carries its kind in the note, and the importer counts and drops a system message, because the target writes its own.
- A message with no role is counted, and so is a tool message with no call id.
- The summary the context starts from is held with the origin `compaction_summary`, which is what an `undo` stops at. A tool result and the assistant message a step opens are never real user inputs.

**The two spellings, and the parts**

- The `toolCall` function reads a tool call of an appended message in either spelling the journal uses. Version 1.0 wrote `{function: {name, arguments}}`, and the tool migrates those records forward on read (`wire/migration/v1.1.ts`).
- To read both spellings here means a journal from before the migration imports, and the reader does not need to know which release wrote it.
- The `toolCallArguments` function accepts both forms too. The journal writes `args` as the tool input object, and version 1.0 wrote the model's raw string.
- Anything else is JSON-encoded the way the fold does it (`JSON.stringify(event.args)`), and the shared normaliser then turns it into an object.
- The `assistantPart` function carries a text part and a `think` part. The importer counts an encrypted-only `think` part and does not carry it as a signature. The blob is the source model's, and the provider a resumed session talks to cannot check it. A part with neither text nor a blob says nothing and is not worth a report line.
- The importer counts an image the assistant referenced by URL and does not fetch it. A part the assistant produced is content the target cannot re-request, and a URL is not an image until something fetches it.
- An image part is carried only when it holds its bytes: `data:<mime>;base64,<bytes>`. A URL needs a fetch, and a transcript is not the place to start network I/O on the user's behalf.
- The `userContent` function joins the text parts of a user message, and an image that travels with them follows the text. The `toolOutputContent` function keeps the text of a tool result and any image the tool returned.
- A tool result's content must be non-empty, or the first `--continue` fails. So an empty result carries the text `(no output recorded)`.
- The reader reads a compaction's summary from `contextSummary` first, because that is what the context starts from when both keys are present (`contextSummary ?? summary`). The raw summary is the model's own output and can carry the prompt's own wrapper text with it.
- The raw summary can be a string or a content-part list, and the list's text parts are joined. The importer counts a compaction with no summary text and drops it.

**The end of a step**

- The `stopReasonOf` function maps the end of a step into the target's vocabulary. The values are the source's own. Its loop normalizes them before they reach the journal (`normalizeFinishReason`). The three names become `tool_use`, `end_turn` and `max_tokens`, from `tool_calls`, `completed` and `truncated`.
- The `filtered` reason is a provider's safety filter that cut the answer off, and it maps to the target's `refusal`. That is a deliberate stop rather than a failure of the request, and the user has to be able to see it.
- The reader reads a step with no reason at all from its content, the way the readers of the sources that record no reason do. A content with a tool call becomes `toolUse`, and anything else becomes `stop`.
- That covers an older journal and a step cut off before its `step.end`. The `interrupted` reason maps to `aborted`, and `error` maps to `error`.
- The `stepUsage` function reads the usage a `step.end` recorded into the target's four buckets. Kimi splits input into the cached and uncached halves (`inputOther` leaves both cache counts out), which is how the target counts them too.
- The names differ and the four numbers are the same: `inputOther` to `input`, `output` to `output`, `inputCacheRead` to `cacheRead`, and `inputCacheCreation` to `cacheWrite`.
- The `isNewerProtocol` function compares the dot-separated numbers of a wire's `protocol_version` against `KNOWN_WIRE_PROTOCOL` (`1.5`). A wire newer than the known one gets a report line and reads as the known version, and the note names both versions.
- Every record's time falls back to the last time the reader saw, and the session's `startedAt` seeds it. A time the reader cannot parse is not a new time.

### OpenCode

**The source of the source**

- OpenCode is the one source with a development checkout named here. Every fact in this section is transcribed from the OpenCode source at `G:\Bunttta\opencode-dev` rather than from its documentation. Where the source and a blog post disagree, this module follows the source and says so.
- OpenCode's user state is the three merged settings documents, the provider and MCP tables they hold, the instruction file, and the skill trees.

**The two spellings, v1 and v2**

- Eight config keys changed spelling between v1 and v2. In each pair, v2's name comes first and v1's second. The pairs are `provider`→`providers`, `permission`→`permissions`, `agent`→`agents`, `command`→`commands`, `plugin`→`plugins`, `snapshot`→`snapshots`, `attachment`→`attachments`, and `reference`→`references`. Each pair is one edit. V1 had a singular name for most of what became a table in v2, and v2 made the plural the real one.
- Nothing was renamed in the other direction. The `isV1` check (`core/src/v1/config/migrate.ts:31-33`) decides a document is v1 when it finds a v1 key. A file that holds only v2 names is not v1 at all and goes to the v2 engine unlowered.
- Both spellings are read, v2 first, so a file OpenCode already migrated and a file it did not both import. The spelling the file actually used travels to the report. A line that reads `opencode.json → provider.gw` points at a key that is not in the file the user has open.
- The `OpencodeConfigKey` type records which spelling won and whether only the v2 engine reads it there. That second flag answers a question a report line has to get right: whether `"it stays in opencode's file, which is where opencode reads it from"` is true. For a v1 name it is true. For a v2 name in a v1-shaped file it is false. V2's own migration rebuilds the document from a literal key list (`core/src/v1/config/migrate.ts:36-72`), and it drops every name not on that list. A v2-only key left in an old file is a value OpenCode is about to delete.

**The three merged documents**

- The three settings documents merge the way `loadGlobal` merges them (`opencode/src/config/config.ts:272-274`): `config.json`, then `opencode.json`, then `opencode.jsonc`, each with a `mergeDeep`. A key present in more than one document takes the value from the last one that has it.
- `mergeDeep` merges objects key by key and replaces everything else. The array-valued keys (`plugin`, `instructions`, `enabled_providers`) are replaced rather than concatenated, and that is what makes a shadowed document worth a report line.
- The merge is reproduced rather than replaced with a spread. A spread lets `config.json`'s `mcp` block be wholly replaced by `opencode.jsonc`'s. OpenCode's `mergeDeep` keeps the two servers that only one of them names.
- All three documents go through `ConfigParse.jsonc` (`:240`) whatever their extension. A document that does not parse becomes a report line that names it, rather than a document that quietly contributes nothing. The file text is parsed here with `parseJsonc` rather than through `readJsonc` for that reason.
- The reason given for a parse failure is fixed rather than the parser's own message, which can quote the text it choked on. For this source the text it choked on may be an API key.

**Is this install here at all**

- The presence test judges whether this install ever ran, and the config root alone is not enough. `DatabaseMigration.apply(db)` runs in the database service's own constructor (`core/src/database/database.ts:24-35`). So the database file is there after the first launch whether or not the user ever wrote a setting. A user who deleted, or never created, `~/.config/opencode` still has every session in that database. To report that machine as `no source configuration found` is a true sentence in front of a false conclusion.
- So the source counts as present when the config root exists, or when the database exists, or when the pre-SQLite `storage/` tree exists. The last two are things only a run leaves behind.

**The credentials, by the root they live in**
